FOREWORD 

There is herewith presented an annotated code of all laws of a general and permanent nature affecting the regulation of rates, services, facilities, and practices of public service companies, including the acts of 1949 and 1951 creating the present public service commission. It is hoped that this compilation will serve to satisfy a longfelt need for an up to date recodification of such laws as the prior publication has been out of print for some time and in any event is obsolete as it was issued more than sixteen years ago. 

The present publication is geared to the numbering system adopted for the new Revised Code of Washington, and thus is not independently chaptered or section-numbered as was the earlier one. It consists primarily of Chapters 22.20, 22.24 and 43.53 RCW and Titles 80 and 81 RCW, the latter embodying, together with certain earlier laws, the basic public service act of 1911. The remainder of the publication brings together a number of acts of a miscellaneous nature with respect to the administration of which the public service commission is charged with some duty. 

It should be noted that the new Revised Code of Washington has broken the main body of public service law into two separate titles, one relating to public utilities and the other to transportation, and as a result several of the earlier chapters in Titles 80 and 81 virtually duplicate each other.

We have not included in this publication three chapters of Title 80 dealing with municipal utilities and one chapter of Title 81 dealing with taxicabs and certain for hire vehicles other than auto stages, jurisdiction over which is vested elsewhere than in the Public Service Commission.

43.53.010 Commission created - Appointment of members- 

Terms-Removal. There is hereby created and established a state commission to be known and designated as the Washington public service commission, and in this chapter referred to as the commission.

The commission shall be composed of three members appointed by the governor, with the consent of the senate. Not more than two members of said commission shall belong to the same political party. 

The members of the first commission to be appointed after taking effect of this section shall be appointed for terms beginning April 

1, 1951, and expiring as follows: One commissioner for the term expiring January 1, 1953; one commissioner for the term expiring 

January 1, 1955; one commissioner for the term expiring January 

1, 1957. Each of the commissioners shall hold office until his successor is appointed and qualified. Upon the expiration of the terms of the three commissioners first to be appointed as herein provided, each succeeding commissioner shall be appointed and hold office for the term of six years. One of such commissioners to be designated by the governor, shall, during the term of the appointing governor, be the chairman of the commission. 

Each commissioner shall receive a salary of ten thousand dollars per annum, payable monthly.

Any member of the commission may be removed for inefficiency, malfeasance or misfeasance in office, upon specific written charges filed by the governor, who shall transmit such written charges to the member accused and to the chief justice of the supreme court.

Public Service Laws 

The chief justice shall thereupon designate a special tribunal composed of three judges of the superior court to hear and adjudicate the charges. Such tribunal shall fix the time, place and procedure for the hearing, and the hearing shall be public. The decision of such tribunal shall be final and not subject to review. 

If the tribunal specified herein finds the charges of the governor to be true, the governor shall have the right to immediately remove the commissioner from office, to declare the position of the commissioner vacant, and appoint another commissioner to the position in accordance with the provisions of the law. 

Any vacancy arising in the office of commissioner shall be filled by appointment by the governor, and an appointee selected to fill such vacancy shall hold office for the balance of the full term for which his predecessor on the commission was appointed. 

If a vacancy occurs while the senate is not in session, the governor shall make a temporary appointment until the next meeting of the senate, when he shall present to the senate his nomination or nominations for the office to be filled. [1951 c 260 § 1; 1949 c 117 

§ 1; formerly Rem. Supp. 1949 § 10964-115-1.] Construction: "This act shall be construed as a continuation of chapter 81, Laws of 1905, chapter 117,

Laws of 1911, and the acts amendatory thereof and supplemental thereto." [1949 c 117 § 9, part.] 

43.53.020 Qualifications of commissioners and employees-Pecuniary interest in persons subject to regulation prohibited. Each commissioner shall be a qualified elector of this state and no person in the employ of or holding any official relation to any corporation or person, which corporation or person is subject in whole or in part to regulation by the commission, and no person owning stocks or bonds of any such corporation or who is in any manner pecuniarily interested therein shall be appointed or hold the office of commissioner or be appointed or employed by the commission: Provided, That if any such person becomes the owner of such stocks or bonds or becomes pecuniarily interested in such corporation otherwise than voluntarily, he shall within a reasonable time divest himself of such ownership or interest, and failing to do so his office or employment shall become vacant. [1949 c 117 § 2, part; Rem. Supp. 1949 § 10964-115-2, part.] 

43.53.030 Commissioner's oath of office-Bond. Each commissioner shall, before entering upon the duties of his office, take and subscribe the constitutional oath of office, and furnish bond to the state in the sum of twenty thousand dollars conditioned for the faithful discharge of the duties of his office and for the proper accounting for all funds that may come into his possession by virtue of his office. [1949 c 117 § 2, part; Rem. Supp. 1949 § 10964-115-2, part.] 

43.53.040 Commission to employ secretary and other assistants 

Secretary's duties-Deputies. The commission shall appoint and

employ a secretary and such accounting, engineering, expert and clerical assistants, and such other qualified assistants as may be necessary to carry on the administrative work of the commission. 

The secretary shall be the custodian of the commission's official seal, and shall keep full and accurate minutes of all transactions, proceedings and determinations of the commission and perform such other duties as may be required by the commission. 

The commission may deputize one or more of its assistants to perform, in the name of the commission, such duties of the commis sion as it deems expedient. [1949 c 117 § 4; Rem. Supp. 1949 § 10964115-4.] 

1. Neither this section nor any other empowers the Commission to pay a yearly fee for the support of the office of the General Solicitor of the National Association of Railroad and Utilities Commissioners. Op. Atty. Gen. April 12, 1940. 

2. There is no constitutional prohibition against the hiring by the Department of State Representatives to act as Supervising Field Agent and Field Agent, respectively. Op. Atty. Gen. June 5, 1945. 

43.53.050 General powers and duties of commission. The public service commission shall: 

(1) Exercise all the powers and perform all the duties prescribed therefor by this chapter and by Titles 22, 80, and 81, or by any other law. (2) Regulate in the public interest, as provided by the public service laws, the rates, services, facilities, and practices of all persons engaging in the transportation by whatever means of persons or property within this state for compensation, and related activities; including, but not limited to, air transportation companies, auto transportation companies, express companies, freight and freight line companies, motor freight companies, motor transportation agents, private car companies, railway companies, sleeping car companies, steamboat companies, street railway companies, toll bridge companies, storage warehousemen, and wharfingers and warehousemen. (3) Regulate in the public interest, as provided by the public service laws, the rates, services, facilities, and practices of all persons engaging within this state in the business of supplying any utility service or commodity to the public for compensation, and related activities; including, but not limited to, electrical companies, gas companies, irrigation companies, telegraph companies, telephone companies, and water companies. (4) Make such rules and regulations as may be necessary to carry out its other powers and duties. [(i) 1949 c 117 § 3; Rem. Supp. 

1949 § 10964-115-3. (ii) 1945 c 267 § 5; Rem. Supp. 1945 § 10459-5. (iii) e 1945 c 267 § 6; Rem. Supp. 1945 § 10459-6.] 

43.53.060 Quorum-Hearings-Actions deemed those of commission, when. A majority of the commissioners shall constitute a quorum for the transaction of any business, for the performance of

any duty, or for the exercise of any power of the commission, and may hold hearings at any time or place within or without the state. 

Any investigation, inquiry or hearing which the commission has power to undertake or to hold may be undertaken or held by or before any commissioner or any examiner designated and authorized by the commission as provided in RCW 43.53.070. 

All investigations, inquiries and hearings of the commission, and all findings, orders or decisions, made by a commissioner, when approved and confirmed by the commission and filed in its office, shall be and be deemed to be the orders or decisions of the com mission. [1949 c 117 § 6; Rem. Supp. 1949 § 10964-115-6.] 

43.53.070 Examiners-Powers. The commission may designate employees of the commission as examiners when it deems such action necessary for its general administration. 

Such examiners may administer oaths, issue subpoenas for the attendance of witnesses and the production of papers, waybills, books, accounts, documents and testimony, examine witnesses, and receive testimony in any inquiry, investigation, hearing, or proceeding in any part of the state, under such rules and regulations as the commission may adopt. [1925 ex.s. c 164 § 1; RRS § 10779-1.] 

1. Objection to taking testimony before examiner waived.

Great Nor thern Ry. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 137 Wash. 548 (1926). 

43.53.080 Joint investigations, hearings, orders. The commission shall have full power to make joint or concurrent investigations, hold joint or concurrent hearings, and issue joint or concurrent orders in conjunction or concurrence with any official, board, or commission of any state or of the United States, whether in the holding of such investigations or hearings or in the making of such orders the commission functions under agreements or compacts between states or under the concurrent power of states to regulate interstate commerce or as an agency of the federal government or otherwise. When necessary the commission may hold such joint hearing or investigation outside the state. [1949 c 117 §7; Rem.

Supp. 1949 § 10964-115-7.] 

1. Under this section the Department could hold hearings with the Idaho 

Public Utilities Commission in Idaho to consider a telephone rate increase in the Washington area served by a telephone exchange located in Idaho. State ex rel. Clarkston Chamber of Commerce v. Dept. of Public Utilities, 34 Wn. (2d) 141 (1949). 

43.53.090 Public service revolving fund created-Purpose. As of

April 1, 1949, there is created in the state treasury a "public service revolving fund" to which shall be transferred all moneys which then remain on hand to the credit of the transportation revolving fund and the public utilities revolving fund, subject, however, to outstanding warrants and other obligations chargeable to appropriations made from such funds. From and after April 1, 1949, regulatory fees payable by all types of public service companies

te 20 shall be deposited to the credit of the public service revolving fund. 

All expense of operation of the commission shall be payable out of the public service revolving fund. [1949 c 117 § 11; Rem. Supp. 

1.

Fund must be devoted solely to defraying administrative expenses of act. Great Northern Ry. Co. v. State, 184 Wash. 648 (1935)-Reversed on facts in 300 U. S. 154, 57 Sup. Ct. 397, 81 L. Ed. 573 (1937). 

2. Fees based on gross operating revenues not an unconstitutional burden on interstate commerce provided amount of fees reasonably commensurate with cost of supervision, but where charges against fund were not properly segregated, burden of proving reasonableness shifts to state. Id. 

3. Statute directing the Department to collect state excise tax from truckers when collecting fees for permits and identification plates must be complied with although no specific funds were provided for the expenses incurred. 

Op. Atty. Gen. Dec. 19, 1945. 

43.53.100 Proceedings public records-Seal-Biennial report. All proceedings of the commission and all documents and records in its possession shall be public records, and it shall adopt and use an official seal. The commission shall make and submit to the governor and the legislature a biennial report containing a statement of the transactions and proceedings of its office, together with the information gathered by the commission as required by law and such other facts, suggestions, and recommendations as it deems necessary. 

[1949 c 117 § 5; Rem. Supp. 1949 § 10964-115-5.] 

1. If specific law authorizing them to keep records does not provide otherwise, state, county, and city officials may remove their records to places of safety during the emergency. Op. Atty. Gen. Mar. 10, 1942.

COORDINATING COMMITTEE SAFETY COUNCIL 43.60.130 Coordinating Committee-Composition. The official coordinating committee shall be composed of the governor, the director of highways, the chief of the state patrol, the director of licenses, the superintendent of public instruction, the chairman of the public service commission and the director of labor and industries, and such other state officials as the governor may designate. 

The governor shall serve as chairman of this committee. [1951 c 247 § 23.] 

T.B.A. MEMBERSHIP 

47.56.020 Authority created-Members-Compensation and expenses. There is hereby created the Washington toll bridge authority, composed of the governor, state auditor, chairman of the public service commission, director of highways, and the director of public institutions. Members shall serve without compensation other than that received in the office by virtue of which they are members. 

Any expenses incurred for clerical or other assistance and necessary supplies shall be paid for in the manner and from funds as provided herein. A majority of the members of the authority shall constitute a quorum for the transaction of business. [1937 c 173 § 2; RRS § 6524-2.]

80.04.010 Definitions. As used in this title, unless the context indicates otherwise: "Commission" means the Washington public service commission; "Company" includes a corporation, association, joint stock association, partnership, or person, their lessees, trustees, or receivers; "Gas plant" means all property used or to be used for or in connection with the manufacture, distribution, sale, or furnishing of gas for light, heat, or power; "Gas company" means every company and every city or town owning, controlling, operating, or managing any gas plant for hire; "Electric plant" means all property used or to be used for or in

connection with the generation, transmission, distribution, sale, or furnishing of electricity for light, heat, or power; "Electrical company" means every company and every city or town owning, controlling, operating, or managing any electric plant for hire; "Telephone plant" means all property used or to be used for or in connection with the transmission or furnishing of telephonic communication; "Telephone company" means every company and every city or town owning, controlling, operating, or managing any telephone plant for hire; "Telegraph plant" means all property used or to be used for or in connection with the transmission or furnishing of telegraphic communication; "Telegraph company" means every company owning, controlling, operating, or managing any telegraph plant for hire; "Water system" means all property used or to be used for or in connection with the supply, storage, distribution, sale, or furnishing of water for power, irrigation, reclamation, manufacturing, municipal, domestic, or other beneficial uses; "Water company" means every company and every city or town owning, controlling, operating, or managing any water system for hire; "Public service company" includes every gas company, electrical company, telephone company, telegraph company, and water company;

The term "service" is used in this title in its broadest and most inclusive sense. [1929 c 223 § 1, part, last am'ds 1911 c 117 § 8; RRS § 10344, part.]

Public service commission: Chap ter 43.53. "common carrier” a. Telephone and telegraph companies are common carriers subject to legislative control. State v. Superior Court of King County, 67 Wash. 37, L.R.A. 

1915C, 287 Ann. Cases 1913D, 78 (1912). b. Telephone companies are common carriers subject to regulation under the police power. State v. Skagit River Telephone & Telegraph Co., 85 

Wash. 29 (1915); modified 89 Wash. 625 (1916). "electrical company" a. The city of Tacoma may conduct its light and water business without interference by the Department. McCormacks Inc. v. Tacoma, 170 Wash. 

103 (1932). b. Electric service rendered tenants by owner of an apartment house does not constitute a service to the public for hire and rates are not under the jurisdiction of the Department. Op. Atty. Gen. Feb. 13, 1929. c. See 199 Wash. 527, electric co-operatives. "telegraph company" a. Nothing in the act compels the Commission to treat the telegraph department of a railroad company as an independent company. Op. Atty. 

Gen. April 24, 1916.

"telephone company" a. Telephone system which affords communication to people living within 

Washington but passes through a central exchange located in Idaho is interstate and subject to the jurisdiction of the Interstate Commerce Commission. 

Op. Atty. Gen. March 21, 1924. b. A telephone company may engage in both public and private service and may lease to private concerns facilities not needed for its public service. 

Op. Atty Gen. March 30, 1927. 

c. What telephone companies are within state regulatory acts. 21 A.L.R.

1162. "water system" a. The supplying of water to a few neighbors does not make a water system a public utility. There is no dedication to public use. Clark v. Olson, 177 

Wash. 237, 93 A.L.R. 240 (1934). b. Furnishing of water from spring to one person under contract does not constitute a public use-there is no indication of dedication to public use. 

Op. Atty. Gen. June 21, 1928, Aug. 4, 1928, and Aug. 23, 1928. "water company" a. The term "water company" does not include wells and the Commission has no jurisdiction. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1911-12, p. 132. b. Company supplying water for hire is under act. State ex rel. Addy v. 

Dept. of Public Works, 158 Wash. 462 (1930). 

c. Irrigation company as a public utility. 15 A.L.R. 1227. d. Port district is not a public service company subject to commission's powers and jurisdiction as to overcharges collected by the district itself. 

Charge made by a lessee of the district would be subject to commission's jurisdiction. Op. Atty. Gen. June 9, 1920. 

e. Incidental service to members of farmers picnic association does not make the association a public service water company. Op. Atty. Gen. June 

25, 1927. f. The department does not have jurisdiction over any of the rates, services, and facilities of a water district. Op. Atty. Gen. July 18, 1927. g. Supplying a few persons with water for a nominal rate and not for profit does not constitute the system a public utility so as to enable the public to demand service. Clark v. Olson, 177 Wash. 237, 93 A.L.R. 240 (1934). See also, Inland Empire Rural Electrification Inc. v. Dept. of Public Service, 199 

Wash. 527. See also, 87 A.L.R. 1205; 16 Am. Jur. 298. h. A water company which does not hold itself out to serve the public generally but only a few persons of its own selection is not a public service company. Op. Atty. Gen. April 13, 1942. "public service company" a. Irrigation company made up partly of land owners served by its canal and charging a $2 per acre maintenance charge is a public service company even though the organizational plan contemplates eventual complete ownership of the company by the landowners. Op. Atty. Gen. March 7, 1916. b. Company furnishing water at wholesale rates to city for distribution and sale through city's own system is subject to regulation by department. Op. Atty. Gen. Jan. 18, 1927. c. Company furnishing for hire mineral water from lake for bath purposes is a public service company if water system exists. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1921-22, p. 197. d. Right of condemnation allowed an electrical company which proposes to generate electricity for manufacturing and domestic uses. State ex rel. 

Chelan Electric Co. v. Superior Court, 142 Wash. 270 (1927). 

e. Farmers Mutual Telephone Company leasing pole space to a public telephone company does not thereby become a public service company. Op. 

Atty. Gen. March 9, 1927. 

f. A company supplying water for hire is under act. State ex rel. Addy v.

Dept. of Public Works, 158 Wash. 462 (1930). 

g. Incidental service to public as making individual or corporation whose principal business is of a different nature a public utility. 18 A.L.R. 764; 

93 A.L.R. 248.

h. Regaining of its private status by a corporation after having become subject to the duties and obligations of a public utility. 34 A.L.R. 175. i. Electric company furnishing two companies with electricity under special contracts does not hold itself out as willing to serve others and is not a public service company. Op. Atty. Gen. May 2, 1927. j. Incidental service to members of farmers' picnic association does not make the association a public service water company. Op. Atty. Gen. June 

25, 1927. k. Department does not have jurisdiction over any of the rates, services, or facilities of a water district. Op. Atty. Gen. July 18, 1927. 

1. American Telechronometer Company is not furnishing service to the public for hire and is not therefore a public service company. Op. Atty. 

Gen. April 23, 1928, May 17, 1928. m. Membership corporation or association or cooperative group furnishing to its members electric, telephone or other service commonly supplied by public utility is subject to jurisdiction of commission. 132 A.L.R. 1495. n. Supplying a few persons with water for nominal rate and not for profit does not constitute the system a public utility so as to enable the public to demand service. Clark v. Olson, 177 Wash. 237; 93 A.L.R. 240 (1934). o. A telephone company operating in several northwestern states and performing both inter- and intrastate service in Washington is a public service company within the scope of this law. State ex rel. Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co. v. Dept. of Public Service, 19 Wn. (2d) 200 (1943). 

80.04.020 Oaths, subpoenas-Compelling attendance of witness. 

Any member of the commission may administer oaths, certify to all official acts, and issue subpoenas for the attendance of witnesses and the production of papers, books, accounts, documents, and testimony in any inquiry, investigation, hearing, or proceeding in any part of the state.

The superior court of the county in which any such inquiry, investigation, hearing, or proceeding may be had, may compel the attendance of witnesses and the production of papers, books, accounts, documents, and testimony as required by such subpoena. 

The commission, or the authorized representative thereof, before whom the testimony is to be given or produced, in case of the refusal of any witness to attend or testify or produce any papers required by the subpoena, shall report to the superior court of the county in which the proceeding is pending by petition, setting forth that due notice has been given of the time and place of attendance of said witness, or the production of said papers, and that the witness has been summoned in the manner prescribed in this chapter and that the fees and mileage of the witness have been paid or tendered to him for his attendance and testimony, and that he has failed and refused to attend or produce the papers required by the subpoena in the cause or proceedings specified in the notice and subpoena, or has refused to answer questions propounded to him in the course of such proceedings, and shall ask an order of the court, compelling the witness to attend and testify before the commission. 

The court, upon such petition, shall enter an order directing the witness to appear before the court at a time and place to be fixed in such order, and then and there show cause why he has not responded

it 2 it to the subpoena. A copy of the order shall be served upon the witness.

If it shall appear to the court that the subpoena was regularly issued, it shall enter an order that said witness appear at the time and place fixed in the order, and testify or produce the required papers and upon failing to obey said order, the witness shall be dealt with as for contempt of court. [1911 c 117 § 75, part; RRS § 10413, part.] 

1. Procedure before the Commission is not objectionable for lack of due process because the attendance of witnesses cannot be compelled, since attendance may be compelled by an order of the superior court. State ex rel. 

Oregon R. & N. Co. v. Railroad Comm., 52 Wash. 17 (1909). 

2. Commissioners may issue subpoenas duces tecum and although they cannot be "omnibus" subpoenas, they need not be as detailed in specifying what books and records are sought as would be required of a subpoena issued by a court. Op. Atty. Gen. Nov. 13, 1917. 

3. The Commission may issue an omnibus subpoena requiring a utility to produce all account books of every kind and nature showing every account between certain years; every voucher or bill paid; every canceled check and every paper, etc., or other instrument in writing showing payment or reception of money. The inconvenience to the utility must give way to the paramount rights of the complainant. Op. Atty. Gen. Nov. 23, 1917. 

80.04.030 Number of witnesses may be limited. In all proceedings before the commission, it shall have the right to limit the number of witnesses testifying upon any subject or proceeding to be inquired of. [1911 c 117 § 75, part; RRS § 10413, part.] 

1. Provision that at hearing Commission may limit number of witnesses is not an unreasonable one. State ex rel. Oregon R. & N. Co. v. Railroad 

Comm., 52 Wash. 17 (1909). 

80.04.040 Witness fees and mileage. Each witness who shall appear under subpoena shall receive for his attendance three dollars per day and five cents per mile traveled by the nearest practicable route in going to and returning from the place of hearing. No witness shall be entitled to fees or mileage from the state when summoned at the instance of the public service companies affected. [1911 c 117 § 76, part; RRS § 10414, part.] 

80.04.050 Protection against self-incrimination. The claim by any witness that any testimony sought to be elicited may tend to incriminate him shall not excuse such witness from testifying, but such evidence or testimony shall not be used against such person on the trial of any criminal proceeding, excepting in a prosecution for perjury. [1911 c 117 § 76, part; RRS § 10414, part.] 

80.04.060 Depositions-Service of process. The commission may take the testimony of any witness by deposition, and for that purpose the attendance of witnesses and the production of books, documents, papers, and accounts may be enforced in the same manner as in the case of hearings before the commission or any authorized representative thereof. Process issued under the provisions of this

chapter shall be served as in civil cases. [1911 c 117 § 76, part; RRS § 10414, part.] 

80.04.070 Access to books and records. The commission, or any person authorized by it, may at any time inspect the accounts, books, papers, and documents of any public service company, and may examine under oath any officer, agent or employee of such public service company in relation thereto, and with reference to the affairs of the company.

Any person other than a commissioner who shall make any such demand shall produce his authority from the commission to make such inspection. [1911 c 117 § 77; RRS § 10415.] 

1. Commission is under no obligation to make an investigation to determine a reasonable rate. It may rest its decision upon evidence produced by the parties. Whether or not it shall make an investigation on its own motion is a matter within the discretion of the Commission and cannot be controlled by the courts. State ex rel. City of Seattle v. Public Service Comm., 76 Wash. 492 (1913). 

2. Commission has authority to inspect books and documents of public service company and it is preferable for it to send one of its accountants to secure information needed to correct annual report than to ask Attorney General to institute legal proceedings. Op. Atty. Gen. June 21, 1927. 

80.04.080 Annual reports. Every public service company shall annually furnish to the commission a report in such form as the commission may require, and specifically answer all questions concerning which the commission may need information. The annual reports shall show in detail the amount of capital stock issued, the amounts paid therefor and the manner of payment, the dividends paid, the surplus fund, if any, and the number of stockholders, the funded and floating debts and the interest paid thereon, the cost and value of the company's property, franchises, and equipment, the number of employees and the salaries paid each class, the accidents to employees and other persons and the cost thereof, the amounts expended for improvements each year, how expended and the character of such improvements, the earnings or receipts from each franchise or business and from all sources, the proportion thereof earned from intrastate business and the proportion earned from interstate business, the operating and other expenses and the proportion of such expense incurred in transacting intrastate business and the proportion incurred in transacting interstate business, such division to be shown according to such rules of division as the commission may prescribe, the balances of profit and loss, and a complete exhibit of the financial operations of the company each year, including an annual balance sheet. The report shall also contain such information in relation to rates, charges, or regulations concerning charges, or agreements, arrangements, or contracts affecting the same, as the commission may require.

The commission may, for the purpose of enabling it better to carry out the provisions of this title, prescribe the period of time

within which all public service companies shall have, as near as may be, a uniform system of accounts, and the manner in which such accounts shall be kept. 

Such detailed report shall contain all the required statistics for the period of twelve months ending on the last day of any particular month prescribed by the commission for any company. Such reports shall be made out under oath and filed with the commission at its office in Olympia within three months after the close of the designated year for which the report is made, unless additional time is granted by the commission. The commission may require any company to file monthly reports of earnings and expenses, and to file periodical or special, or both periodical and special, reports concerning any matter about which the commission is authorized or required to inquire into or keep itself informed about, or which it is required to enforce, such periodical or special reports to be under oath whenever the commission so requires. [1911 c 117 § 78, part; 

RRS & 10416, part.] 

1. Department not justified in fixing rates on the basis of erroneous reports filed by carriers without affording carrier an opportunity to correct report. State ex rel. Clear Lake Lumber Co. v. Kuykendall, 130 Wash. 674 (1924). 

2. Where the pier of a steamship company is used only in its interstate commerce carrying business, and its rates and charges for its wharfage affect only interstate commerce, the state is without power to require the company to file annual reports and pay fees on wharfage charges and collections under this section, notwithstanding its lease of the pier provides that the state reserves the right to regulate the leased area occupied by the pier since it would be a direct burden on interstate commerce, the field of regulation of which is entirely occupied by the federal government. Luckenbach Steamship 

Co. v. Denney, 152 Wash. 548 (1929). 

3. A city's right to place a license fee or tax on business or income of a telephone company is not impaired by the state's requiring payment of a gross revenue fee. Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co. v. City of Seattle, 172 Wash. 649 (1933). 

80.04.090 Forms of records to be prescribed. The commission may prescribe the forms of all accounts, records, and memoranda to be kept by public service companies, including the accounts, records, and memoranda of sales of products and the receipt and expenditure of money. The commission shall at all times have access to all accounts, records, and memoranda kept by public service companies, and may employ special agents or examiners, who may administer oaths and, under the order of the commission, examine witnesses and inspect and examine any and all accounts, records, and memoranda kept by the companies. The commission may prescribe the forms of all reports, accounts, records, and memoranda to be furnished and kept by any public service company, whose system extends beyond the limits of this state, which is operated partly within and partly without the state, so that they shall show any information required by the commission concerning the receipts and ex-

penditures appertaining to those parts of the system within the state.

[1911 c 117 § 78, part; RRS § 10416, part.] 

80.04.100 Production of out of state books and records. The commission may by order, with or without hearing, require the production within this state, at such time and place as it may designate, of any books, accounts, papers, or records kept by any public service company in any office or place without this state, or at the option of the company, verified copies thereof, so that an examination thereof may be made by the commission or under its direction. [1933 c 165 

§ 2; 1911 c 117 § 79; RRS § 10421.] 

80.04.110 Complaints-Hearings. Complaint may be made by the commission on its own motion, or by any person, corporation, chamber of commerce, board of trade, or any commercial, mercantile, agricultural, or manufacturing society, or any body politic or municipal corporation, by complaint in writing, setting forth any act or thing done or omitted by any public service company claimed to be in violation of any provision of law or of any order or rule of the commission.

No complaint shall be entertained by the commission, except upon its own motion, as to the reasonableness of the schedule of the rates or charges of any gas company, electrical company, water company, or telephone company, unless it is signed by the mayor, or the legislative authority of the city or town in which the company complained of is engaged in business, or by not less than twenty-five consumers of such gas, electricity, water, or telephone service.

If two or more companies, except municipal and other public corporations, are engaged in competition in any locality any one may make complaint against the others that the rates, charges, rules, regulations, or practices of those with respect to which the complainant is in competition, are unreasonable, unremunerative, discriminatory, illegal, unfair, or tending to oppress the complainant, to stifle competition, or to create or encourage the creation of monopoly. Upon such complaint, or upon complaint of the commission on its own motion, the commission may, after notice and hearing, by its order, subject to appeal as in other cases, correct the abuse complained of by establishing such uniform rates, charges, rules, regulations, or practices in lieu of those complained of, to be observed by all of such competing companies in the locality specified as shall be found reasonable, remunerative, nondiscriminatory, legal, and fair, or tending to prevent oppression or monopoly or to encourage competition, and upon the hearing the commission may take into consideration the rates, charges, rules, regulations, and practices of the companies complained of in other localities in the state. 

All matters upon which complaint is founded may be joined in one hearing, and no motion shall be entertained against a complaint

for misjoinder of complaints or grievances, or misjoinder of parties; and in any review by the courts of orders of the commission the same rules shall apply with regard to the joinder of complaints and parties as herein provided: Provided, That all grievances to be inquired into are plainly set forth in the complaint. No complaint shall be dismissed because of the absence of direct damage to the complainant. 

Upon the filing of a complaint, or upon complaint of the commission on its own motion, the commission shall cause a copy thereof to be served upon the company complained of, which shall be accompanied by a notice fixing the time when and place where a hearing will be had thereon. The time fixed for hearing shall not be less than ten days after the date of the service of the notice and complaint. Rules of practice and procedure not otherwise provided for in this chapter may be prescribed by the commission. [1913 c 145 

§ 1; 1911 c 117 § 80; RRS § 10422.] 

1. After a full hearing, the Commission cannot, upon a complaint as to certain distance tariffs, make an order that certain other commodity rates shall not be increased, where the complaint alleged the latter to be fair and reasonable; since no issue was presented thereon and no order could be made relating thereto without notice and hearing; the remedy for an increase of such rates being by another complaint. State ex rel. Great Northern R. Co. v. Railroad Comm., 47 Wash. 627 (1907). 

2. The act of 1907, authorizing the state railroad commission to hear and determine complaints against railroad companies as a mediatory court on matters of railroad regulation is not objectionable in that the Commission may be the accuser and file the complaint against the companies, there being no presumption of partiality or unfairness from such fact. State ex rel. Oregon 

R. & Nav. Co. v. Railroad Comm., 52 Wash. 17 (1909). 

3. A complaint filed by the railroad commission before itself, alleging that the depot facilities of the defendant at its various stations are inadequate, is a sufficient compliance with the statute requiring all grievances to be set out in the complaint; and confers jurisdiction to enter an order requiring the company to erect suitable depots at certain stations within a specified time. 

State ex rel. Railroad Comm. v. Oregon R. & Nav. Co., 68 Wash. 160 (1912). 

4. Tariff properly noticed and filed automatically goes into effect at the expiration of the thirty day period unless suspended or adjudged unreasonable by Commission.

Commission is under no obligation to make investigation to determine a reasonable rate. It may rest its decision upon evidence produced by the parties.

Discretion of Commission whether or not it shall make an investigation on its own motion and extent of such investigation cannot be controlled by the courts. State ex rel. Seattle v. Public Service Comm., 76 Wash. 492 (1913). 

5. Under this section, an order of the Commission fixing reasonable rates after a hearing, without fixing any time for it to become effective, takes effect and becomes operative twenty days after the service thereof. State ex rel. 

Great Northern R. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 80 Wash. 218 (1914). 

6. A consumer, discriminated against by a water company in the matter of rates charged for water, has authority to file a complaint with the Commission, under this section; the proviso pertaining only to complaints affecting the reasonableness of the schedule of rates or charges of a public service corporation, and not abridging the authority of an individual to complain of a discrimination in rates. State ex rel. Goss v. Metaline Falls Light & Water Co., 80 Wash. 652 (1914). 

7. Where an order of the Public Service Commission was not superseded and became effective in twenty days, it will be presumed that it was complied

with and therefore it cannot be objected to as too indefinite to be enforced.

State ex rel. Northern Pac. R. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 95 Wash. 376 

(1917). 

8. Where a hearing ordered on Commission's own motion in regard to gas company's filing of increased rates was not set with proper ten days' notice to the gas company, a city could not take advantage of that irregularity to later avoid the Commission's order, nor could the filing of a complaint with the Commission by the city against the gas company less than ten days before the hearing date deprive the Commission of jurisdiction to hold the hearing on that date when the city was later accorded a full hearing. State ex rel. City of Seattle v. Public Service Comm., 107 Wash. 17 (1919). 

9. Courts have jurisdiction of actions by private individuals to protect rights infringed-mandamus to compel superior court to hear case. State ex rel. Seattle & Rainier Valley R. Co. v. Court, 123 Wash. 116 (1923). 

10. Department has no jurisdiction to hear and determine matters not set forth in the complaint. Northern Pacific Public Service Co. v. Kuykendall, 127 Wash. 73 (1923). 

11. Complaint must state facts sufficient to give the Department jurisdiction and to authorize relief to the complainant. Northern Pacific Railway Com pany v. Dept. of Public Works, 136 Wash. 389 (1925). 

12. On an application for an order directing telephone connections between relator and defendant company, a third company having connection with defendant is a necessary party to the case, where its lines serve relator's territory and it claims exclusive rights. Defect of parties cannot be cured in an action to enforce order. State ex rel. Dept. of Public Works v. Pacific Tele phone & Telegraph Co., 144 Wash. 383 (1927). 

13. Department has no jurisdiction to entertain a petition to classify property as operating or non-operating property. May classify in valuation proceedings. Northern Pacific Railway Company v. Denney, 155 Wash. 544 (1930). 

14. When one company holds a certificate authorizing service between 

Seattle, Bremerton and Chico and intermediate points, and another company holds a certificate authorizing direct service between Seattle and Bremerton, the two companies are operating vessels upon the same route and between the same districts (so far as concerns Seattle and Bremerton) and are subject to continuing control by the Department with respect to competition. 

Although the statute contemplates a complaint against unlawful competition, the protest of the Navigation Company against the proposed operations of the Washington Route brought the matter before the Department. State ex rel. Puget Sound Nav. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 161 Wash. 622, 297 

Pac. 795 (1931). 

15. Department ordered telechronometers out of Everett on its own motion. State ex rel. American Telechronometer Co. v. Baker, 164 Wash. 483 (1931). 

16. Findings of fact must be such that their meaning will not be misunderstood. State ex rel. Bohon v. Dept. of Public Service, 6 Wn. (2d) 676 (1940). 

17. The Department in a rate case is limited to the hearing and determination of those issues only which are raised by the pleadings. Id. 

18. Purpose of limitation at hearing to issues raised by the pleadings is to insure the utilities affected full opportunity to be heard before a ruling is made. Id. 

19. Complaint charging increased fares and defective equipment resulting in irregular bus schedules raises no issue as to the revocation of a certificate of convenience and necessity, and there is no need for an allegation as to such revocation. State ex rel. Northeast Transportation Co. v. Abel, 10 Wn. (2d) 

20. "Order to Show Cause" and "Notice of Hearing" directing company to appear and show cause why its certificate should not be cancelled for failure to comply with a prior order of the Department held sufficient notice to company to give Department jurisdiction to cancel certificate of convenience and necessity. Id.

21. Department's order revoking certificate of bus company for certain violations of law held invalid for failure of Department to find that said violations were willful. Id. 

22. Public Utility Districts duly organized have capacity to initiate and maintain legal action under the Public Service law. State ex rel. PUD No. 1 of 

Okanogan County v. Dept. of Public Service, 21 Wn. (2d) 201 (1944). 

23. A complaint filed with the Department by a public utility district to 

E compel a power company to file a schedule of rates, does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action, and a demurrer to such complaint was properly sustained where it appears that the district was not seeking relief because of any charge, rate, rule or regulation of the power company but simply desired certain information advisory in nature, to enable the district to perfect its organization by financing the same. Id. 

24. Under this section, the Department has jurisdiction to hear and determine complaints by bus companies that the operations of another bus company were unlawful and in violation of the certificate rights of the complaining companies, in that such operations constituted the giving of service already being given by such companies under their respective certificates. State ex rel. North Bend Stage Lines, Inc. v. Dept. of Transportation, 26 Wn. (2d) 

485 (1946). 

25. When, under this section, operations of a common carrier were brought before the Department, and the Department, after proceedings under 

RCW 80.04.130 or 81.04.130, entered its order after the close of the hearing, the carrier was a public service company, subject to the jurisdiction of the Department, and the carrier had a right to have the order reviewed by the superior court and under RCW 80.04.190 or 81.04.190 to appeal from that judgment to the Supreme Court. State ex rel. Puget Sound Navigation Co. v. 

Dept. of Transportation, 33 Wn. (2d) 448 (1949). 

26. Section concerning complaint by one public service company against a competing company because of rates, charges, rules, regulations or practices does not give Commission jurisdiction to order electric railway to bond its tracks so as to prevent electrolysis between tracks and complainant's gas mains. Op. Atty. Gen. July 26, 1915. 

27. While original Public Service law did not apply to warehouses, provisions since have made it so applicable and a warehouse complaint against railroads for building loading platforms and diverting its business may be heard by the Commission. Op. Atty. Gen. April 5, 1929. 

80.04.120 Hearings, order, record. At the time fixed for the hearing, the complainant and the company complained of shall be heard and may introduce such evidence as may be desired. The commission shall issue process to enforce the attendance of all necessary witnesses.

At the conclusion of the hearing the commission shall make findings concerning the subject matter and facts inquired into, and enter its order based thereon. A copy of the order, certified under the seal of the commission, shall be served upon the company complained of, or its attorney, which order shall, of its own force, take effect and become operative twenty days after the service thereof, except as otherwise provided. 

When, in the judgment of the commission, an order cannot be complied with within twenty days, it may prescribe such additional time as in its judgment is reasonably necessary to comply with the order, and it may, on application and for good cause shown, extend the time for compliance fixed in its order.

A full and complete record of all proceedings had before the commission, or any authorized representative thereof, on any formal hearing, and all testimony, shall be taken down by a stenographer appointed by the commission, and the parties shall be entitled to be heard in person or by attorney. 

In an action to review an order of the commission, a transcript of the testimony, together with all exhibits introduced, and of the record and proceedings in the cause, shall constitute the record of the commission. [1911 c 117 § 81; RRS § 10423.] 

1. The procedure before the commission upon disputed hearings concerning the regulation of railroads is not objectionable for lack of due process because the attendance of witnesses cannot be compelled; since attendance may be compelled through an order of the superior court. (See RCW 80.04.020 and 81.04.020.)

Nor is it objectionable for want of penalties for perjury, since the general law of perjury applies. 

The Railroad Commission act creating a regulative commission with executive and administrative duties may also confer on the Commission judicial powers as a mediatory court, since the constitutional division of governmental powers into three separate departments applies only in a limited sense, and does not restrict one set of officers to one department exclusively. 

The act is not objectionable in that it empowers the Commission to limit the number of witnesses. State ex rel. Oregon R. & Nav. Co. v. Railroad 

Comm., 52 Wash. 17. Reversed: State v. Fairchild, 224 U. S. 510, 56 L. Ed. 

863-"evidence failed to sustain findings that public necessity required the track connection" (1909). 

2. Under the statute providing that an order of the railroad commission for furnishing additional facilities shall be served on the company by delivering certified copy, an oral announcement by the chairman at the end of a hearing is not an order binding on the company, and does not preclude the Commission from later entering a formal order and serving a copy on the company. State ex rel. Railroad Comm. v. Oregon R. & Nav. Co., 68 Wash. 160 (1912). 

3. Under the railroad commission act, making the Commission an intermediate tribunal with original jurisdiction to hear and determine questions relating to the duty of common carriers, its orders being final unless appealed from, under fixed penalties for violation of the order, the power to fix the time within which a depot must be completed is conferred by necessary implication. 

State ex rel. Railroad Comm. v. Great Northern R. Co., 68 Wash. 257 (1912). 

4. The question of the Commission's entering upon an inquiry on its own motion, as to the reasonableness of a proposed increase in a rate, or an inquiry instituted at the instance of another, beyond the evidence brought by complainant and the company, is one of discretion in the Commission. State v. 

Public Service Comm., 76 Wash. 492 (1913). 

5. An order made pursuant to the power conferred in this section, which does not fix the time within which it is to become effective, takes effect and becomes operative twenty days after service thereof. State ex rel. G. N. Ry. 

Co. v. Public Service Comm., 80 Wash. 218 (1914). 

6. Power given the Department to rescind or amend any order made does not give it jurisdiction to reopen a proceeding which has become final because no review was sought by the party injured thereby, and after the lapse of four years, when the period of the statute creating the bar to the claim had elapsed. Northern Pacific Ry. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 122 Wash. 673 

(1923). 

7. An order of an administrative tribunal is not invalidated by the admission of matter inadmissible under rules of evidence nor by mere error in reasoning. Northern Pacific Ry. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 268 U. S. 39, 

69 L. Ed. 836, reversing 125 Wash. 584 (1923).

8.

Proceedings involving the same matters may be consolidated by the 

Department. State ex rel. Oregon Washington Railroad & Navigation Company v. Dept. of Public Works, 135 Wash. 644 (1925). 

9. Department is an inferior tribunal and nothing is presumed in favor of its exercise of power. Everything must appear in the record. When the 

Department fails to make findings of fact vital to its jurisdiction, case should be remanded for further findings. P. S. Nav. Co. v. Department, 152 Wash. 

417 (1929). 

10. Court will notice rule of Department quoted in findings, even though not introduced in evidence. N. P. Coast Freight Bureau v. Department, 156 

Wash. 137 (1930). 

11. The findings of fact required by the statute on appeal from the Department in a rate case against a common carrier, must be direct and certain, and should have regard to the interest of the general public; and findings on immaterial matters affecting only the interests of an individual shipper have but little weight. Great Nor. R. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 161 Wash. 29 

(1931). 

12. A change in the personnel of the Department during the course of regulatory proceedings affecting the service of a utility, is immaterial where the members making the order had before them the entire record. State ex rel. American Telechronometer Co. v. Baker, 164 Wash. 483 (1931). 

13. Findings of the Department are prima facie correct. State ex rel. 

Pacific Power & Light Co., Puget Sound Power & Light Co., Washington 

Water Power Co. v. Department, 179 Wash. 461 (1934); State ex rel.

Winlock 

Water Co. v. Department, 180 Wash. 278 (1934). 

14. Findings of the Department are prima facie correct, but the facts found must be sufficient to justify the order of the Department, and there must also be substantial evidence to sustain the findings. State ex rel. Puget 

Sound Power & Light Co. v. Department, 181 Wash. 105 (1935). 

15. Statute (RCW 80.04.260 or 81.04.260) authorizing Commission to procure injunction against any public service company violating the law or order of Commission manifests intent that injunction is to be obtained to restrain an existing or threatened specific violation and cannot be used as a correction for past abuses nor to enjoin a future general course of conduct. State ex rel. 

Dept. of Public Works v. Skagit River Navigation & Trading Co., 181 Wash. 

642 (1935). 

16. Courts may set aside orders of regulatory bodies fixing rates where the findings were not based on evidence sufficient to support. them. Id. 

17. A regulatory body may properly take into consideration the results of its own inspection of the physical conditions involved, results of its previous experience in similar situations, and the general information concerning the subject which goes to make up its fund of expert knowledge. State ex rel. 

Country Club of Seattle v. Dept. of Public Service, 198 Wash. 37 (1939). 

18. Departmental opinion was not a clear and definite finding.

State ex rel. Bohon v. Dept. of Public Service, 6 Wn. (2d) 676 (1940). 

19. The statute requiring all grievances against a company holding a certificate of convenience and necessity being inquired into by the Department to be set forth in the complaint is for the purpose of apprising the company of the charges against it, and where comprehensive notice of 15 days that its certificate might be revoked was given by a show cause order, the requirements of the statute were satisfied. State ex rel. Northeast Transportation Co. v. 

Abel, 10 Wn. (2d) 349 (1941). 

20. The Department possesses exclusive and original jurisdiction and courts may review and determine only those questions actually passed upon by the Department. Id. 

21. The statute giving the Department power to revoke a certificate of convenience and necessity provides that four requirements must be complied with: a hearing upon notice to the holder of the certificate; holder's opportunity to be heard; willful violation or refusal to obey Department's order; findings clearly indicating willful violation. Id.

22. Orders by regulatory bodies establishing rates of return upon investment are always subject to revision in view of changed circumstances. State ex rel. Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co. v. Dept. of Public Service, 19 Wn. (2d) 200 (1943). 

23. Nothing limits the length of time of a hearing. Op. Atty. Gen. Nov. 27, 1939. 

24. Department may enter into a contract with a shorthand reporter for the recording of its hearings and the nature of such contract is entirely a matter for the Department. Op. Atty. Gen. Feb. 27, 1940. 

80.04.130 Suspension of tariff changes. When a public service company files with the commission any schedule, classification, rule, or regulation, the effect of which is to change any rate, charge, rental, or toll theretofore charged, the commission may, either upon its own motion or upon complaint, upon notice, enter upon a hearing concerning the proposed change and the reasonableness and justness thereof, and pending the hearing and the decision thereon the commission may suspend the operation of the new rate, charge, rental, or toll for a period not exceeding ten months from the time it would otherwise go into effect, and after a full hearing the commission may make such order in reference thereto as would be provided in a hearing initiated after it had become effective. 

At any hearing involving any change in any schedule, classification, rule, or regulation, the effect of which is to increase any rate, charge, rental, or toll theretofore charged, the burden of proof to show that the increase is just and reasonable shall be upon the com pany. [1941 c 162 § 1, last am'ds 1911 c 117 § 82; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 10424.] 

1. The burden of proof imposed by this section held sustained by a gas company seeking a reasonable increase in rates. State ex rel. Seattle v. Public 

Service Comm., 107 Wash. 17 (1919). 

2. Increase in rates sustained on the facts. State ex rel. Seattle v. Public 

Service Comm., 114 Wash. 646 (1921). 

3. Before tariff takes effect, burden is on company to show reasonableness of tariff; if tariff in effect, burden is on complainant. North Coast Power 

Co. v. Kuykendall, 117 Wash. 563 (1921). 

4. Commission in fixing freight rates may, under certain conditions, take into consideration things done by shippers which would reduce the cost of transportation, provided they are of such importance as to distinguish such shippers from others, but they should be individual acts of material consequence and not a number of acts each of small significance. Public Service 

Comm. v. State ex rel. Great Northern R. Co., 118 Wash. 629, 25 A.L.R. 186 (1922). 

5. It is the policy of the public service act of 1911 that the owner of a franchise receive a fair and reasonable return in interest on the reasonable value of his property used and useful in public service. State v. Kuykendall, 119 Wash. 107 (1922). 

6. Determination of the reasonableness of an expenditure incurred in obtaining new business by a public service company falls within the regulatory power of the Department. Id. 

7. Where the evidence showed prima facie that rates are discriminatory and unlawful, the burden of proof is upon the carrier to support their new proposed schedule. Northern Pac. R. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 125 Wash. 

584 (1923). 

8. Where the data for rate making, furnished by a carrier, is manifestly incorrect and inconsistent, the carrier should be given an opportunity to cor-

rect the same, and it is arbitrary and unlawful for the Department to base rates thereon on the theory that the responsibility for the error was that of the carrier. State ex rel. Clear Lake Lumber Co. v. Kuykendall, 130 Wash. 

674 (1924). 

9. Although Commission has jurisdiction over rates and charges of a public service company, it does not have power to abrogate provisions of a franchise obligating a water company to furnish to a city free water for fire fighting and street flushing since the arrangement was consideration for the granting of the franchise and does not constitute preferential treatment to one of a group of customers. Monroe Water Co. v. Town of Monroe, 135 Wash. 355; reversing on rehearing 130 Wash. 351 (1925). 

10. Canceling of tariff falsely marked a reduction, sustained. North Pa cific Coast Freight Bureau v. Public Works, 156 Wash. 137 (1930). 

11. When rate is filed, published, and permitted to become effective, it remains until properly challenged the only lawful rate to be charged and collected. Puget Sound Navigation Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 157 Wash. 

557; affirmed on rehearing 160 Wash. 703 (1931). 

12. Where, after hearing before the Commission, a public service corporation has been denied an increase in rates, and where there is diversity of citizenship, the corporation may apply to the federal courts for injunctive relief from confiscatory rates since the corporation has exhausted its administrative remedies. Pac. Tel. & Tel. Co. v. Kuykendall, 265 U. S. 196, 68 L. Ed. 975 (1923). 

13. Average working capital of public utility was properly taken into account by special master in determining rate base. Pac. Tel. & Tel. Co. v. 

Whitcomb, 12 F. (2d) 279. Affirmed: 276 U. S. 97, 72 L. Ed. 483 (1927). 

14. Outlays as to reasonable future needs as to equipment is a matter of business management which may not be arbitrarily interfered with under guise of rate making. Id. 

15. Rate-making body may consider effect change in established rate will have on community at large. Great Northern Ry. Co. v. Dept. of Public 

Works, 161 Wash. 29 (1931). 

16. In fixing rates, value of service to consumer, ability of consumer to pay, and prevailing economic conditions are to be considered in connection with all other circumstances bearing upon what is a reasonable return to the utility. State ex rel. Puget S. P. & L. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 179 

Wash. 461 (1934). 

17. Difference between a legal and a lawful rate not recognized but rates specified in schedules filed and in effect are presumed reasonable and constitute the only lawful rates and remain such so long as they are effective. State ex rel. Standard Oil Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 185 Wash. 235 (1936). 

18. The Department in a rate case is limited to the hearing and determination of those issues raised by the pleadings. State ex rel. Bohon v. Dept. of 

Public Service, 6 Wn. (2d) 676 (1940). 

19. Department has the duty to fix a just and reasonable rate of return to be realized by a utility, and on review of such an order, it will be affirmed unless it appears that the rate fixed is confiscatory. State ex rel. Pac. Tel. & Tel. v. Dept. of Public Service, 19 Wn. (2d) 200 (1943). 

20. In determining rate of return to be allowed a utility, the regulating authorities should consider current rates of interest asked and received on investments or loans. Id. 

21. The Department has the duty to protect the traveling public from overcharges by a common carrier, and, at the same time, to maintain tariffs that will assure the carrier a fair and adequate return for the service rendered 

State ex rel. Puget Sound Navigation Co. v. Dept. of Transportation, 33 Wn. 

(2d) 448 (1949). 

22. Power vested in the Department to allow a new tariff to become effective implies power to allow it to become immediately effective subject to reasonable conditions or limitations. Id.

23. Temporary rates allowed by the Department are subject to examination from the standpoint of fairness to both carrier and public, and the Department has the power to allow a carrier to charge according to current tariffs conditioned upon refunds to patrons if temporary rates prove unreasonably high. Id. 

24. The Department has the authority to control temporary rates of common carriers, and under this section is vested with power to suspend temporary rates and to prevent their becoming effective. Id. 

25. Neither this nor any other section limits the amount of time a hearing may take. Op. Atty. Gen. Nov. 27, 1939. 

80.04.140 Order requiring joint action. When any order of the commission requires joint action by two or more companies, the order shall specify that such action shall be made at their joint cost, and the companies affected shall have thirty days, or such further time as the commission may prescribe, in which to agree upon the part of cost which each shall bear, and the cost of operation and maintenance in the future, or the proportion of charges or revenue each shall receive from such joint service and the rules to govern future operations. If at the expiration of such time the companies fail to file with the commission a statement that an agreement has been made for the apportionment of such cost, the division of cost of operation and maintenance to be incurred in the future, the proportion of charges or revenue each shall receive from such joint service, and the rules to govern future operations, the commission may, after further hearing, enter a supplemental order fixing the proportion of cost or expense to be borne by each company, and the manner in which they shall be paid and secured. [1911 c 117 § 83; RRS § 10425.] 

1. Regulation of rates-Joint rates-Constitutional law-Appeal and re view. State ex rel. Great Northern Railway Co. v. Public Service Comm., 76 Wash. 625 (1913). 

2. Where terms of physical connection are agreed upon, Commission has no power to provide for cost.

Commission has no power to fix joint rates in same order in which physical connection is ordered, but if companies fail to fix rates and tolls, Commission must do so. State ex rel. Public Service Comm. v. Skagit River Telephone & Telegraph Co., 85 Wash. 29; 89 Wash 625 (1915). 

3. Joint rates-Through_route, with milling in transit privilege-Prosser 

Mill case. State ex rel. Northern Pacific Railway Co. v. Public Service Comm., 95 Wash. 376 (1917). 

80.04.150 Remunerative rates cannot be changed without approval. Whenever, after a hearing had upon its own motion or upon complaint, the commission finds that any rate, toll, rental, or charge which has been the subject of complaint and inquiry is sufficiently remunerative to the company affected thereby, it may order that such rate, toll, rental, or charge shall not be changed or discontinued, nor shall there be any change in the classification which will change such rate, toll, rental, or charge, without first obtaining the consent of the commission authorizing the change. [1911 c 117 § 84; 

RRS § 10426.]

1. Future traffic cannot be burdened with past deterioration-Rates cannot exceed value of service. Puget Sound Electric Railway v. Railroad Comm., 65 Wash. 75 (1911). 

2. Change in gas rates Reasonableness of rates Burden of proof 

Effect of dismissal. State ex rel. Seattle v. Public Service Comm., 76 Wash.

492 (1913). 

3. Where rates found by a regulatory body to be compensatory are attacked as being confiscatory, the courts may enquire into the method by which its conclusion is reached. Northern Pacific Railway Co. v. Dept. 

of Public Works, 69 L. Ed. 836. 

4. In fixing reasonable rates for an interurban railroad, there should be allowed an annual renewal fund upon which to draw for necessary replacement; but, where company has failed to do so, the company cannot ask that future traffic be made to bear the deterioration of all the past years. Puget 

Sound Electric Railway v. Railroad Comm., 65 Wash. 75 (1911). 

5. Right of a carrier to fix rates which will earn a fair return on its investment is qualified by the rule that it cannot exact rates higher than the service is reasonably worth or more than the traffic will bear. Id. 

80.04.160 Rules and regulations. The commission may adopt, promulgate, and issue rules and regulations covering the transmission and delivery of messages and conversations, and the furnishing and supply of gas, electricity, and water, and all services concerning the same, or connected therewith; and generally such rules as pertain to the convenience of the public concerning the subjects treated of in this chapter.

Such rules and regulations shall be promulgated and issued by the commission on its own motion, and shall be served on the public service companies affected thereby as orders of the commission are served.

Any public service company affected, and deeming any of such rules and regulations improper, unjust, unreasonable, or contrary to law, may within twenty days from the date of service of such order upon it, file objections thereto with the commission specifying the particular grounds of such objections. Upon receipt of such objections, the commission shall fix a time and place for hearing the same, and after hearing may make such changes or modifications therein as the evidence may justify.

The commission may adopt rules to govern its proceedings, and to regulate the mode and manner of all investigations and hearings. 

No person desiring to be present at a hearing shall be denied permission. Actions may be instituted to review rules and regulations promulgated under this section as in the case of orders of the commission. [1911 c 117 § 85, part; RRS § 10427, part.] 

1. Contract to use electric current for not less than five years, to exclusion of all other artificial illumination, void in that respect. Ops. Atty Gen. 1913-14, p. 566. 

2. Department has no power to make rule requiring a public service company to accept warrants of an irrigation district as payment for electricity furnished to the district. Op. Atty. Gen. May 6, 1921. 

3. Department has power to determine who shall be allowed to represent parties in hearings and proceedings before it and Department should not allow laymen or non-members of the bar who are not parties in interest to

engage in representative functions involving taking of testimony, filing of pleadings, formal hearing and formation of record whereon an appeal may be taken to the courts, except in matters as rate fixing or matters not requiring a high degree of skill. Op. Atty. Gen. Sept. 17, 1941. 

80.04.170 Review of orders. Any complainant or any public service company affected by any findings or order of the commission, and deeming them contrary to law, may, within thirty days after the service of the findings or order upon him or it, apply to the superior court of Thurston county for a writ of review, for the purpose of having the reasonableness and lawfulness of the findings or order inquired into and determined. 

The writ shall be made returnable not later than thirty days from the date of its issuance, unless upon notice to all parties affected further time is allowed by the court, and shall direct the commission to certify its record in the case to the court. The cause shall be heard by the court without a jury, on the evidence and exhibits introduced before the commission and certified to by it. Upon the hearing the court shall enter judgment either affirming or setting aside or remanding for further action the findings or order of the commission. 

The reasonable cost of preparing the transcript of testimony taken before the commission shall be assessable as part of the court costs, and the amount thereof, if collected by the commission, shall be deposited in the public service revolving fund. In case the findings or order are set aside, or reversed and remanded, the court shall make specific findings based upon evidence in the record indicating clearly all respects in which the commission's findings or order are erroneous. [1937 c 169 § 3; 1911 c 117 § 86; RRS § 10428.] 

1. Supreme court will not dismiss an appeal upon motion because superior court did not have jurisdiction. If jurisdiction of superior court is involved, jurisdiction of supreme court may be invoked. State ex rel. Pac. P. & L. Co. v. 

Public Service Comm., 77 Wash. 1 (1913). 

2. An order by the Commission that certain improvements be made in the water system and that plans therefor be submitted to the Commission for approval within a certain time, is not a final order from which an appeal may be taken. State ex rel. Pac. P. & L. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 81 Wash. 

24 (1914). 

3. If order of Commission is rendered and is not attacked in accordance with the provisions of this section, it is conclusive on all parties unless void for lack of authority.

Void order may be attacked collaterally. State ex rel. Public Service 

Comm. v. Skagit River T. & T. Co., 85 Wash. 29 (1915). 

4. A public service corporation cannot maintain an action to set aside an order of the Commission, made without notice, annulling its previous judgment fixing rates, since the only remedy is by writ of review, in view of the sections providing for such review and that an order is conclusive unless set aside in the review provided for in the act. Willapa Power Co. v. Public Service Comm., 110 Wash. 193 (1920). 

5. Only company valued can appeal from valuation. City of Everett v.

Dept. of Public Works, 125 Wash. 341 (1923). 

6. Findings of Department are entitled to same presumption of verity accorded to findings of regular judicial tribunal and will be sustained unless against the clear weight of the evidence. 

The fact that complaint is filed by Department does not change the rule.

Department may make its order retroactive to the date jurisdiction was acquired. Pac. Coast Elev. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 130 Wash. 620 (1924). 

7. An application for a writ of certiorari to review valuation findings as a basis for telephone rates and service, made eighteen months after the order, is not timely, although the time is not fixed by statute. State ex rel. Homé 

Telephone & Telegraph Co. v. Kuykendall, 134 Wash. 620 (1925). 

8. Courts in review proceedings consider questions of law and incidentally pass on questions of fact. Department is the tribunal for determining questions involving business, engineering, and economic situations and requiring expert investigations and careful study. Changes in personnel of Department after hearing does not prevent entry of order as the new members have the entire record before them. State ex rel. Am. Telechronometer Co. v. Baker, 164 

Wash. 483 (1931). 

9. On review from the Department, the trial court may, in its discretion, reverse an order and remand the cause for further action, as expressly provided in this section. West Coast Tel. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 175 Wash. 

301 (1933). 

10. Findings of the Department are prima facie correct, but the facts found must be sufficient to justify the order of the Department, and there must also be substantial evidence to sustain the findings. State ex rel. Puget Sound 

Power & Light Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 181 Wash. 105 (1935). 

11. Separate applications for writ of review by each of three public service companies affected by an order of the Department held not defective for failure to make other two companies parties defendant to each application where each company operated individually and was individually affected by the order. State ex rel. P. S. P. & L. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 179 Wash. 

461 (1934). 

12. Presumption is that an order of the Department is correct and burden of proving it otherwise is on him seeking to overthrow it. Here Department placed too much emphasis on cost of reproduction and final valuation was arbitrary. State ex rel. O. W. Water Service Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 

184 Wash. 460 (1935). 

13. Remedy provided by this section does not preclude an action for a declaratory judgment to determine the status of a corporation as a public service corporation and its legal relation under the Public Service law. Inland 

Empire Rural Electrification Inc. v. Dept. of Public Service, 199 Wash. 527 

(1939). 

14. Act does not authorize recovery from a utility company as costs, of legal fees and other expenses of the Attorney General incurred in defending the Department in legal proceedings arising as a result of the Department's order. State v. Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co., 27 Wn. (2d) 893 (1947). 

15. A departmental order based upon a superior court judgment following a Supreme Court remittitur permitting a public utility to pass on to subscriber in its rate structure any and all taxes whether denominated occupation, excise, business or use-of-streets, was proper and in compliance with the superior court judgment and Supreme Court decision. State ex rel. Seattle v. 

Dept. of Public Utilities, 33 Wn. (2d) 896 (1949). 

16. A judgment of the superior court entered upon a remittitur in obedience to an order of the Supreme Court becomes the law of the case. Id. 

17. Denial by the Supreme Court of a petition for the recall of a remittitur and correction of a judgment with regard to occupation, use-of-streets and other taxes in effect approved the judgment entered by the superior court. Id. 

18. Under this section, the Department can recover, in cases where it is entitled to costs, only the cost of preparing the original transcript-and the ordinary attorney and court fees. Op. Atty. Gen. Aug. 25, 1941. 

80.04.180 Supersedeas. The pendency of a writ of review shall not of itself stay or suspend the operation of the order of the commission, but the court may restrain or suspend, in whole or in part, the

operation of the order pending the final hearing and determination of the cause.

No order restraining or suspending an order of the commission relating to rates, charges, tolls, or rentals, or rules or regulations, practices, classifications, or contracts affecting them, shall be made by the court otherwise than upon three days' notice and after hearing, and if a supersedeas is granted, the order granting it shall contain a specific finding, based upon evidence submitted to the court, and identified by reference thereto, that great or irreparable damage would otherwise result to the petitioner, and specifying the nature of the damage.

In case the order of the commission under review is superseded, the court shall require a bond, with good and sufficient surety, conditioned that the company petitioning for review shall answer for all damages caused by the delay in the enforcement of the order of the commission, and all compensation for whatever sums for transmission or service any person shall be compelled to pay pending the review proceedings in excess of the sum such person would have been compelled to pay if the order of the commission had not been suspended.

The court, in addition to, or in lieu of, the bond herein provided for, may require such other or further security for the payment of the excess charges or damages as it may deem proper. [1933 c 165 

§ 6; 1911 c 117 § 87; RRS § 10429.] 1. Temporary relief by U. S. Courts-denial of supersedeas. Pacific Tel. 

& Tel. Co. v. Kuykendall, 265 U. S. 196. 

2. Mandamus by court of appeals to compel hearing by district judge. 

Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co. v. Cushman, 292 Fed. 930. 

80.04.190 Appeal to supreme court. The commission, any company or any complainant may, within twenty days after the entry of judgment in the superior court in any action of review, prosecute an appeal to the supreme court.
