Franchises on state highways: 

Chapter 47.44. 

80.32.010 Cities and counties may grant franchises. The legislative authority of a city or the board of county commissioners may grant authority for the construction, maintenance, and operation of lines for transmitting electricity, together with all necessary appurtenances, on or across any public road or street within its jurisdiction and may prescribe the terms and conditions thereof and the grade and elevation. [1903 c 173 § 1, part; RRS § 5430, part.] 80.32.020 Hearing-Notice-Order. When application for such grant of authority is made to a county, the board of county commissioners shall fix a time and place of hearing thereon, and the county auditor shall, at the expense of the applicant, post notice of such hearing in three public places in the county seat and in at least one place on the road to be affected, at least fifteen days before the hearing, and publish a like notice three times in a daily newspaper published in the county, and if there is no such newspaper, then in the newspaper doing the county printing; the last publication to be at least five days before the hearing. The notice shall give the time and place of the hearing, the name of the applicant and a description of the road covered by the application. The hearing may be adjourned without further notice.

After the hearing, the board may enter an order granting the application in whole or in part as it deems for the public interest, and shall prescribe the location of the line so as to cause the least interference with other uses of the road. [1903 c 173 § 1, part; RRS § 5430, part.] 

80.32.030 Franchise holder liable for damage to street or road.

If the transmission line is located in part on a private right-of-way,

it may cross any public road, doing no unnecessary damage thereby. 

The person constructing a transmission line along or across a public road shall be liable to the county for all necessary expense to the county for restoring the road to its former condition. [1903 c 173 

§ 1, part; RRS § 5430, part.] 

80.32.040 Grant of franchise subject to referendum. A franchise to conduct or distribute electric current granted by the legislative authority of a city is subject to popular referendum under general laws or the city charter, if the petition therefor is filed within six months from the day such franchise was granted. [(i) 1941 c 114 § 1; 

Rem. Supp. 1941 § 5430-1. (ii) 1941 c 114 § 2; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 54302.] 

80.32.050 Sale or lease of plant and franchises. Any corporation organized under the laws of any state or territory of the United States, for the purpose of manufacturing, transmitting, or selling electricity, may lease or purchase and operate the whole or any part of the plant and equipment for manufacturing or distributing electricity of any other corporation, together with its franchises and other rights connected therewith.

Such lease or sale shall be made only upon the consent of the holders of at least two-thirds in amount of the capital stock of the lessor or grantor. [1903 c 173 § 3; RRS § 5431.] 

80.32.060 Eminent domain. Every corporation incorporated under the laws of any state or territory of the United States, and doing business in this state, for the purpose of manufacturing or transmitting electric power, shall have the right to appropriate real estate and other property for right-of-way or for any corporate purpose, in the same manner and under the same procedure as provided by law in the case of ordinary railroad corporations authorized by the laws of this state to exercise the right of eminent domain: 

Provided, That such right of eminent domain shall not be exercised with respect to any public road or street until the location of the transmission line thereon has been authorized in accordance with this chapter. [1903 c 173 § 2.] 

80.32.070 Right of entry. Such corporation may enter upon any land between the termini of the proposed lines for the purpose of examining, locating and surveying the lines, doing no unnecessary damage thereby. [1899 c 94 § 2; RRS § 11085.] 

Reviser's note: The 1899 act was superseded by 1903 c 173 and 175 but there was no express repeal in the 1903 acts so the nonconflicting part of the 1899 act still stands. 

80.32.080 Duties of electrical companies exercising power of eminent domain. Any corporation authorized to do business in this state, which is authorized to condemn property for the purpose of generating and transmitting electrical power for the operation of

railroads, or railways, or for municipal lighting, and which by its charter or articles of incorporation, assumes the additional right to sell electric power and electric light to private consumers outside the limits of a municipality and to sell electric power to private consumers within the limits of a municipality, which shall provide in its articles that in respect of such purposes it will assume and undertake to the state and to the inhabitants thereof the duties and obligations of a public service corporation, shall be deemed to be in respect of such purposes a public service corporation.

Any such corporation may sell electric light outside the limits of a municipality and electric power both inside and outside such limits to private consumers from the.electricity generated and transmitted by it for public purposes and not needed by it therefor. 

It shall furnish such excess power at equal rates, quantity and conditions considered, to all consumers alike, and shall supply it to the first applicants therefor until the amount available shall be exhausted. It shall not be obliged to furnish such excess power to any one consumer to an amount exceeding twenty-five percent of the total amount of the excess power generated or transmitted by it. 

In exercising the power of eminent domain for public purposes it shall not be an objection thereto that a portion of the electric current generated will be applied to private purposes, provided the principal uses intended are public. No public service or quasi public service corporation shall sell, deliver, and dispose of electrical power in bulk to manufacturing concerns at the expense of its public service functions, and any person who is a patron of such corporation as to such public function may by court action correct any violation of the provisions of this section. [1907 c 159 § 1; RRS § 5432.] 

80.32.090 Limitation on use of electricity. Whenever any corporation has acquired property by appropriation under the provisions of RCW 80.32.080, no portion of the electricity generated or transmitted by it by means of such property shall be used or applied by the corporation to a business or trade not under present laws deemed public or quasi public conducted by itself. [1907 c 159 § 2; RRS § 5433.] 

80.32.100 Remedy for violations. In the event of the violation. of any of the requirements of RCW 80.32.080 and 80.32.090 by any corporation availing itself of their provisions, suit may be maintained in the name of the state upon the relation of the attorney general, or, if he fails to act, upon the relation of any individual aggrieved by the violation to compel the corporation to comply therewith. Such violation shall cause the forfeiture of the corporate franchise if the corporation fails to comply with the order of the court. [1907 c 159 § 3; RRS § 5434.]

80.36.010 Eminent domain. The right of eminent domain is extended to all telegraph and telephone companies organized or doing business in this state. [1890 p 292 § 1; RRS § 11338.] 

80.36.020 Right of entry. Every corporation incorporated under the laws of any state or territory of the United States, for the purpose of constructing, operating, or maintaining any telegraph or

www. telephone in this state, may enter upon any land between the termini of its proposed lines for the purpose of examining, locating, and surveying the lines, doing no unnecessary damage thereby. [1888 

p 65 § 1; RRS § 11339.] 

80.36.030 Extent of appropriation. Any telegraph or telephone company may appropriate so much land as may be actually necessary for its line of telegraph or telephone, with the right to enter upon land immediately adjacent thereto, for the purpose of constructing, maintaining, and operating its lines and making all necessary repair. It may also, for such purpose, enter upon and appropriate such portion of the right-of-way of any railroad company as may be necessary for the construction, maintenance, and operation, of its line: Provided, That such appropriation shall not interfere with the operation of the railroad or the travel thereon. [1888 p 66 § 2; RRS § 11342.] 

80.36.040 Use of roads and streets. Any telegraph or telephone company, or the lessees thereof, doing business in this state, may construct and maintain all necessary lines of telegraph or telephone for public traffic along and upon any public road, street, or highway, along or across the right-of-way of any railroad corporation, and may erect poles, posts, piers, or abutments for supporting the insulators, wires, and other necessary fixtures of their lines, in such manner and at such points as not to interfere with the public use of the railroad or highway, or interrupt the navigation of the waters: 

Provided, That when the right-of-way of such corporation has not been acquired by any grant from the United States, or this state, or any county, city, or town therein, the right to construct and maintain such lines shall be secured only by the exercise of the right of eminent domain and where the right-of-way is within the corporate limits of any city, the consent of the city council thereof shall be first obtained before such lines can be erected thereon. [1890 p 292 

§ 5; RRS § 11352.] 

80.36.050 Use of railroad rights-of-way. Every railroad operated in this state is a "post road" and the owning company shall allow any telegraph and telephone company to construct and maintain telegraph and telephone lines on its right-of-way. A violation of this section shall be punished by a fine of not less than one thousand dollars nor more than five thousand dollars and one hundred dollars for each day's continuance thereof. [(i) 1890 p 292 § 3; RRS § 11340. 

(ii) 1890 p 293 § 9; RRS § 11356.] 

80.36.060 Liability for wilful injury to telephone or telegraph property. Any person who wilfully and maliciously does any injury to any necessary or useful fixtures of a telegraph or telephone property is liable to the company for five times the amount of actual damages sustained thereby. [1890 p 293 § 7; RRS § 11354.]

80.36.070 Liability for negligent injury to property. Any person who injures or destroys, through want of proper care, any necessary or useful fixtures of any telegraph or telephone company is liable to the company for all damages sustained thereby. 

Any vessel which breaks, injures, or destroys the underwater cable of a telegraph or telephone company subjects its owners to the damages hereinbefore specified: Provided, That the company has placed a monument on either bank of the water under which the cable lies, indicating the location of the cable, and published for one month in a newspaper most likely to give notice to navigators, a notice giving a description and the purpose of the monument and the general course, landings, and termini of the cable. [(i) 1890 p 293 

§ 6; RRS § 11353. (ii) 1890 p 293 § 10; RRS § 11357.] 

80.36.080 Rates, services and facilities. All rates, tolls, contracts and charges, rules and regulations, of telephone and telegraph companies, for messages, conversations, services rendered, and equipment and facilities supplied, whether such message, conversation, or service to be performed is over one company or line or over or by two or more companies or lines, shall be fair, just, reasonable, and sufficient, and the service shall be rendered and performed in a prompt, expeditious, and efficient manner and the facilities, instrumentalities, and equipment furnished by it shall be safe, kept in good condition and repair, and its appliances, instrumentalities, and service shall be modern, adequate, sufficient, and efficient. [1911 

c 117 § 35, part; RRS § 10371, part.] 

1. Rule of telephone company making rentals payable in advance and adding fifty cents penalty is reasonable. State ex rel. McMahon v. Ind. Tel. Co., 59 Wash. 156 (1910). 

2. Courts have no jurisdiction in the first instance of alleged damages for "unjust" practices by company. Robinson v. Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co., 118 Wash. 318 (1922). 

3. Order of Department requiring removal_of telechronometers and return of flat rate affirmed. State ex rel. Am. Telechronometer Co. v. Dept. 

Public Works, 164 Wash. 483 (1931). 

4. Telephone company not authorized to require trustee in bankruptcy to pay arrears of bankrupt as condition precedent to rendering him service. Ops. 

Atty. Gen. 1913-14, p. 374. 

5. Commission has no authority to approve or disapprove the transfer of one telephone company's line to another company, but it may determine whether or not it will order either company to continue the service now being rendered. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1915-16, p. 45. 

6. Telephone system which affords connections to people living within 

Washington, but passes through a central exchange in Idaho, is interstate and subject to the jurisdiction of the Interstate Commerce Commission. Op. Atty. 

Gen. March 21, 1924. 

7. Telephone company may engage in both public and private service and may lease to private concerns facilities not needed for its public service. Op. 

Atty. Gen. March 30, 1927. 

8. American Telechronometer Company is not furnishing service to the public for hire and is not therefore a public service company. Ops. Atty. Gen. 

April 23, 1928, May 17, 1928.

9. Right to render or duty to refuse service that may facilitate billing.

153 A.L.R. 463. 

10. Nothing in the act requires that the telegraph department of a railroad be treated as an independent company. Op. Atty. Gen. April 24, 1916. 

11. Where common interest exists between two contiguous communities, the Commission has power to order extended area service between the two separate telephone companies if it can be arranged without loss to either company. Op. Atty. Gen. October 14, 1938. 

12. Department may render engineering services to utilities customers and prospective customers, informing them as to lawful charges utility may make and means whereby such charges could be reduced. Op. Atty. Gen. Sept. 

25, 1941. 

13. Under terms of franchise binding telephone company to furnish a certain amount of free phone service for city for "strictly municipal purposes," company must furnish telephones for use in civilian defense centers. Op. Atty. 

Gen. June 8, 1943. 

14. Proposed bill preventing telephone company from changing telephone number of subscriber without his consent would be unconstitutional. Op. 

Atty. Gen. February 14, 1947. 

80.36.090 Service to be furnished on demand. Every such company operating in this state shall provide and maintain suitable and adequate buildings and facilities therein, or connected therewith, for the accommodation, comfort, and convenience of its patrons and employees.

Every telephone company shall, upon reasonable notice, furnish to all persons who apply therefor and are reasonably entitled thereto, suitable and proper facilities and connections for telephonic communication and furnish telephone service as demanded. [1911 c 117 

§ 35, part; RRS § 10371, part.] 

1. Telephone company is not entitled to refuse to install a telephone for the non-payment of telephone charges by someone else, even though the name used was the same. Op. Atty. Gen. January 14, 1931. 

80.36.100 Tariff schedules to be filed. Every telephone and telegraph company shall file with the commission and shall print and keep open to public inspection at such points as the commission may designate, schedules showing its rates, tolls, rentals, contracts, and charges for messages, conversations, and services rendered and equipment and facilities supplied for messages and services to be performed within the state between each point upon its line and all other points thereon, and between each point upon its line and all points upon every other similar line operated or controlled by it, and between each point on its line or upon any line leased, operated, or controlled by it and all points upon the line of any other similar company, whenever a through service and joint rate has been established or ordered between any two such points. If no joint rate covering a through service has been established, the several companies in such through service shall file, print, and keep open to public inspection as aforesaid the separately established rates, tolls, rentals, contracts, and charges applicable for such through service. 

The schedules shall plainly state the places between which tele-

phone or telegraph service, or both, will be rendered, and shall also state separately all charges and all privileges or facilities granted or allowed, and any rules or regulations or forms of contract which may in any wise change, affect, or determine any of the aggregate of the rates, tolls, rentals, or charges for the service rendered. A schedule shall be plainly printed in large type, and a copy thereof shall be kept by every such company readily accessible to and for convenient inspection by the public at such places as may be designated by the commission, which schedule shall state the rates charged from such station to every other station on such company's line, or on any line controlled and used by it within the state. Any such schedule shall be immediately produced by the company upon the demand of any person. A notice printed in bold type, and stating that such schedules are on file and open to inspection by any person, the places where they are kept, and that the agent will assist such person to determine from the schedules any rate, toll, rental, rule or regulation which is in force, shall be kept posted by every such company in a conspicuous place in every station or office of the company. [1911 c 117 § 36; RRS § 10372.] 

1. Where a temporary schedule of rates for a telephone company was put into force by an order of a federal court in a suit to enjoin enforcement of rates fixed by a state commission, the right of the company to charge such rates is not affected by its failure to file the schedule as required by state statute where it had been enjoined by a state court from making such filing. 

Pacific Telephone & Telegraph Co. v. Agnew, 5 Fed. (2d) 221 (1925); reversing 

2 Fed. (2d) 155. 2. In a hearing before the Department for an order directing telephone connections between the relator and defendant company, other companies having connections with the defendant are necessary parties to the proceeding, without whose presence there can be no valid judgment. 

Where there was a failure to bring in the necessary parties on an application to the Department for an order for telephone connections, the defect cannot be supplied upon an appeal to the superior court to review the decision of the Department. State ex rel. Dept. of Public Works v. Pacific Tel. & Tel. 

Co., 144 Wash. 383 (1927). 

80.36.110 Tariff changes-Statutory notice Exception. Unless the commission otherwise orders, no change shall be made in any rate, toll, rental, contract, or charge, which has been filed and published by any telephone or telegraph company, except after thirty days' notice to the commission and publication for thirty days, which notice shall plainly state the changes proposed to be made in the schedule then in force, and the time when the changed rate, toll, contract, or charge will go into effect, and all proposed changes shall be shown by printing, filing, and publishing new schedules, or shall be plainly indicated upon the schedules in force at the time and kept open to public inspection. The commission for good cause shown may allow changes in rates, charges, tolls, rentals, or contracts without requiring the thirty days' notice and publication, by an order specifying the change so to be made and the time when it shall take effect, and the manner in which it shall be filed and published. When any change is made which increases any rate, toll, rental, or charge

then existing, attention shall be directed on the copy filed with the commission to such increase by some character immediately preceding or following the item in such schedule, which character shall be in such form as the commission may designate. [1911 c 117 § 37; 

RRS § 10373.] 

1. Change of telephone rate while another rate is undetermined. 16 A.L.R. 1219. 

80.36.120 Joint rates, contracts, etc. The names of the several companies which are parties to any joint rates, tolls, contracts, or charges of telephone or telegraph companies for messages, conversations, and service to be rendered shall be specified therein, and each of the parties thereto, other than the one filing it, shall file with the commission such evidence of concurrence therein or acceptance thereof as may be required or approved by it; and where such evidence of concurrence or acceptance is filed, it shall not be necessary for the companies filing the same to also file copies of the tariff in which they are named as parties. [1911 c 117 § 38; RRS § 10374.] 

80.36.130 Published rates to be charged-Exceptions. No telephone or telegraph company shall charge, demand, collect, or receive different compensation for any service rendered or to be rendered than the charge applicable to such service as specified in its schedule on file and in effect at that time, nor shall it refund or remit, directly or indirectly, any portion of the rate or charge so specified, nor extend to any person any form of contract or agreement or any rule or regulation or any privilege or facility except such as are specified in its schedule, and regularly and uniformly extended to all persons under like circumstances for like or substantially similar service. 

No such company shall, directly or indirectly, give any free or reduced service or any free pass or frank for the transmission of messages by either telephone or telegraph between points within this state, except to its officers, employees, agents, pensioners, surgeons, physicians, attorneys at law, and their families; persons exclusively engaged in charitable and eleemosynary work; ministers of religion; 

Young Men's Christian Associations; Young Women's Christian As sociations; indigent and destitute persons, and officers and employees of other telephone and telegraph companies and railroad and street railroad companies.

Passes or franks for their officers, attorneys, agents, employees, and their families may be exchanged by telephone and telegraph companies with other such companies and with railroad, express, and sleeping car companies. [(i) 1911 c 117 § 40; RRS § 10376. (ii) 

1929 c 96 § 1, part; 1911 c 117 § 18; RRS § 10354.] 

1. See 2 Fed. (2d) 155 and 5 Fed. (2d) 221. 

2. If telephone company does not wish to extend reduced rate to minister teaching at a college, it need not do so, and Commission cannot require it because this section makes such practice discretionary with the company. Op. 

Atty. Gen. December 13, 1913.

80.36.140 Commission to fix just, reasonable and compensatory rates. Whenever the commission finds, after hearing had upon its own motion or upon complaint, that the rates, charges, tolls, or rentals charged or collected by any telegraph company or telephone company for the transmission of messages by telegraph or telephone, or for the rental or use of any line, instrument, or apparatus, or any telephone extension or extension system, or that its rules, regulations, or practices affecting such rates, charges, tolls, rentals, or service are unjust, unreasonable, unjustly discriminatory, or unduly preferential, or in any wise in violation of law, or that such rates, charges, tolls, or rentals are insufficient to yield reasonable compensation for the service rendered, the commission shall determine the just and reasonable rates, charges, tolls, or rentals to be thereafter observed, and fix them by order.

Whenever the commission finds, after hearing, that the rules, regulations or practices of any such company are unjust or unreasonable, or that its equipment, facilities, or service is inadequate, inefficient, improper, or insufficient, it shall determine the just, reasonable, proper, adequate, and efficient rules, regulations, practices, equipment, facilities, and service to be thereafter installed, observed and used, and fix them by order or rule. [1911 c 117 § 55; 

RRS § 10391.] 

1. Duty to furnish service and facilities: A telephone company will not be compelled by mandamus to publish on or about April 15 a directory of subscribers to its telephone system, where it had answered that it was then engaged in compiling and publishing such a directory in the regular course of its business, which it would have ready for distribution early in the month of 

April. State ex rel. Bauer v. Sunset Tel. & Tel. Co., 30 Wash. 676 (1903). 

2. The rule of a telephone company that its rentals shall be payable monthly in advance, and fifty cents added as a collection fee if not paid on the tenth of the month, is a reasonable rule which such a company may enforce as a public service corporation; and the fifty-cent fee is not in addition to the maximum charge allowed by its franchise, but is a reasonable regulation and charge for default and delinquency. State ex rel. MacMahon v. Independent 

Tel. Co., 59 Wash. 156; 31 L.R.A. (N.S.) 329 (1910). 

3. Under this section the Commission has power to require a telephone company to raise its rates above the maximum permitted by its city franchise, where such increased rates have become necessary to provide sufficient revenue to give an adequate service. State ex rel. Webster v. Superior Court, 67 

Wash. 37; Ann. Cas. 1913D 78; L.R.A. 1915C 287 (1912). 

4. Where one telephone company has by contract opened its lines to physical connection and services for another telephone company upon certain terms, its act is equivalent to a declaration of a purpose to waive its primary right of independence, and it can be required, as a state regulation within the police power, to accord the same facilities, conveniences and uses to other telephone companies upon equal terms. State ex rel. Public Service Comm. v. 

Skagit River Tel. & Tel. Co., 85 Wash. 29 (1915) and, see also, 89 Wash. 625 (1916). 

5. A telephone franchise ordinance limiting the amount of the rates or charges is a contract obligation, binding until abrogated by the state in the exercise of its police power; and the company having accepted and agreed upon the terms cannot complain thereof until relieved by the Commission. 

State ex rel. Ellertsen v. Home Tel. & Tel. Co., 102 Wash. 196 (1918).

6. During period of control of telegraph and telephone by the postmaster general, Commission had no jurisdiction to make orders approving or making effective any tariff rate to be charged by telegraph or telephone systems. 

State v. Public Service Comm., 110 Wash. 130 (1920). 

7. State department's order establishing rates of telephone company had effect of abrogating franchise rates and establishing department or statutory rates. Pacific Telephone & Telegraph Co. v. Whitcomb, 12 Fed. (2d) 279 (1926); affirmed 276 U. S. 97, 72 L. Ed. 483. 

8. In hearing suit by a telephone company to enjoin department's rate order on the ground the rates fixed were confiscatory, federal court is not engaging in rate fixing. Id. 

80.36.150 Contracts to be filed with commission. Every telephone and telegraph company shall file with the commission, as and when required by it, a copy of any contract, agreement or arrangement in writing with any other such company, or with any other person relating in any way to the construction, maintenance, or use of a telephone or telegraph line or service by, or rates and charges over and upon, any such telephone or telegraph line. [1911 c 117 § 39; 

RRS § 10375.] 

1. In contract between electrical company and its customer, a clause binding customer to use that company's electricity to exclusion of all others for a period of five years is against public policy and would not be upheld by courts. 

Op. Atty. Gen. December 21, 1914. 

80.36.160 Physical connections may be ordered. In order to provide toll telephone service where no such service is available, or to promote the most expeditious handling or most direct routing of toll messages and conversations, or to prevent arbitrary or unreasonable practices which may result in the failure to utilize the toll facilities of all telephone companies equitably and effectively, the commission may, on its own motion, or upon complaint, notwithstanding any contract or arrangement between telephone companies, investigate, ascertain and, after hearing, by order, (1) require the construction and maintenance of suitable connections between telephone lines for the transfer of messages and conversations at a common point or points and, if the companies affected fail to agree on the proportion of the cost thereof to be borne by each company, prescribe the proportion of cost to be borne by each; (2) prescribe the routing of toll messages and conversations over such connections and the practices and regulations to be followed with respect to such routing; or (3) establish reasonable joint rates or charges by or over said lines and connections and just, reasonable, and equitable divisions thereof as between the companies participating therein. It may resort to all of these remedies simultaneously. 

This section shall not be construed as conferring on the commission jurisdiction, supervision, or control of the rates, service, or facilities of any mutual, cooperative, or farmer line company or association, except for the purpose of carrying out the provisions hereof. [1943 c 68 § 1, last am'ds 1911 c 117 § 73; Rem. Supp. 1943 § 10409.]

1. For two cases involving the same parties and the same matter in the second of which the court admits and rectifies a mistake of law made in the first, see State ex rel. Public Service Commission v. Skagit River Tel. & Tel. 

Co., 85 Wash. 29 (1915) and 89 Wash. 625 (1916). Cases involve Commission's order requiring physical connections between two telephone companies. 

2. In a hearing before the Department for an order directing telephone connections between the relator and the defendant company, other companies having connections with the defendant are necessary parties to the proceeding, without whose presence there can be no valid judgment where their lines constitute a web of lines serving the plaintiff's territory and they claim certain exclusive rights to have and maintain connections with the defendant company. State ex rel. Dept. of Public Works v. Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co., 144 Wash. 383 (1927). 

3. Validity of contracts between telephone companies for exclusive connections. 36 L.R.A. (N.S.) 124. 

4. Regulations or provisions upon requiring physical connection of telephone lines. 16 A.L.R. 352. 

5. Right and duty of telephone companies to make or discontinue physical connection of exchanges or lines. 76 A.L.R. 953. 

6. Cooperative telephone company having connection with a public telephone company and doing toll business must file annual reports covering business done through such connection, and must pay fee on the gross operating revenue derived from charges accruing through such connected service. Op. 

Atty. Gen. March 9, 1927. 

7. Farmer's Mutual Telephone Co. leasing pole space to a public telephone company does not thereby become a public service company. Op. Atty. Gen. 

March 9, 1927. 

8. Under this section the Commission may take jurisdiction over a dispute between two telephone companies where it appears that their inability to reach agreement as to division of toll charges may result in unreasonable practices or failure to utilize available facilities. Dept. Memo. October 15, 1943. 

9. An action for damages grounded on the poor service rendered by a telephone company to a subscriber is not within the jurisdiction of the courts in the first instance, since under this section the remedy for unjust and unreasonable practices on the part of a public service corporation must be sought by application to the Commission. Robinson v. Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co., 118 Wash. 318 (1922). 

10. Under this section the Department, in order to determine the reasonableness of rates for a metered telephone service, has power to authorize tests and prescribe experimental rates for stated periods until the further order of the Department. Everett v. Dept. of Public Works, 125 Wash. 526 (1923). 

11. Upon affirming an order fixing the value of public service property of a telephone company for rate-making purposes, there can be no question of confiscation of the property until there has been an attempt to apply the order of valuation to the fixing of rates so low as to confiscate the property. Columbia River Tel. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 154 Wash. 32 (1929). 

12. Under this section and others of this act, the Department had the right to inquire into the system employed by a telephone company in computing charges and into mechanical devices used for that purpose. State ex rel. 

American Telechronometer Co. v. Baker, 164 Wash. 483 (1931). 

13. Real estate acquired by a telephone company with expectations of erecting buildings thereon and at a time when business was rapidly expanding, should be included in the rate base even though such property was not at the time being used in the conduct of business. State ex rel. Pacific Telephone & 

Telegraph Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 19 Wn. (2d) 200 (1943). 

14. Rate of return for telephone company fixed by Department at 5 per cent was not so low as to amount to confiscation. Id. 

80.36.170 Unreasonable preferences prohibited. No telegraph or telephone company shall make or give any undue or unreasonable

preference or advantage to any person, corporation, or locality, or subject any particular person, corporation, or locality to any undue or unreasonable prejudice or disadvantage. [1911 c 117 § 42; RRS 

§ 10378.] 

80.36.180 Rate discrimination prohibited. No telegraph or telephone company shall, directly or indirectly, or by any special rate, rebate, drawback or other device or method, charge, demand, collect, or receive from any person a greater or less compensation for any service rendered or to be rendered with respect to communication by telegraph or telephone, or in connection therewith, except as authorized in this chapter, than it charges, demands, collects, or receives from any other person for doing a like and contemporaneous service with respect to communication by telegraph or telephone under the same or substantially the same circumstances. [1911 c 117 

§ 41; RRS § 10377.] 

1. A telephone company is not bound to give another company or its patrons connection with its switchboard on an equality with its own patrons in absence of statutory provision requiring it; nor does the fact that it has granted such right to one company entitle others to the same privilege. Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co. v. Anderson, 196 Fed. 699 (1912). 

2. Discrimination by telegraph company in respect to extension of credit. 

12 A.L.R. 964. 

3. Franchise provisions for free or reduced rates as within constitutional or statutory provision prohibiting discrimination. 15 A.L.R. 1200. 

80.36.190 Long and short distance provision. No telegraph or telephone company shall charge or receive any greater compensation in the aggregate for the transmission of any long distance conversation or message of like kind for a shorter than for a longer distance over the same line, in the same direction, within this state, the shorter being included within the longer distance, or charge any greater compensation for a through service than the aggregate of the intermediate rates, but this shall not be construed as authorizing it to charge and receive as great a compensation for a shorter as for a longer distance. Upon application of any such company the commission may, by order, authorize it to charge less for longer than for a shorter distance service for the transmission of conversation or messages in special cases, but the order must prescribe the extent to which the company making such application is relieved from the operation of this section, and only to the extent so prescribed shall it be relieved from the requirements of this section. [1911 c 117 § 44; 

RRS § 10380.] 

1. Opinion treating with violations of this section by comparison of interstate and intrastate rates. Op. Atty. Gen. August 21, 1941. 

80.36.200 Messages of other lines. Every telegraph and telephone company operating in this state shall receive, transmit, and deliver, without discrimination or delay, the messages of any other telegraph or telephone company. [1911 c 117 § 45; RRS § 10381.]

80.36.210 Order of sending messages. Any telegraph company doing business in the state shall transmit all dispatches in the order in which they are received, under the penalty of one hundred dollars, to be recovered with costs of suit, by the person whose dispatch is postponed out of its order: Provided, That communications to and from public officers on official business may have precedence over all other communications, and intelligence of general and public interest may be transmitted for publication out of its order. [Code 

1881 § 2361; RRS § 11344. Prior: 1866 p 77 § 20.] 

80.36.220 Duty to transmit messages. Telegraph and telephone companies shall receive, exchange, and transmit each other's messages without delay or discrimination, and all telephone companies shall receive and transmit messages for any person. A violation of any provision of this section shall be punished by a fine of not less. than one hundred dollars nor more than five hundred dollars. [(i) 

1890 p 292 § 2; RRS § 11343. (ii) 1890 p 293 § 8; RRS § 11355.] 

80.36.230 Exchange areas for telephone companies. The commission may prescribe exchange area boundaries or territorial boundaries, or both, for telephone companies. [1941 c 137 § 1; Rem.

Supp. 1941 § 11358-1.] 

1. There is no reason why a farmers' telephone association should not be allowed to file a map with its tariff describing its exchange area boundaries, although such filing does not vest it with any exclusive or quasi-exclusive rights in the area. Dept. Memo. December 8, 1943. 

80.36.240 Procedure to establish. The commission in conducting hearings, promulgating rules, and otherwise proceeding to make effective the provisions of RCW 80.36.230, shall be governed by, and shall have the powers provided in, chapter 80.04 of this title. 

All provisions as to review of the commission's orders and appeals to the supreme court contained in said chapter shall be available to all parties affected by the commission's orders. [1941 c 137 § 2; Rem. 

Supp. 1941 § 11358-2.] 

80.36.250 Commission may complain of interstate rates. The commission may investigate all interstate rates and charges, classifications, or rules or practices relating thereto, for or in relation to the transmission of messages or conversations. Where any acts in relation thereto take place within this state which, in the opinion of the commission, are excessive or discriminatory, or are levied or laid in violation of the federal communications act of June 19, 1934, and acts amendatory thereof or supplementary thereto, or are in conflict with the rulings, orders, or regulations of the Federal Communications Commission, the commission shall apply by petition to the Federal Communications Commission for relief, and may present to such federal commission all facts coming to its knowledge respect-

ing violations of such act or the rulings, orders, or regulations of the federal commission. [1911 c 117 § 58; RRS § 10394.] 

1. Petition by Commission before Interstate Commerce Commission alleging certain interstate rates were unreasonable and excessive was sufficient to bring rates to test. Op. Atty. Gen. Dec. 15, 1915. 

80.36.260 Betterments may be ordered. Whenever the commission finds, after hearing had upon its own motion or upon complaint, that repairs or improvements to, or changes in, any telegraph or telephone line ought reasonably to be made, or that any additions or extensions should reasonably be made thereto in order to promote the security or convenience of the public or employees, or in order to secure adequate service or facilities for telegraphic or telephonic communications, it shall make and serve an order directing that such repairs, improvements, changes, additions, or extensions be made in the manner to be specified therein. [1911 c 117 § 71; RRS § 10407.] 

1. Department whether on its own or upon complaint, held authorized to order telephone company to remove telechronometers. State ex rel. American 

Telechronometer Co. v. Baker, 164 Wash. 483 (1931). 

80.36.270 Effect on existing contracts. Nothing in this chapter shall prevent any telegraph or telephone company from continuing to furnish the use of its line, equipment or service under any contract in force on June 7, 1911, at the rates fixed in such contract: 

Provided, That the commission may direct by order that such contract be terminated by the telegraph or telephone company party thereto, and thereupon such contract shall be terminated as directed by such order. [1911 c 117 § 43; RRS § 10379.] 

1. Commission may order telephone company to increase rates fixed by franchise, if necessary to provide adequate revenue. State ex rel. Webster v. 

Superior Court, 67 Wash. 37 (1912). 

2. Where there is no compliance with this section, a franchise limiting one-party residence rates to two dollars is not affected by the company's filing a schedule raising the rate to three dollars, until affirmative action is taken by the Commission. State ex rel. Ellertsen v. Home Tel. & Tel. Co., 102 Wash. 196 (1918). 

3. Overcharges before relief by Commission recovered in courts. State ex rel. Home Tel. & Tel. Co. v. Court, 110 Wash. 396 (1920). 

4. New rates are not protected by franchise. Denney v. Pacific Tel. & Tel. 

Co., 276 U. S. 97, 72 L. Ed. 483 (1928). 

5. State's power to change private contract rates. 9 A.L.R. 1435. 

6. Construction of franchise or statute as respects rates and service of telephone company. 14 A.L.R. 1385. 

7. Power of Public Service Commission to increase franchise rates. 29 

A.L.R. 356. (Chapters 80.40, 80.44 and 80.48 are not published herein as they involve matters over which Public Service Commission is without effective jurisdiction.)

81.04.010 Definitions. As used in this title, unless the context indicates otherwise: "Commission" means Washington public service commission; "Company" means a corporation, association, joint stock association, partnership, or person, their lessees, trustees, or receivers; "Street railroad" means every railroad, by whatever power operated, and any extension or branch thereof, for public use in the conveyance of persons or property mainly upon, above, or below any street or public place in a city, and all property used by or in connection with such railroad; "Street railroad company" means every company, and every city or town owning, controlling, operating, or managing any street railroad for hire; "Railroad" means every railroad other than a street railroad, by whatever power operated, for public use in the conveyance of persons or property, and all property used by or in connection with such railroad; "Railroad company" means every company owning, controlling, operating, or managing any railroad for hire; "Express company" means every company which engages in the business of carrying freight, merchandise, or property for hire on the line of any common carrier; "Common carrier" includes all railroads, railroad companies, street railroads, street railroad companies, steamboat companies, express companies, car companies, sleeping car companies, freight companies, freight line companies and every company and every city or town owning, controlling, operating, or managing any such agency for public use in the conveyance of persons or property for hire; "Vessel" means every species of watercraft, by whatever power operated, for public use in the conveyance of persons or property upon water, excepting all towboats, tugs, scows, barges, and lighters, and excepting rowboats and sailing boats under twenty tons gross burden, open steam launches of five tons gross and under, and

vessels under five tons gross propelled by gas, fluid, naphtha, or electric motors; "Steamboat company" means every company owning, controlling, operating, or managing any vessel for hire; "Conveyance of property" includes any service in connection with the receiving, delivery, elevation, transfer in transit, ventilation, refrigeration, icing, storage, and handling of the property transported, and the transmission of credit; "Conveyance of persons" includes any service in connection with the receiving, carriage and delivery of the person transported and his baggage and all facilities used, or necessary to be used, in connection with his safety, comfort, and convenience; "Public service company" means every common carrier; 

The term "service" is used in this title in its broadest and most inclusive sense. [1929 c 223 § 8, part, last am'ds 1911 c 117 § 8; RRS § 10344, part.] "street railroad company" a. Corporation owning a street railway line in the hands of a construction company completing it is a common carrier when it permits construction company to run cars and collect fare from passengers. Cogswell v. West 

Street & North End Electric Ry. Co., 5 Wash. 46 (1892) (1893). 

b. A railway company which performs the service of an ordinary street railroad in a city under a franchise granted by the city is, as to such service, a street railroad, though it is also an interurban railroad engaged in carrying passengers beyond the limits of the city, and the fact that the Commission has assumed jurisdiction over it does not prevent it from being subject to the franchise so far as it operates a system within the city. 

State v. Seattle R. & S. Ry. Co., 64 Wash. 167 (1911). c. Public Service Commission has exclusive jurisdiction to regulate and control street railways with reference to overcrowding of cars, operating in accordance with schedule, etc., and power of city thereover is revoked; and it is not necessary that the Commission should have acted in order to divest the city of its right since cities may invoke the aid of the Commission. 

Seattle Electric Co. v. City of Seattle, 78 Wash. 203 (1914). d. The Seattle, Renton & So. R. R. Co. is a street railroad within the meaning of this section and may issue free or reduced transportation (RCW 81- .28.080) to mail carriers. Ops. Atty Gen. 1911-12, p. 219. e. A street car company operating both within and without a city which substituted auto bus service for its street railway service must comply with all the provisions of the auto transportation act. Op. Atty. Gen. 

March 31, 1926. f. Company operating street railway system and electric light plants serving various cities comes within statutory definitions of "street railway" and "electric company." State v. Denney, 150 Wash. 690 (1929). "railroad" a. The term "railroad" as used in this section, includes every railroad other than a street railroad by whatsoever power operated, and the same general rules are to apply to all railroads and railroad equipment whether operated by steam or some other power. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1911-12, p. 241. b. Railway operated by the City of Seattle in connection with its Skagit 

River power plant from Rockford to the damsite and carrying persons and property for hire is a railroad company within the meaning of this act. 

Op. Atty. Gen. Oct. 23, 1930. "express company" a. An express company is a common carrier, but an interstate express company may renounce its intrastate business in order to escape a tax upon such business imposed by the state. State v. Northern Express Co., 80 Wash. 309 overruling 76 Wash. 636 (1914).

b. Forwarding company which picks up and delivers freight it ships consigned to itself is an "express company" and a "common carrier" subject to this act and must pay gross revenue fees applicable. Op. Atty. Gen. Feb. 

18, 1931. "common carrier" a. A corporation owning a street railway line in the hands of a construction company completing it is a common carrier when it permits construction company to run cars and collect fares from passengers. Cogs well v. West Street & North End Electric Ry. Co., 5 Wash. 46 (1892). 

b. Street railway companies are common carriers. State ex rel. Grinsfelder v. Spokane Street Ry. Co., 19 Wash. 518 (1898). c. Express company is a common carrier. See (a) under "express company. 

d. A forwarder of goods is a common carrier. Kettenhoffen v. Globe 

Transfer & Storage Co., 70 Wash. 645 (1912). e. Branch line of a railway completed but not turned over to operating department is not a common carrier. Op. Atty. Gen. Feb. 17, 1917. f. A logging railroad is not a common carrier even though it has intermittently carried freight as a common carrier. What is a common carrier is a question of fact. State ex rel. Silver Lake Ry. & Lumber Co. v. Public 

Service Comm., 117 Wash. 453 (1921). g. Freight forwarding company although not an auto transportation company is a common carrier and must file its tariffs and annual reports with the Department. Op. Atty. Gen. Feb. 18, 1931. h. Omission of term "motor buses" does not imply they are excluded from the jurisdiction of the Commission, there being no question but that they are affected with public interest. State ex rel. Spokane United Ry's v. Dept. of Public Service, 191 Wash. 595 (1937). 

i. Even if the expressed purpose of a forwarding company's business were materials, its designation that it was a "forwarder" and "distributor" would be sufficient to estop it from claiming that it was not a common carrier. Lee v. Fidelity Storage & Transfer Co., 51 Wash. 208 (1908). 

j. A transfer and storage company engaged in a business of warehousing goods and forwarding them for compensation in carload lots, held a common carrier and as such liable for the destruction of goods while in its warehouse. Note (d). k. A common carrier is one who transports persons or things from place to place for hire or reward and holds himself out to the world as ready and willing to serve the public indifferently, but if the undertaking be a single transaction, not a part of the general business engaged, the service is that of a private and not a common carrier. Cushing v. White, 101 Wash. 

172, L.R.A. 1918F 463 (1918). 

1. Question as to whether a corporation is a common carrier is one of fact, to be determined on the evidence. State v. Public Service Comm., 117 

Wash. 453 (1921). m. A common carrier is one whose occupation is the transportation of persons or things for hire and who holds himself out as ready to serve the public indifferently. Independent Truck Co. v. Wright, 151 Wash. 372 (1929). n. One engaged in casual hauling is not a common carrier. 

Aetna Life Ins. Co., 19 Wn. (2d) 601, 149 A.L.R. 1289 (1943). 

Larson v. o. While question of what constitutes a common carrier is one of law, the status of a carrier as such must be determined from his method of operation. 

Miles v. Enumclaw Co-op. Creamery Corp., 12 Wn. (2d) 377 (1942). "transportation of property" "transportation of persons" a. See notes under "express company." b. Public Service Commission has no jurisdiction over rates and charges of booming and driving companies for booming and driving of logs. Wishkah Boom Co. v. Greenwood Timber Co., 88 Wash. 568 (1915). c. The term "five gross tons" as used in this section relating to vessels engaged in the transportation of persons and property should be ascertained

according to the rules of the federal government relating to registry, enrollment, licensing, and inspection of vessels without making the deductions specified in said rules for the purpose of ascertaining the net or register tonnage. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1911-12, p. 198. "vessel" a. "Five gross tons" defined. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1911-12, p. 132. 

Op. Atty. b. Department has jurisdiction over ferry boats operated on Puget Sound and not operated by any county or under county authority. 

Gen. April 30, 1924. "steamboat companies" a. Interstate ferries not required to have certificate of convenience and necessity. Op. Atty. Gen. July 7, 1927. See also Op. Atty. Gen. Dec. 6, 1928. 

81.04.020 Oath, subpoenas-Compelling attendance of witness.

Any member of the commission may administer oaths, certify to all official acts, and issue subpoenas for the attendance of witnesses and the production of papers, waybills, books, accounts, documents, and testimony in any inquiry, investigation, hearing, or proceeding in any part of the state. 

The superior court of the county in which any such inquiry, investigation, hearing, or proceeding is had, may compel the attendance of witnesses and the production of papers, waybills, books, accounts, documents, and testimony as required by such subpoena. 

The commission, or the authorized representative thereof before whom the testimony is to be given or produced, in case of the refusal of any witness to attend or testify or produce any papers required by the subpoena, shall report to the superior court of the county in which the proceeding is pending by petition setting forth that due notice has been given of the time and place of attendance of said witness, or the production of said papers, and that the witness has been summoned in the manner prescribed in this chapter, and that the fees and mileage of the witness have been paid or tendered to him for his attendance and testimony, and that he has failed and refused to attend or produce the papers required by the subpoena, in the cause or proceedings specified in the notice and subpoena, or has refused to answer questions propounded to him in the course of such proceedings, and shall ask an order of the court, compelling the witness to attend and testify before the commission. 

The court, upon such petition, shall enter an order directing the witness to appear before the court at a time and place to be fixed in such order, and then and there show cause why he has not responded to the subpoena. A copy of the order shall be served upon the witness. If it appears to the court that the subpoena was regularly issued, the court shall enter an order that said witness appear at the time and place fixed in the order, and testify or produce the required papers, and upon failing to obey said order, the witness shall be dealt with as for contempt of court. [1911 c 117 § 75, part; 

RRS § 10413, part.] 

1. Procedure before the Commission is not objectionable for lack of due process because the attendance of witnesses cannot be compelled, since attend-

ance may be compelled by an order of the superior court. State ex rel. Oregon 

R. & N. Co. v. Railroad Comm., 52 Wash. 17 (1909). 

2. Commissioners may issue subpoenas duces tecum and although they cannot be "omnibus" subpoenas, they need not be as detailed in specifying what books and records are sought as would be required of a subpoena issued by a court. Op. Atty. Gen. Nov. 13, 1917. 

3. The Commission may issue an omnius subpoena requiring a utility to produce all account books of every kind and nature showing every account between certain years; every voucher or bill paid; every cancelled check and every paper, etc., or other instrument in writing showing payment or reception of money. The inconvenience to the utility must give way to the paramount rights of the complainant. Op. Atty. Gen. Nov. 23, 1917. 

81.04.030 Number of witnesses may be limited. In all proceedings before the commission, it may limit the number of witnesses testifying upon any subject or proceeding to be inquired of. [1911 c 117 § 75, part; RRS § 10413, part.] 

1. Provision that at hearing Commission may limit number of witnesses is not an unreasonable one. State ex rel. Oregon R. & N. Co. v. Railroad Comm., 52 Wash. 17 (1909). 

81.04.040 Witness fees and mileage. Each witness who appears under subpoena shall receive for his attendance three dollars per day and five cents per mile traveled by the nearest practicable route in going to and returning from the place of hearing. No witness shall be entitled to fees or mileage from the state when summoned at the instance of the public service companies affected. [1911 c 117 § 76, part; RRS § 10414, part.] 

81.04.050 Protection against self-incrimination. The claim by any witness that any testimony sought to be elicited may tend to incriminate him shall not excuse such witness from testifying, but such evidence or testimony shall not be used against such person on the trial of any criminal proceeding, excepting in a prosecution for perjury. [1911 c 117 § 76, part; RRS § 10414, part.] 

81.04.060 Depositions-Service of process. The commission may take the testimony of any witness by deposition, and for that purpose the attendance of witnesses and the production of books, waybills, documents, papers, and accounts may be enforced in the same manner as in the case of hearings before the commission, or any authorized representative. Process issued under the provisions of this chapter shall be served as in civil cases. [1911 c 117 § 76, part; 

RRS § 10414, part.] 

81.04.070 Access to books and records. The commission, or any person authorized by it, may at any time inspect the accounts, books, papers, and documents of any public service company, and may examine under oath any officer, agent, or employee of such public service company in relation thereto, and with reference to the affairs of the company. Any person other than a member of the commission who shall make any such demand shall produce his

authority from the commission to make such inspection.

[1911 c 117 

§ 77; RRS § 10415.] 

1. Commission is under no obligation to make an investigation to determine a reasonable rate. It may rest its decision upon evidence produced by the parties. Whether or not it shall make an investigation on its own motion is a matter within the discretion of the Commission and cannot be controlled by the courts. State ex rel. City of Seattle v. Public Service Comm., 76 Wash. 

492 (1913). 

2. Commission has authority to inspect books and documents of public service company and it is preferable for it to send one of its accountants to secure information needed to correct annual report than to ask Attorney General to institute legal proceedings. Op. Atty. Gen. June 21, 1927. 

81.04.080 Annual reports. Every public service company shall annually furnish to the commission a report in such form as the commission may require, and shall specifically answer all questions concerning which the commission may need information. The annual reports shall show in detail the amount of capital stock issued, the amounts paid therefor and the manner of payment, the dividends paid, the surplus fund, if any, and the number of stockholders, the funded and floating debts and the interest paid thereon, the cost and value of the company's property, franchises, and equipment, the number of employees and the salaries paid each class, the accidents to passengers, employees, and other persons and the cost thereof, the amounts expended for improvements each year, how expended and the character of such improvements, the earnings or receipts from each franchise or business and from all sources, the proportion thereof earned from business moving wholly within the state and the proportion earned from interstate traffic, the nature of the traffic movement showing the percentage of the ton miles each class of commodity bears to the total ton mileage, the operating and other expenses and the proportion of such expense incurred in transacting business wholly within the state, and the proportion incurred in transacting interstate business, such division to be shown according to such rules of division as the commission may prescribe, the balances of profit and loss, and a complete exhibit of the financial operations of the carrier each year, including an annual balance sheet. The report shall also contain such information in relation to rates, charges, or regulations concerning fares, charges, or freight, or agreements, arrangements, or contracts affecting the same, as the commission may require. 

The commission may, for the purpose of enabling it better to carry out the provisions of this title, prescribe the period of time within which all public service companies shall have, as near as may be, a uniform system of accounts, and the manner in which such accounts shall be kept. 

Such detailed report shall contain all the required statistics for the period of twelve months ending on the last day of any particular month prescribed by the commission for any company. Such reports

shall be made out under oath and filed with the commission at its office in Olympia within three months after the close of the designated year for which the report is made, unless additional time is granted by the commission. The commission may require any company to file monthly reports of earnings and expenses, and to file periodical or special, or both periodical and special, reports concerning any matter about which the commission is authorized or required to inquire into or keep itself informed, or which it is required to enforce, such periodical or special reports to be under oath whenever the commission so requires. [1911 c 117 § 78, part; 

RRS § 10416, part.] 

1. Department not justified in fixing rates on the basis of erroneous reports filed by carriers without affording carrier an opportunity to correct report. State ex rel. Clear Lake Lumber Co. v. Kuykendall, 130 Wash. 674 (1924). 

2. Where the pier of a steamship company is used only in its interstate business, and its rates and charges for its wharfage affect only interstate commerce, the state is without power to require the company to file annual reports and pay fees on wharfage charges and collections under this section, notwithstanding its lease of the pier provides that the state reserves the right to regulate the leased area occupied by the pier, since it would be a direct burden on interstate commerce, the field of regulation of which is entirely occupied by the federal government. Luckenbach Steamship Co. v. Denney, 152 Wash. 548 (1929). 

3. A city's right to place a license fee or tax on business or income of a telephone company is not impaired by the state's requiring payment of a gross revenue fee. Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co. v. City of Seattle, 172 Wash. 649 (1933). 

81.04.090 Forms of records to be prescribed. The commission may prescribe the forms of all accounts, records, and memoranda to be kept by public service companies, including the accounts, records, and memoranda of the movement of traffic and the receipt and expenditure of money. The commission shall at all times have access to all accounts, records, and memoranda kept by public service companies, and may employ special agents or examiners, who may administer oaths and, under the order of the commission, examine witnesses and inspect and examine any and all accounts, records, and memoranda kept by such companies. The commission may prescribe the forms of all reports, accounts, records, and memoranda to be furnished and kept by any public service company whose line or lines extend beyond the limits of this state, which are operated partly within and partly without the state, so that they shall show any information required by the commission concerning the traffic movement, receipts, and expenditures appertaining to those parts of the line within the state. [1911 c 117 § 78, part; RRS § 10416, part.] 

81.04.100 Production of out-of-state books and records. The commission may by order, with or without hearing, require the production within this state, at such time and place as it may designate, of any books, accounts, papers, or records kept by any

public service company in any office or place without this state, or at the option of the company, verified copies thereof, so that an examination thereof may be made by the commission or under its direction. [1933 c 165 § 2; 1911 c 117 § 79; RRS § 10421.] 

81.04.110 Complaints-Hearings. Complaint may be made by the commission on its own motion or by any person, corporation, chamber of commerce, board of trade, or any commercial, mercantile, agricultural, or manufacturing society, or any body politic or municipal corporation, by complaint in writing, setting forth any act or thing done or omitted by any public service company claimed to be in violation, of any provision of law or of any order or rule of the commission.

If two or more public service companies, except municipal and other public corporations, are engaged in competition in any locality any one may make complaint against the others that the rates, charges, rules, regulations, or practices of those with respect to which the complainant is in competition, are unreasonable, unremunerative, discriminatory, illegal, unfair, or tending to oppress the complainant, to stifle competition, or to create or encourage the creation of monopoly. Upon such complaint, or upon complaint of the commission on its own motion, the commission may, after notice and hearing, by its order, subject to appeal as in other cases, correct the abuse complained of by establishing such uniform rates, charges, rules, regulations, or practices in lieu of those complained of, to be observed by all of such competing companies in the locality specified as shall be found reasonable, remunerative, nondiscriminatory, legal, and fair, or tending to prevent oppression or monopoly or to encourage competition, and upon the hearing the commission may take into consideration the rates, charges, rules, regulations, and practices of the companies complained of in any other localities in the state. 

All matters upon which complaint is founded may be joined in one hearing, and no motion shall be entertained against a complaint for misjoinder of complaints or grievances, or misjoinder of parties; and in any review by the courts of orders of the commission the same rules shall apply with regard to the joinder of complaints and parties as herein provided: Provided, That all grievances to be inquired into are plainly set forth in the complaint. 

No complaint shall be dismissed because of the absence of direct damage to the complainant.

Upon the filing of a complaint, or upon complaint of the commission on its own motion, the commission shall cause a copy thereof to be served upon the company complained of, which shall be accompanied by a notice fixing the time when and the place where a hearing will be had thereon. The time fixed for hearing shall not

be less than ten days after the date of service of the notice and complaint. Rules of practice and procedure not otherwise provided for in this chapter may be prescribed by the commission. [1913 

c 145 § 1; 1911 c 117 § 80; RRS § 10422.] 

Note: Annotations under this section appear under RCW 80.04.110. 

81.04.120 Hearings-Order-Record. At the time fixed for the hearing, the complainant and the company complained of shall be heard and may introduce such evidence as may be desired. The commission shall issue process to enforce the attendance of all necessary witnesses.

At the conclusion of the hearing the commission shall make findings concerning the subject matter and facts inquired into, and enter its order based thereon. A copy of the order, certified under the seal of the commission, shall be served upon the company complained of, or its attorney, which order shall, of its own force, take effect and become operative twenty days after the service thereof, except as otherwise provided.

When in the judgment of the commission, an order cannot be complied with within twenty days, it may prescribe such additional time as in its judgment is reasonably necessary to comply with the order, and it may, on application and for good cause shown, extend the time for compliance fixed in its order. 

A full and complete record of all proceedings had before the commission or any authorized representative thereof on any formal hearing, and all testimony, shall be taken down by a stenographer appointed by the commission, and the parties shall be entitled to be heard in person or by attorney. 

In an action to review an order of the commission, a transcript of the testimony, together with all exhibits introduced, and of the record and proceedings in the cause, shall constitute the record of the commission. [1911 c 117 § 81; RRS § 10423.] 

Note: Annotations under this section appear under RCW 80.04.120. 

81.04.130 Suspension of tariff changes. When a public service company files with the commission any schedule, classification, rule, or regulation, the effect of which is to change any rate, fare, or charge theretofore charged, the commission may either upon its own motion or upon complaint, upon notice, enter upon a hearing concerning the proposed change and the reasonableness and justness thereof, and pending the hearing and the decision thereon the commission may suspend the operation of the new rate, fare, or charge for a period not exceeding seven months, from the time it would otherwise go into effect, and after a full hearing the commission may make such order in reference thereto as would be provided in a hearing initiated after it had become effective.

4444 At any hearing involving any change in any schedule, classification, rule, or regulation the effect of which is to increase any rate, fare, or charge theretofore charged, the burden of proof to show that the increase is just and reasonable shall be upon the company. When any common carrier subject to the jurisdiction of the commission shall file any tariff, classification, rule, or regulation the effect of which is to decrease any rate, fare, or charge, the burden of proof to show that the decrease is just and reasonable shall be upon the common carrier. [1941 c 162 § 1, last am'ds 1911 

c 117 § 82; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 10424.] 

Note: Annotations under this section appear under RCW 80.04.130. 

81.04.140 Order requiring joint action. Whenever any order of the commission requires joint action by two or more companies, the order shall specify that such action shall be taken at their joint cost, and the companies affected shall have thirty days, or such further time as the commission may prescribe, in which to agree upon the part of cost which each shall bear, and the cost of operation and maintenance in the future, or the proportion of charges or revenue each shall receive from such joint service and the rules to govern future operations. If at the expiration of such time the companies fail to file with the commission a statement that an agreement has been made for the apportionment of such cost, the division of costs of operation and maintenance to be incurred in the future, the proportion of charges or revenue each shall receive from such joint service, and the rules to govern future operations, the commission may, after further hearing, enter a supplemental order fixing the proportion of cost or expense to be borne by each company, and the manner in which they shall be paid and secured. [1911 

c 117 § 83; RRS § 10425.] 

1.

Regulation of rates-Joint rates-Constitutional law-Appeal and re view. State ex rel. Great Northern Railway Co. v. Public Service Comm., 76 

Wash. 625 (1913). 

2. Where terms of physical connection are agreed upon, Public Service 

Commission has no power to provide for cost. 

Commission has no power to fix joint rates in same order in which physical connection is ordered, but if companies fail to fix rates and tolls, Commission must do so. State ex rel. Public Service Comm. v. Skagit River Telephone & 

Telegraph Co., 85 Wash. 29, 89 Wash. 625 (1916). 

81.04.150 Remunerative rates cannot be changed without approval. Whenever, after a hearing, had upon its own motion or upon complaint, the commission finds that any rate, fare, or charge which has been the subject of complaint and inquiry is sufficiently remunerative to the company affected thereby, it may order that such rate, fare, or charge shall not be changed or discontinued, nor shall there be any change in the classification which will change such rate, fare, or charge, without first obtaining the consent of the commission authorizing the change. [1911 c 117 §84; RRS 

§ 10426.]

1. Future traffic cannot be burdened with past deterioration-Rates cannot exceed value of service. Puget Sound Electric Railway v. Railroad 

Comm., 65 Wash. 75 (1911). 

2. Change in gas rates-Reasonableness of rates-Burden of proof-Effect of dismissal. State ex rel. Seattle v. Public Service Comm., 76 Wash. 492 (1913). 

3. Where rates found by a regulatory body to be compensatory_are attacked as being confiscatory, the courts may inquire into the method by which its conclusion is reached. Northern Pacific Railway Company v. De partment of Public Works, 69 L. Ed. 836 (1925). 

4. In fixing reasonable rates for an interurban railroad, there should be allowed an annual renewal fund upon which to draw for necessary replacement; but, where company has failed to do so, the company cannot ask that future traffic be made to bear the deterioration of all the past years. Puget 

Sound Electric Railway v. Railroad Comm., 65 Wash. 75 (1911). 

5. Right of a carrier to fix rates which will earn a fair return on its investment is qualified by the rule that it cannot exact rates higher than the service is reasonably worth or more than the traffic will bear. Id. 

81.04.160 Rules and regulations. The commission may adopt, promulgate, and issue rules and regulations covering the bulletining of trains, showing the time of arrival and departure of all trains, and the probable arrival and departure of delayed trains; the conditions to be contained in and become a part of contracts for transportation of persons and property, and all services concerning the same, or connected therewith; the time that station rooms and offices shall be kept open, rules governing demurrage and reciprocal demurrage, and providing reasonable penalties to expedite the prompt movement of freight and release of cars, the limits of express deliveries in cities and towns, and generally such rules as pertain to the comfort and convenience of the public concerning the subjects treated of in this chapter. Such rules and regulations shall be promulgated and issued by the commission on its own motion, and shall be served on the public service companies affected thereby as orders of the commission are served.

Any public service company affected, and deeming any of such rules and regulations improper, unjust, unreasonable, or contrary to law, may within twenty days from the date of service of such order upon it, file objections thereto with the commission, specifying the particular grounds of such objections. Upon receipt of such objections, the commission shall fix a time and place for hearing the same, and after hearing may make such changes or modifications therein as the evidence may justify. The commission may adopt rules to govern its proceedings, and to regulate the mode and manner of all investigations and hearings. No person desiring to be present at a hearing shall be denied permission. Actions may be instituted to review rules and regulations promulgated under this section as in the case of orders of the commission. [1911 c 117 § 85, part; RRS § 10427, part.] 

1. Act of legislature arbitrarily fixing weights of standards for lumber cars and requiring that weight be deducted from net weight of lumber, is un-

reasonable and unconstitutional. State ex rel. Washington Mill Co. v. G. N. 

Ry. Co., 43 Wash. 658 (1906). 

2. Condition in excursion ticket sold at less than usual fare, limiting liability for loss of baggage to $100 is reasonable and will be enforced by courts. 

Gomm v. O. R. & N. Ry. Co., 52 Wash. 685 (1909). 

3. Contract requiring claim for damages to livestock be made in thirty days valid is a condition precedent to recovery. Henry v. Chicago M. & St. 

P. R. Co., 84 Wash. 633 (1915). 

4. Demurrage rules of interstate commerce-Jurisdiction of state courts. 

Courts have no jurisdiction to pass on reasonableness of rules, regulations, rates, and practices of a common carrier except in review of orders of the Commission whereby these are prescribed; nor have courts jurisdiction to pass on reasonableness of demurrage rule prior to affirmative order of Commission relative thereto. N. P. Ry. Co. v. Carstens Packing Co., 92 Wash. 243; see also: 

119 Wash. 119. 

5. State has power to regulate delivery of cars by railroad company only in respect to intrastate shipments. 

Legislature cannot delegate power to make substantive law but can delegate power to determine facts or state of things upon which statute makes its own operation depend. State ex rel. Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul Ry. Co. v. Public 

Service Comm., 94 Wash. 274 (1917). 

6. Carrier closing station presumed reasonable in absence of rule of Commission. Davenport v. Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul Ry. Co., 103 Wash. 645 (1918). 

7. Requiring transportation of caretakers of livestock sustained.

Camas 

Prairie Railroad Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 125 Wash. 653 (1923). 

8. Findings of the Department that a railroad company may close its station during certain months, will not be set aside as unreasonable, where it appears that the company maintains stations within three or four miles in either direction which adequately serve the community during such months, and increased motor transport service has supplanted part of the railway business, which is small, and the evidence as to the reasonableness of the service was sustained both by the Department and trial court. Dryden Commercial 

Club v. Dept. of Public Works, 142 Wash. 317 (1927). 

9. Court will notice rule of Department quoted in findings, even though not introduced in evidence. N. P. Coast Freight Bureau v. Dept. of Public 

Works, 156 Wash. 137 (1930). 

10. Department has no power to promulgate rule making it unlawful for shippers to accept transportation of commodities at less than the published tariff rates. Op. Atty. Gen. April 24, 1923. 

11. Under certain circumstances, the Department may lawfully authorize a waiver of the collection of demurrage charges. Op. Atty. Gen. March 20, 1929. 

12. Regulations as to checking and handling of baggage. 21 A.L.R. 323. 

13. Power of federal and state governments, respectively, as regards railroad stations. 39 A.L.R. 1372. 

14. Commission has no power to promulgate and enforce rule making shipper subject to penalty who merely sends or receives goods transported at a rate lower than published rate where shipper did nothing to induce granting of such a rate. Op. Atty. Gen. April 24, 1923. 

15. Department has power to determine who shall be allowed to represent parties in hearings and proceedings before it and Department should not allow laymen or non-members of the bar who are not parties in interest to engage in representative functions involving taking of testimony, filing of pleadings, formal hearing and formation of record whereon an appeal may be taken to the courts, except in matters as rate fixing or matters not requiring a high degree of skill.
