81.28.010 Duties as to rates, services and facilities. All charges made for any service rendered or to be rendered in the transportation of persons or property, or in connection therewith, by any common carrier, or by any two or more common carriers, shall be just, fair, reasonable, and sufficient. 

Every common carrier shall construct, furnish, maintain, and provide safe, adequate, and sufficient service facilities, trackage, sidings, railroad connections, industrial and commercial spurs, and equipment to enable it to promptly, expeditiously, safely, and properly receive, transport, and deliver all persons or property offered to or received by it for transportation, and to promote the safety, health, comfort, and convenience of its patrons, employees, and the public. 

All rules and regulations issued by any common carrier affecting or pertaining to the transportation of persons or property shall be just and reasonable. [1911 c 117 § 9; RRS § 10345.] 

1. A street "railway" is not, in either the popular or legislative sense, a "railroad." Front Street Cable Ry. Co. v. Johnson, 2 Wash. 112 (1891). 

2. Corporation owning a street railway line in hands of construction company completing it, is a common carrier when it permits construction company

to run cars and collect fares from passengers. Cogswell v. West Street & No. 

End Elec. Ry. Co., 5 Wash. 46 (1892). 

3. Courts will take judicial notice that railroad companies are common carriers. Boyle v. Great Northern R. Co., 13 Wash. 383 (1896). 

4. Street railway company accepting franchise from state cannot discontinue passenger service without consent from state. State ex rel. Grinsfelder v. Spokane St. Ry. Co., 19 Wash. 518 (1898). 

5. Even if the expressed purpose of a forwarding company's business were material, its designation that it was a "forwarder" and "distributor" would be sufficient to estop it from claiming that it was a mere forwarder and not a common carrier. Lee v. Fidelity Storage & Transfer Co., 51 Wash. 208 (1908). 

6. Orders of the Commission respecting railroad service and facilities will not be set aside on appeal as unreasonable, unless they clearly so appear, the presumptions being that they are reasonable. State ex rel. Great Northern 

Ry. v. R. R. Comm., 60 Wash. 218 (1910). 

7. Order of Railroad Commission requiring company to put on daily mixed train on a line 14 miles long held unreasonable. State ex rel. Northern 

Pacific Ry. v. R. R. Comm., 62 Wash. 193 (1911). 

8. Carrier entitled to a return but rates must not be higher than service is worth nor more than traffic will bear. Seven per cent is a reasonable profit on investment of interurban railroad. Puget Sound Electric R. Co. v. R. R. 

Comm., 65 Wash. 75 (1911). 

9. Complaint filed by Commission before itself alleging that depot facilities of carrier at various stations are inadequate, is sufficient and confers jurisdiction to enter an order requiring construction of depots within a certain time. State ex rel. R. R. Comm. v. O. R. & N. Co., 68 Wash. 160 (1912). 

10. A transfer and storage company engaged in warehousing goods and forwarding them for a compensation in carload lots held a common carrier so as to make it liable as such for the destruction of the goods while in its warehouse. Kettenhofen v. Globe Transfer & Storage Co., 70 Wash. 645; 42 L.R.A. (N.S.) 902 (1912). 

11. Order of Commission requiring track connections must be upon necessity shown. O. R. & N. Co. v. Fairchild, 224 U. S. 510, 56 L. Ed. 863 (1912). 

12. Public Service Commission has exclusive jurisdiction to regulate and control street railways with reference to overcrowding of cars, operating in accordance with schedule, etc., and power of city thereover is revoked; and it is not necessary that Commission should have acted in order to divest city of right since cities may invoke the aid of the Commission. Seattle Electric 

Co. v. City of Seattle, 78 Wash. 203 (1914). 

13. Theory of public service law opposed to idea that public will be better served by two closely parallel lines where one will amply suffice. Day v. Tacoma Railway & Power Co., 80 Wash. 161 (1914). 

14. Interstate express company may renounce intrastate business. State v. Northern Express Co., 80 Wash. 309 (1914). 

15. Reasonableness and lawfulness of an order of Commission respecting train service to be rendered a town is reviewable and presumption that order is reasonable and lawful must be overthrown. 

Although railroad operates only interstate trains, Commission may require it to render adequate local service for the accommodation of the traveling public. 

Finding that different service than that rendered is necessary is supported by certain evidence and cannot be overthrown by showing that such change will require an extra man and will delay the train from 6 to 9 minutes. State ex rel. G. N. Ry. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 81 Wash. 275 (1914). 

16. Regulation of common carriers is inherent in the state-may be exercised through legislature or through medium of Commission. 

Power assumed by Commission in establishing its own power to pass on question of what penalty should be paid by carrier for failure to furnish cars, is unconstitutional as an assumption of both legislative and judicial authority by an administrative body. State ex rel. Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul Ry. 

Co. v. Public Service Comm., 94 Wash. 274 (1917).

IS 17.

Showing of public necessity necessary to sustain order for additional service. Puget Sound Traction, L. & P. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 100 Wash. 329 (1918). 

18. A "common carrier" is one who transports persons or things from place to place for hire or reward and holds himself out to the world as ready and willing to serve the public indifferently, but if the undertaking be a single transaction, not a part of the general business or occupation engaged in, service is that of a private and not a common carrier. Cushing v. White, 101 

Wash. 172; L.R.A. 1918F, 463 (1918). 

19. The disputed question of whether a corporation is a common carrier held one of fact to be determined by the courts on evidence. State v. Public 

Service Comm., 117 Wash. 453 (1921). 

20. Whether a corporation is a common carrier is not concluded by the fact that its charter gives it the power of such a carrier but is to be determined by what it does. Id. 

21. Facts relative to purpose of organization of a railway and lumber company, its road as constructed, its equipment, and the business carried on by it, held to show that it never advanced beyond the status of a private carrier, and so was not obligated to receive as a common carrier logs for transportation. Id. 

22. In a proceeding involving question of whether a lumber company operating a railroad was as to such railroad a common carrier, insufficiency of evidence as to the exact sums collected from outside persons held immaterial, where sum so collected was inconsequential as compared with the freight carried by the railroad for the company itself. Id. 

23. The law does not contemplate identical charges to every shipper. It requires a tariff which is general and public, and that shipments be made according thereto, and not according to exceptions. Id. 

24. Demurrage does not run on cars which cannot be placed on delivery tracks on account of flood, notwithstanding average agreement. O. W. R. R. & N. Co. v. McGoldrick Lumber Co., 119 Wash. 119 (1922). 

25. Order of Department regulating intrastate traffic, even though reasonable, is void if it conflicts with an order of the Interstate Commerce Commission.

Order requiring free transportation for caretaker of livestock in intrastate shipments conflicts with I.C.C. rule, and is void. Camas Prairie R. Co. v.

Dept. Public Works, 125 Wash. 653 (1923). 

26. The power of the Department to fix rates is legislative and not judicial.

Pac. Coast Elev. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 130 Wash. 620 (1924). 

27. Intrastate log rates based on operating cost obtained by taking weighted average operating cost per 1,000 gross tons of all revenue freight carried on system and applying figure to log movement, are arbitrary and on a fundamentally wrong basis. Northern Pacific Railway Co. v. Dept. 

of Public Works, 268 U. S. 39, 69 L. Ed. 836 (1925). 

28. Public service law deprives city of Seattle of authority to pass ordinance requiring street railway companies to sell commutation tickets on cars. 

Seattle Electric Co. v. City of Seattle, 206 Fed. 955. 

29. A "common carrier” is one who undertakes to transport for hire from one place to another the goods of such as wish to employ him. Bay v. Merrill & Ring Lumber Co., 211 Fed. 717. Affirmed: 220 Fed. 295, 136 C.C.A. 277. 

30. Concern carrying only its own products is not a "common carrier." 

Campbell River Mills Co. v. Chicago, Milwaukee, St. Paul & P. R. Co., 42 F. (2d) 775. Affirmed: 53 F. (2d) 69. Certiorari denied 285 U. S. 536, 76 L. Ed. 930. 

31. Test of "public carrier" is whether he holds himself out as being ready and willing for hire to carry particular classes of goods for all who may desire transportation between places which he professes to serve. State v. Wash. 

Tug and Barge Co., 140 Wash. 613 (1926). 

32. Whether carrier is a "public carrier" must be determined by business actually carried on and not by secret intention or mental reservation of owners or operators. Id.

33. Order of the Commission allowing station at Dryden to be closed was not unreasonable, where there are other stations within five miles of Dryden on either side and the business of the station was seasonal. 

Ordinarily a railroad company will maintain a station at any point where such action is profitable. Dryden Com. Club v. Dept. of Public Works, 142 

Wash. 317 (1927). 

34. What constitutes common carrier is question of law, but whether carrier comes within definition is a question of fact. Big Bend Auto Freight v. Ogers, 148 Wash. 521 (1928). 

35. Intrastate rates apply on logs cut in Canada, transported to Columbia 

Spur in Washington by private logging railroad and transported by defendant from Columbia Spur to Bellingham; logs moved by owner on own private railroad are not in transit so as to make entire shipment interstate; fact that cars belonged to defendant did not make movement from forest to spur a shipment, as no transportation charges were paid, no bill of lading issued and carrier was not in control. Cars were subject to demurrage. State ex rel. 

Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul v. Dept. of Public Works, 149 Wash. 129 (1928). 

36. "Common carrier" is one whose occupation is transportation of persons or things for hire and who holds himself out as ready to serve public indifferently. Independent Truck Co. v. Wright, 151 Wash. 372 (1929). 

37. Ferry may issue commutation rates on trucks. Such a rate does not violate constitutional provision against discrimination. Puget Sound Nav. 

Co. v. Department, 156 Wash 489 (1930). 

38. While the question of what constitutes a common carrier is a question of law, the status of a carrier as such must be determined from his method of operation. Miles v. Enumclaw Co-op. Creamery Corporation, 12 Wn. (2d) 377 (1942). 

39. One engaged in casual hauling is not a "common carrier." Larson v. 

Aetna Life Insurance Co., 19 Wn. (2d) 601 (1943). 

40. Generally, where a carrier undertakes to carry only for the particular occasion, he is not a "common carrier," and if the carrier be employed to carry for one or a definite number of persons by way of special undertaking, he is only a "private carrier," and to constitute one a common carrier, he must make that a regular and constant business or must for the time hold himself ready to carry for all, indifferently, who choose to employ him. Id. 

41. Under the original railroad commission act the Commission had the right to declare any existing rate on any railroad, regardless of its length, unreasonable, and establish a just and reasonable rate. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1907-8, p. 29. 

42. If any railroad company holds itself out as carrier of petroleum in bulk and shipping demand justifies it, the Commission may require the company to furnish tank cars to shippers of that commodity. Op. Atty. Gen. Nov. 10, 1910. 

43. The Seattle, Renton & So. R. R. Co. was a "street railroad" within the meaning of this section and it may issue free or reduced transportation to mail carriers. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1911-12, p. 219. 

44. The term "railroad" as used in this section, includes every railroad other than a street railroad, by whatsoever power operated, and the same general rules are to apply to all railroads and railroad equipment whether operated by steam power or some other power. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1911-12, p. 241. 

45. The term "railroad" does not include a tramway about one mile long used for transporting grain as an incident to a warehouse. Ops. Atty. Gen. 

1911-12, p. 243. 

46. Commission has no power to require a railway company at its own expense, to construct a warehouse on its right of way for use by prospective shippers in sorting and assembling fruit prior to its delivery to the railway company for transportation. Op. Atty. Gen. Aug. 22, 1912. 

47. Commission has no power to compel the construction and operation of street railway lines within city not authorized by franchise granted by municipal authorities and accepted by the traction company. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1911-12, p. 327.

48. Commission has no power to compel a railroad company to permit a portion of its right of way to be used for construction and operation of a grain elevator. Op. Atty Gen. April 23, 1914. 

49. Carrier may insist upon furnishing ice for refrigeration of perishable fruits shipped over its lines, provided the practices and charges are just and reasonable. Op. Atty. Gen. Sept. 3, 1914. 

50. Railway cannot refuse use of spur track to shipper where no other accessible facilities are furnished. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1913-14, p. 91. 

51. Commission has no power to affirmatively authorize abandonment of service. Op. Atty. Gen. April 19, 1915. 

52. Commission has exclusive jurisdiction relative to ventilation, fumigation, and heating of street cars. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1915-16, p. 294. 

53. Matters relating to rates and service (ventilation, fumigation, heating of street railway cars) lie exclusively within the jurisdiction of the Commission but cities may, under exercise of their police powers, enforce ordinances regarding keeping the public peace. Op. Atty. Gen. May 3, 1916. 

54. Branch line of railway completed but not turned over to operating department is no common carrier and the Commission has no authority to investigate accidents occurring thereon. Op. Atty. Gen. Feb. 17, 1917. 

55. Commission has no jurisdiction to compel railroad to cut weeds along its right of way. Op. Atty. Gen. Aug. 21, 1920. 

56. Duty of carrier and shipper in connection with cleaning and cooperage of cars for grain carriage. Op. Atty. Gen. Aug. 22, 1927. 

57. Department has no jurisdiction over a proceeding to require unificatioin of terminals in Tacoma. Op. Atty. Gen. Dec. 12, 1928. 

58. If railroad is willing and action would not result in undue preference, railroad may waive collection of demurrage charges where logging cars were immobilized by accidental burning of bridges and Commission is willing to authorize waiver. Op. Atty. Gen. March 20, 1929. 

59. In absence of order requiring certain train schedules, discontinuance of those schedules by railroad violates no order of Department and Department has no authority to prohibit discontinuance. Department can order reinstatement of service after hearing. Op. Atty. Gen. Feb. 20, 1940. 

60. By virtue of its general authority over common carriers, Department may require railroads to file time schedule with Department; but Department has no authority to suspend proposed change in time schedule. Op. Atty. Gen. 

Feb. 20, 1940. 

61. Department has power to set minimum rates under this section after hearing either on complaint or on suspension on its own motion. Op. Atty. Gen. 

April 6, 1941. 

62. Street car fares fixed by Public Service Commission. 5 A.L.R. 36; 39 

A.L.R. 1517. 

63. Judicial release of carrier from contract rates which have become inadequate. 6 A.L.R. 1659; 10 A.L.R. 1335. 

64. Amortization of cost of road, right to rates which will permit. 9 A.L.R. 1232. 

65. Patron's right to question reasonableness of rate established by public authority. 12 A.L.R. 404. 

66. Consideration of body of rates in determining reasonableness of carrier's rates for a particular commodity. 15 A.L.R. 185. 

67. Special services or facilities afforded by shipper as a factor in fixing carrier's rates. 25 A.L.R. 191. 

68. Street easements as a factor in fixing a rate base for a street railway company. 49 A.L.R. 1477. 

69. Pullman and sleeping car companies. 11 A.L.R. 996. Bus or stage lines. 

42 A.L.R. 853. Switching company. 54 A.L.R. 620. Who is "common carrier" under terms of insurance policy providing for indemnity for injury or death while in conveyance operated by common carrier. 149 A.L.R. 1293.

81.28.020 Duty of carriers and shippers to expedite traffic. Every common carrier shall under reasonable rules and regulations promptly and expeditiously receive, transport, and deliver all persons or property offered to or received by it for transportation. All persons receiving cars for loading shall promptly and expeditiously load the same, and all persons receiving property shall promptly and expeditiously receive and remove the same from the cars and freight rooms. [1911 c 117 § 10; RRS § 10346.] 

1. Department has no jurisdiction to determine whether or not certain private individuals are entitled to compensatory damages because of time lost by workmen of a steamship company caused by error in navigation. Op. Atty. 

Gen. Oct. 9, 1927. 

81.28.030 Routing of freight-Connecting companies-Damages.

All transportation companies doing business wholly or in part within this state shall, upon receipt of any article of freight, promptly forward it to its marked destination by the route directed by the shipper, or if no directions are given by the shipper, then to any connecting company whose line en route reaches nearest to the point to which the freight is marked. 

Any transportation company failing to comply with the provisions of this section shall be liable for any damages that may be sustained either by the shipper or consignee, from any cause, upon proof that the damages resulted from such failure. Suit for damages may be instituted either at the place of shipping or destination, either by the shipper or consignee, before any court of competent jurisdic tion. [(i) 1890 p 291 § 1; RRS § 10491. (ii) 1890 p 291 § 2; RRS § 10492. 

(iii) 1890 p 291 § 3; RRS § 10493.] 

1. Cited: Pacific Car & Foundry Co. v. Northern Pacific Ry. Co., 123 

Wash. 98 (1923). 

2. Department has no jurisdiction to determine whether or not certain private individuals are entitled to compensatory damages because of time lost by workmen of a steamship company, caused by error in navigation. Op. 

Atty. Gen. Oct. 9, 1927. 

81.28.040 Tariff schedules to be filed. Every common carrier shall file with the commission, and shall print and keep open to public inspection, schedules showing the rates, fares, charges, and classifications for the transportation of persons and property within the state between each point upon its route and all other points thereon; and between each point upon its route and all points upon every route leased, operated, or controlled by it; and between each point on its route or upon any route leased, operated, or controlled by it and all points upon the route of any other common carrier, whenever a through route and joint rate has been established or ordered between any two such points. If no joint rate over a through route has been established, the several carriers in such through route shall file, print, and keep open to the public inspection, the separately established rates, fares, charges, and classifications, applied to the through

transportation. The schedules shall plainly state the places between which property and persons will be carried, and shall also contain classifications of passengers or property in force, and shall also state separately all terminal, storage, icing, and all other charges which the commission may require to be stated, all privileges or facilities allowed, and any rules and regulations which may affect or determine any part, or the aggregate of, the rates, fares, and charges, or the value of the service rendered to the passenger, shipper, or consignee. The schedules shall be plainly printed in large type, and a copy shall be kept by the carrier readily accessible to and for inspection by the public in every station or office where passengers or property are received for transportation, when the station or office is in charge of an agent, and in every station or office of the carrier where passenger tickets for transportation or tickets covering sleeping or parlor cars or other train accommodation are sold or bills of lading or receipts for property are issued. The schedules shall be immediately produced by the carrier for inspection upon the demand of any person. A notice printed in bold type and stating that the schedules are on file with the agent and open to inspection by any person and that the agent will assist any person to determine therefrom any transportation rates or fares, or rules and regulations, shall be kept posted by the carrier in two public and conspicuous places in every such station or office. The form of schedules shall be prescribed by the commission and shall conform in the case of railroad companies as nearly as may be to the form of schedules required by the Interstate Commerce Commission under the act of congress entitled "An Act to regulate commerce," approved February 4, 1887, and acts amendatory thereof and supplementary thereto. 

The commission may, from time to time, determine and prescribe by order such changes in the form of schedules as may be found expedient, and modify the requirements of this section in respect to publishing, posting and filing of schedules either in particular instances or by general rule or order applicable to special or peculiar circumstances. 

The commission may suspend the operation of this section in whole or in part as applied to vessels engaged in jobbing business not operating on regular routes. [1911 c 117 § 14; RRS § 10350.] 

1. Where two freight rates are provided, one in contemplation of the ordinary carrier's liability, and the other a lesser rate by reason of a limitation of that liability, and goods are delivered to it for shipment in the ordinary manner without any agreement relative to any limitation of liability or reduction in freight charges, the carrier assumes the ordinary liability of a carrier, and the law will imply that the usual rate is the one which was intended. 

Harris v. Great Northern Railway Co., 48 Wash. 437 (1908). 

2. An order of the Commission changing commodity freight rates is void where no complaint has been made against the existing rates and no hearing held thereon. State v. Railroad Comm., 52 Wash. 440 (1909). 

3. Tariff properly noticed and filed automatically goes into effect at the expiration of the thirty-day period unless suspended or adjudged unreason-

able by Commission. State ex rel. Seattle v. Public Service Comm., 76 Wash.

492 (1913). 

4. Order not specifying time in which it shall become effective takes effect 

20 days after service thereof. State ex rel. G. N. Ry. Co. v. Public Service 

Comm., 80 Wash. 218 (1914). 

5. The purpose of the statute providing that a carrier must file its schedule showing rates, charges, and classification with the Commission is to so bind the carrier as to prevent its making discriminatory rates, and there is nothing in the act making such filing a notice to all the world or giving thereto any effect beyond the prevention of discrimination. United Artists Corp. v. Puget 

Sound Elec. Ry., 122 Wash. 537 (1922). 

6. One who holds himself out to the world through a regularly filed tariff as willing to perform all the manifold forms of transportation which the public may reasonably be expected to require is devoting his property to public use. 

State ex rel. Stimson Timber Co. v. Kuykendall, 137 Wash. 602; 55 A.L.R. 954 (1926). 

7. A special contract between a ferry and stage companies constituting connecting carriers, to transport the stage companies' stages at a less rate than the tariff fixed therefor by the tariff schedules of the Department, is void and unenforcible, as in violation of the statutes, requiring every common carrier to file a schedule of its rates, and prohibiting less or greater charges than the schedules or any special contract for rebates or discrimination as to any person.

Auto stage lines and ferry line, whether or not constituting connecting public carriers as to through passenger fares, are regulated by any tariff of through fares duly apportioned between them by the public service department, regardless of whether the stages are transported on the ferry. Wolverton Auto Bus Co. v. Robinson, 151 Wash. 67 (1929). 

8. A commutation rate for auto stages operated in regular schedule is not a special rate for the benefit of the two particular shippers involved, but is applicable to all who hereafter operate such service. Puget Sound Nav. Co. v. 

Dept. of Public Works, 157 Wash. 557 (1930). 

9. Ambiguity in a railroad tariff is primarily a question for the Department, doubt to be resolved in favor of the shipper; and where a tariff for logs fails to define "board measure," it cannot be said that the Department acted arbitrarily in adopting a rule of measurement recognized commercially and accepted by 80 to 85 per cent of the dealers in logs. Northern Pac. R. Co. v. 

Sauk River Lumber Co., 160 Wash. 691 (1931). 

10. Necessity of filing rates for services which carrier is not bound to render as a common carrier. 19 A.L.R. 982. 

11. Jurisdiction of state court of action in relation to interstate shipment involving failure to post schedule of rates. 64 A.L.R. 351. 

12. This section revokes the power of cities of second and third class to regulate speed of trains within their limits where the Department has prescribed limitations. Where Department has not acted, ordinances in force before this act was passed may not be changed without approval of Commission. Ordinances passed subsequent to this section are of no effect. Op. Atty. 

Gen. June 12, 1946. 

13. Steamboat companies required to file with Commission schedule of rates for berths and staterooms. Op. Atty. Gen. July 12, 1913. 

81.28.050 Tariff changes-Statutory notice-Exception. Unless the commission otherwise orders, no change shall be made in any classification, rate, fare, charge, rule, or regulation filed and published by a common carrier, except after thirty days' notice to the commission and to the public published as aforesaid, which shall plainly state the changes proposed to be made in the schedule then in force, and the time when the changed rate, classification, fare, or charge will go into effect; and all proposed changes shall be shown

by printing, filing and publishing new schedules or shall be plainly indicated upon the schedules in force at the time and kept open to public inspection. The commission, for good cause shown, may by order allow changes in rates without requiring the thirty days' notice and the publication. When any change is made in any rate, fare, charge, classification, rule, or regulation, the effect of which is to increase any rate, fare, or charge then existing, attention shall be directed to the increase by some character on the copy filed with the commission immediately preceding or following the item in the schedule; such character to be designated by the commission. [1911 

c 117 § 15; RRS § 10351.] 

1. Department is justified in ordering cancelling of a tariff that actually increased rates when increases were not indicated by symbol. 

Court may take notice of rule of Department which is quoted in findings, even though not introduced in evidence. N. P. Coast Freight Bureau v. Department, 156 Wash. 137 (1930). 

2. Cited in 80 Wash. 219; 157 Wash. 561. 

3. A tariff that is under suspension at the time it is ordered cancelled by the Department does not go into effect during review proceedings. N. P. Coast 

Freight Bureau v. Dept. of Public Works, 156 Wash. 137 (1930). 

81.28.060 Joint rates, contracts, etc. The names of the several carriers which are parties to any joint tariff shall be specified therein, and each of the parties thereto, other than the one filing it, shall file with the commission such evidence of concurrence therein as may be required by the commission; and where evidence of concurrence is filed, it shall not be necessary for the carriers filing it also to file copies of the joint tariffs. 

Every common carrier shall file with the commission copies of every contract, agreement, or arrangement with any other common carrier relating in any way to the transportation of persons or property. [1911 c 117 § 16; RRS § 10352.] 

81.28.070 Interstate tariffs. Every common carrier shall print and file with the commission schedules showing the rates, fare, charges, and classifications for the transportation of persons and property between all points within the state and all points without the state upon its route, and between each point within the state and all points without the state upon every route leased, operated, or controlled by it, and between each point upon its route within the state and all points without the state upon the route of any common carrier, whenever a through route and joint rate has been established between any two such points. If no joint rate over a through route has been established, the carrier operating within this state shall print and file with the commission the separately established rates, fares, charges, and classifications applied to the through transportation. The schedules shall plainly state the places between which property and persons will be carried, and shall also contain the classification of passengers or property in force, and shall also

state separately all terminal, storage, icing, or other charges which the commission may require to be stated, all privileges allowed, and any rules or regulations which may affect or determine any part or the aggregate of the rates, fares, and charges or the value of the service rendered to the passenger, shipper, or consignee. [1911 c 117 

§ 17; RRS § 10353.] 

1. Department may require interstate contract carrier to file its contracts with Department but in cooperation with the I.C.C. should keep such contracts confidential. Op. Atty. Gen. Nov. 29, 1941. 

81.28.080 Published rates to be charged-Exceptions. No common carrier shall charge, demand, collect, or receive a greater or less or different compensation for transportation of persons or property, or for any service in connection therewith, than the rates, fares, and charges applicable to such transportation as specified in its schedules filed and in effect at the time; nor shall any such carrier refund or remit in any manner or by any device any portion of the rates, fares, or charges so specified excepting upon order of the commission as provided in this title, or extend to any person any privileges or facilities in the transportation of passengers or property except such as are regularly and uniformly extended to all persons under like circumstances.

No common carrier shall, directly or indirectly, issue or give any free ticket, free pass or free or reduced transportation for passengers between points within this state, except to its employees and their families, its surgeons and physicians and their families, its officers, agents and attorneys at law; to ministers of religion, traveling secretaries of railroad Young Men's Christian Associations, inmates of hospitals, charitable, and eleemosynary institutions and persons exclusively engaged in charitable and eleemosynary work; to indigent, destitute, and homeless persons and to such persons when transported by charitable societies or hospitals, and the necessary agents employed in such transportation; to inmates of the national homes or state homes for disabled volunteer soldiers and of soldiers' and sailors' homes, including those about to enter and those returning home after discharge; to necessary caretakers of livestock, poultry, milk and fruit; to employees of sleeping car companies, express companies, and to linemen of telegraph and telephone companies; to railway mail service employees, post office inspectors, customs inspectors, and immigration inspectors; to newsboys on trains; to baggage agents; to witnesses attending any legal investigation in which the carrier is interested; to persons injured in accidents or wrecks and physicians and nurses attending such persons; to the national guard of Washington when on official duty; and to students going to and returning from the state institutions of learning. [1929 

c 96 § 1, part; 1911 c 117 § 18; RRS § 10354, part.] 

1. Carrier may refuse to build spur over private land to warehouse, when it has not done so for others. Northwestern Warehouse Co. v. O. R. & N. Co., 32 Wash. 218 (1903).

2. The constitutional provision requiring the legislature to establish reasonable maximum transportation rates does not prevent the delegation of such power to a commission, and this act providing a railroad commission impliedly repeals former maximum rate laws of the state, upon the taking effect of conflicting rates established by the Commission. State ex rel. Great Northern 

R. Co. v. Railroad Comm., 52 Wash. 33 (1909). 

3. The fact that a railway company's published tariff of rates is greater than the amount agreed upon between the railway and a logging company for the carriage of its logs is not conclusive evidence that the latter is discriminatory, where an independent consideration passed between the parties in addition to the rates charged. Sultan R. & Timber Co. v. Great Northern 

R. Co., 58 Wash. 604 (1910). 

4. Federal act prohibiting carrier from charging any greater or less fee for transportation than the rates specified in published tariffs renders inoperative a prior contract whereby railroad agreed, in return for land conveyed to it, to carry grantor free for the rest of life, and where railroad has substantially peformed contract they are not liable in damages for failure to fully perform. Cowley v. N. P. Ry. Co., 68 Wash. 558 (1912). 

5. Fares-Rates for school children in franchise-Construction. Does not apply to students of universities or colleges. State ex rel. Seattle v. Seattle 

Elec. Co., 71 Wash. 213 (1912). 

6. Unreasonable discrimination for carrier to charge considerably less rates on class commodities from Seattle than from Tacoma to Spokane. Public 

Service Comm. ex rel. Transportation Bureau of Tacoma Com. Club v. Nor. 

Pac. Ry. Co., 77 Wash. 635 (1914). 

7. Employee riding on free pass, given as part consideration for services, is a passenger for hire, and company is liable for negligent injury. 

But rights as a passenger for hire may be waived where written application and pass both stipulate that it is a pure gratuity. Hageman v. P. S. Elec. Ry. 

Co., 80 Wash. 442 (1914). 

8. Where shipper orders double deck freight cars in which to ship sheep and carrier furnishes single deckers for which a higher tariff rate is chargeable, it cannot exact higher rate from shipper and set up violation of tariff rate filed with Interstate Commerce Commission as a defense, but must refund difference. So. Pac. Co. v. Frye & Bruhn, 82 Wash. 9 (1914). 

9. This act not having expressly conferred upon it authority to fix rates to be charged by booming and driving companies for booming and driving logs, the Commission has no jurisdiction over such companies in the matter of fixing tolls. Wishkah Boom Co. v. Greenwood Timber Co., 88 Wash. 568 (1915). 

10. A carrier in interstate commerce cannot enter into a contract of transportation for which there is no express authority in its filed tariffs. Pac. Fruit & Prod. Co. v. N. P. R. Co., 109 Wash. 481 (1920). 

11. This section is aimed at unjust discrimination, and does not preclude special contracts for the carriage of particular property. Cary-Davis Towing 

Co. v. Spradley, 115 Wash. 93 (1921). 

12. The status of a carrier as a common carrier is a question of fact to be determined by the courts upon the evidence presented in the record, irrespective of the finding of the Commission. State ex rel. Silver Lake R. & L. Co. v. 

Public Service Comm., 117 Wash. 453 (1921). 

13. Where a carrier has agreed that a preferential rate on carriage of freight shall apply to other shippers of the same commodity between points named, its published tariff is, in effect, replaced by a lower one, and a shipper who has been compelled to pay the published rate is damaged to the extent of the difference between the two rates. Tacoma Eastern R. R. Co. v. Public 

Service Comm., 117 Wash. 609 (1921). 

14. Rates are required to be filed to prevent discrimination; shipper is not bound by constructive knowledge. United Artists Corp. v. Puget Sound Elec. 

Ry. Co., 122 Wash. 537 (1922). 

15. Intrastate log rates, based on operating cost obtained by taking weighted average operating cost per 1,000 gross tons of all revenue freight carried on system and applying figure to log movement, are arbitrary and on a

fundamentally wrong basis. N. P. Ry. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 125 Wash. 

584 (1923). Reversed: 268 U. S. 39, 69 L. Ed. 836 (1925). 

16. Department is not justified in fixing rates on the basis of erroneous reports filed by carriers without affording carrier an opportunity to correct reports. State ex rel. Clear Lake Lbr. Co. v. Kuykendall, 130 Wash. 674 (1924). 

17. Limitation of liability to 50¢ a pound published in tariff and carried on express receipt does not limit liability for damages for delay. Kirby v. Am. 

Ry. Exp. Co., 137 Wash. 241 (1926). 

18. The acceptance of ferry charges less than the lawful and reasonable tariff rates filed with the Department is not an accord and satisfaction or a defense to an action to collect the unpaid balance; since the carrier and its patron cannot evade the collection and payment of the lawful rates by agreement between themselves, in view of this section, providing that no common carrier shall collect or receive a greater or less or different compensation for transportation of property or persons than the rates and fares specified in its schedules. Robinson v. Wolverton Auto Bus Co., 163 Wash. 160 (1931). 

19. A contract between a common carrier and a newspaper whereby it is agreed that the carrier shall furnish transportation in return for advertising space is illegal and contravenes this section. Op. Atty. Gen. July 18, 1911. 

20. This section does not authorize free or reduced rates to students going to or coming from state fairs but does authorize reduced rate for exhibits sent. 

Op. Atty. Gen. Feb. 9, 1916. 

21. Failure of carrier to furnish cars for loading at time ordered by shipper does not prevent carrier from charging published rates. Op. Atty. Gen. May 8, 1917. 

22. After release of railroad from federal control, state rates in effect at time of seizure are revived and effective without further act of the Commission. 

Op. Atty. Gen. July 22, 1918. 

23. Carriers must file tariffs showing rates used in hauling for governmental agencies so that discrimination can be avoided. Also where shipper sells to governmental agency and ships goods f. o. b. destination-i.e., paying freight and including it in purchase price, carrier cannot charge reduced rate to such shipper. Op. Atty. Gen. Oct. 20, 1939. 

24. While it may be true that under this section a common carrier may transport property for the state at a rate less than it charges the general public, it does not follow that such transportation is not subject to regulation for other purposes. Op. Atty. Gen. May 25, 1945. 

81.28.090 Further exception-Calamitous visitations. A common carrier may carry passengers free with the object of providing relief in cases of general epidemic, pestilence, or other calamitous visita tion. [1929 c 96 § 1, part; 1911 c 117 § 18; RRS § 10354, part.] 

81.28.100 Commutation or excursion tickets. A common carrier may issue mileage, commutation tickets, or excursion passenger tickets. [1929 c 96 § 1, part; 1911 c 117 § 18; RRS § 10354, part.] 

1. The constitutional and statutory provisions against discrimination by carriers, with the proviso that nothing therein shall prevent the issuance of commutation tickets, give the carrier the right to issue temporary commutation tickets at its discretion. State ex rel. Seattle v. Public Service Comm., 103 Wash. 72 (1918). 

81.28.110 Transportation of city employees. A street railroad may issue free or reduced transportation to mail carriers, members of the police or fire departments, and city officers and employees when engaged in the performance of their duties as such. [1929 

c 96 § 1, part; 1911 c 117 § 18; RRS § 10354, part.]

1. Franchise condition to carry policemen free is proprietary right of city, and not affected by section. State ex rel. Seattle v. Seattle & Rainier Valley 

R. Co., 113 Wash. 684 (1921). 

2. Transportation companies are not required to offer free or reduced transportation to mail carriers but may do so if they wish. Op. Atty. Gen. Feb. 

26, 1912. 

3. The term "policemen" as used in this section does not include sheriffs nor their deputies and a common carrier is prohibited from issuing free or reduced transportation to them. Op. Atty. Gen. March 1, 1912. 

81.28.120 Interchange of passes by carriers. Passes for their officers, attorneys, agents, employees, and their families may be interchanged by railroad, steamboat, express, and sleeping car companies with other like companies. [1929 c 96 § 1, part; 1911 c 117 § 18;

RRS § 10354, part.] 

1. Railway companies may interchange passes with steamboat companies, etc. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1913-14, p. 148. 

81.28.130 Exchange of passes or franks by carriers and communications companies. Passes or franks for their officers, attorneys, agents, employees, and their families may be exchanged by telegraph and telephone companies with railroad, express, and sleeping car companies. [1929 c 96 § 1, part; 1911 c 117 § 18; RRS § 10354, part.] 

81.28.140 Free passage for "seeing eye" dogs. Any sightless person who is a passenger for hire on any common carrier or other conveyance for the transportation of persons for hire, may have with him as his "seeing eye" a dog specially trained for that purpose, without being required to pay any additional charge therefor and the transportation thereof shall be included in such passenger's regular standard fare. [1937 c 26 § 1; RRS § 10354-1.] 

81.28.150 Special exceptions on carriage of property, government freight, etc. Common carriers may carry, store or handle, free or at reduced rates, property for the United States, state, county, or municipal governments; or for charitable purposes; or to or from fairs for exhibition thereat; and may carry, store, or handle, free or at reduced rates, the household goods and personal effects of their employees and those entering or leaving their service and those killed or dying while in their service. [1929 c 96 § 1, part; 1911 c 117 

§ 18; RRS § 10354, part.] 

81.28.160 Contracts for exchange of service by railroad and communications companies. Nothing in this chapter shall prohibit the making of a special contract providing for the mutual exchange of service between any railroad company and any telegraph or telephone company, where the line of the telegraph or telephone company is situated upon or along the railroad right-of-way and used by both companies. [1929 c 96 § 1, part; 1911 c 117 § 18; RRS § 10354, part.] 

81.28.170 "Employee" and "families" defined. As used in this chapter the term "employee" includes furloughed, pensioned, and

superannuated employees; persons who have become disabled or infirm in the service of the carrier; the remains of a person killed or dying in the employment of a carrier; those entering or leaving its service and ex-employees traveling for the purpose of entering the service of any common carrier. The term "families" shall include the families of those persons named in this section; also the families of persons killed and the widows, during widowhood, and minor children, during minority, of persons who died while in the service of any such common carrier. [1929 c 96 § 1, part; 1911 c 117 

§ 18; RRS § 10354, part.] 

81.28.180 Rate discrimination prohibited. No common carrier shall, directly or indirectly, by any special rate, rebate, drawback, or other device, charge, demand, collect or receive from any person a greater or less compensation for any service rendered or to be rendered in the transportation of persons or property except as authorized in this title, than it charges, demands, collects, or receives from any person for doing a like and contemporaneous service in the transportation of a like kind of traffic under the same or substantially similar circumstances. [1911 c 117 § 20; RRS § 10356.] 

1. Foreign traffic-Interstate commerce-Discriminatory charges-Burden of proof-Violation of tariff schedule-Oceanic competition. Fisher v. G. N. 

Ry. Co., 49 Wash. 205 (1908); see also Coeur d'alene & Spokane Ry. Co. v. U. P. 

Ry. Co., 49 Wash. 244 (1908). 

2. Railway commission law does not prevent a railway company from entering into a contract with a shipper to carry particular goods at a fixed rate for a given time, and even though the rate so charged is less than published tariff rate, if consideration passed at time contract was made, it will be valid. 

Sultan Ry. & Timber Co. v. G. N. Ry. Co., 58 Wash. 604 (1910). 

3. At common law, a shipper had a right of action against a common carrier for unjust discrimination in freight rates between himself and others similarly situated whenever such discrimination operated to his special injury. Chas. 

H. Lilly Co. v. Nor. Pac. Ry. Co., 64 Wash. 589 (1911). 

4. The Commission has jurisdiction to investigate a charge of discrimination growing out of the violation of the long and short haul clause since such a discriminatory charge is unreasonable because it is unlawful. Belcher v. 

Tacoma Eastern R. Co., 99 Wash. 34 (1917). 

5. The public service statute does not contemplate that the charge made by a carrier for a particular class of service shall always be exactly the same to all shippers, but it does require the fixing of a tariff which is general and public, rather than individual, and that shipments under it be made according thereto, and not according to exceptions to it. 

The fact that a timber company at its own expense furnishes facilities for the shipment of its logs, enabling each car to be loaded and unloaded once in each twenty-four hours, for which service it is charged the same freight rates as other shippers who do not furnish such facilities, does not show discrimination against it in favor of such other shippers. Public Service Comm. v. 

State ex rel. Great Northern R. Co., 118 Wash. 629; 25 A.L.R. 186 (1922). 

6. Order of Department requiring removal of discrimination sustained. 

Discrimination alone does not warrant reparation. O.-W. R. & N. Co. v. Dept. 

of Public Works, 151 Wash. 142 (1929). 

7. Transportation of freight upon a commutation rate is a special and distinct service different from ordinary traffic, and not under "the same or similar circumstances," within the prohibition of this section; hence if service by commutation rates for trucks loaded is accorded to all persons and

localities and descriptions of traffic, there is no discrimination.

Puget Sound 

Nav. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 156 Wash. 489 (1930). 

8. In determining the reasonableness of a rate, the effect on some particular shipper is immaterial. The test is the effect on the public. Testimony as to the effect of a rate on some particular individual or company is immaterial. 

Where the Department has not made findings on the essential facts, but has decided the case on an erroneous basis, the case will be remanded for further findings. Great Northern R. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 161 Wash. 29 (1931). 

9. Prepayment of freight, discrimination by requiring. 21 L.R.A. (N.S.

) 982. 

10. Switching charges, right of railroad to discriminate in respect of. 2 

A.L.R. 585. 

11. Conspiracy to violate Interstate Commerce Act by giving and receiving rebates. 11 A.L.R. 199. 

12. Franchise provisions for free or reduced rates as within constitutional or statutory provisions prohibiting discrimination. 15 A.L.R. 1200. 

13. Special services or facilities afforded by shipper as a factor in carriers' rates. 25 A.L.R. 191. 

14. Jurisdiction of state court of action involving discrimination in relation to interstate shipment. 64 A.L.R. 341, 346, 356. 

15. Department may at hearing adjust rates so that they bear proper relationship. However, if Department finds that one rate is discriminatory because higher than other rates, the finding, to be sustained, must be based on further finding that rates to which it was compared were just, fair, reasonable and sufficient. Op. Atty. Gen. April 6, 1941. 

81.28.190 Unreasonable preferences prohibited. No common carrier shall give any undue preference or advantage to any person or locality or to any particular description of traffic, or subject any particular person or locality or any particular description of traffic, to any undue or unreasonable prejudice or disadvantage. [1911 c 117 § 21; RRS § 10357.] 

1. It is not unlawful discrimination in charges or facilities for a railroad company to refuse to extend a spur of its track away from its existing tracks and over land not belonging to the railroad to a warehouse or elevator, when it has never done a like service to other shippers in the same line of business, but has confined its service to according them facilities for shipment by granting to them leases upon its right of way for the construction of elevators abutting upon its tracks. Northwestern Warehouse Co. v. Oregon R. & Nav. Co., 32 

Wash. 218 (1903). 

2. The railway commission law, prohibiting discrimination between shippers by common carriers, does not prevent a railway company from entering into a contract with a shipper to carry particular goods at a fixed rate for a given time, or to the amount of a given quantity. Sultan R. & Timber Co. v. 

Great N. R. Co., 58 Wash. 604 (1910). 

3. In an action by a shipper for unjust discrimination by a common carrier, seeking to recover switching charges paid, a complaint is insufficient where it fails to allege that the defendant had failed to comply with the provisions of the act to regulate commerce with reference to the filing of a schedule of rates and that the rate charge exceeded the rate shown on the schedule. Lilly Co. v. 

Northern Pac. R. Co., 64 Wash. 589 (1911). 

4. A community is entitled to something more than mere reasonable rates, since the rates must be relatively equal to all shippers where there is no structural or operative difference favoring a haul from one point over a haul from another to the same shipping points; and it is unreasonable and unlawful discrimination for a railroad company to charge less rates on class commodities from Seattle than from Tacoma to the "jobbing center" of Spokane and tributary points, the difference in rates being sufficiently great to drive the Tacoma manufacturers and jobbers from the markets. Public Service

Comm. ex rel. Transportation Bureau v. Northern Pac. R. Co., 77 Wash.

635 (1914). 

5.

Prosser, an intermediate point, entitled to same milling-in-transit privi leges as Yakima. State ex rel. N. P. R. Co. v. Comm., 95 Wash. 376 (1917). 

6. Charge of discrimination is not libelous. Puget Sound Nav. Co. v. Carter, 233 Fed. 832. 

7. The prohibition of this section is only against discrimination which is unjust and unreasonable; and milling-in-transit privileges may be granted if not unjust; and allowance for a back haul may be fair on a long haul which the road could not be compelled to grant on a shorter haul. State ex rel. Great 

Northern R. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 112 Wash. 520 (1920). 

8. Section does not apply to franchise conditions that are proprietary rights of city, as carrying policemen free. State ex rel. Seattle v. Seattle & 

Rainier Valley R. Co., 113 Wash. 684 (1921). 

9. Permissive order of Department as to name of station on railway made mandatory. State ex rel. Bingen v. Dept. of Public Works, 164 Wash. 563 

(1931). 

10. A common carrier may extend credit to some shippers and deny it to others without violating the provisions of this section. Op. Atty. Gen. Nov. 18, 1912. 

11. A spur track constructed by a railroad company as a part of its general system upon land which it holds for railroad uses for the purpose of handling its own faciilties does not exclude the public from use of said spur where no other facilities are furnished by railroad. Op. Atty. Gen. April 22, 1913. 

12. Department may waive collection of undercharges, and act places no time limit on issuance of such an order. Op. Atty. Gen. Aug. 29, 1929. 

81.28.200 Long and short haul. No common carrier shall charge or receive any greater compensation in the aggregate for the transportation of persons or of a like kind of property, for a shorter than a longer distance over the same line in the same direction, the shorter being included within the longer distance, or charge any greater compensation as a through rate than the aggregate of the intermediate rates; but this shall not authorize any such carrier to charge and receive as great a compensation for a shorter as for a longer distance or haul: Provided, That upon application of a common carrier the commission may by order authorize it to charge less for a longer than for a shorter distance for the transportation of persons or property in special cases after investigation, but the order must specify the extent to which the carrier making the application is relieved from the operation of this section, and only to the extent so specified shall any carrier be relieved from the requirements of this section. [1911 c 117 § 22; RRS § 10358.] 

1. Cited in 97 Wash. 599; 3 A.L.R. 198 (1917). 

2. Commission has jurisdiction concerning charges of unjust discrimination arising from violation of long and short haul provision. Belcher v. Tacoma 

Eastern Railroad Co., 99 Wash. 34 (1917). 

3. The exacting of a switching charge per car of grain delivered to consignee's place of business and received from another line held not a violation of this section. State ex rel. Albers Bros. Milling Co. v. Dept. of Public Service, 197 Wash. 622 (1939). 

4. After rates which have been in effect have been determined to be in violation of long and short haul clause, Commission has no power to issue order retroactive in effect approving those rates. Op. Atty. Gen. Dec. 16, 1912.

5. When long and short haul clause of chapter 81, Laws of 1905, became effective, existing rates of carriers subject to that act which did not conform to its provisions immediately became unlawful unless the Commission, upon the application of the carrier, authorized their continuance. Op. Atty. Gen. Dec. 

16, 1912. 

81.28.210 Transportation at less than published rates-Rebating. 

No common carrier, nor any officer, agent or employee thereof, shall assist or permit any person or corporation to obtain transportation for any person or property between points within this state at less than the rates then established and in force, by means of false billing, false classification, false weight or weighing, or false report of weight, or by any other device or means. No person or corporation, nor any officer, agent, or employee thereof, who delivers property for transportation within the state to a common carrier, shall seek to obtain or obtain such transportation for such property at less than the rates then established and in force therefor, by false billing, false or incorrect classification, false weight or weighing, false representation of the contents or substance of a package, or false report or statement of weight, or by any device or means, whether with or without the consent or connivance of a common carrier or any of its officers, agents, or employees. 

No person or corporation, nor any officer, agent, or employee thereof, shall knowingly or wilfully, directly or indirectly, by false statement or representation as to the cost, value, nature or extent of injury, or by the use of any false billing, bill of lading, receipt, voucher, roll, account, claim, certificate, affidavit, or deposition, knowing the same to be false, fictitious, or fraudulent, or by any false, fictitious, or fraudulent statement or entry, obtain or attempt to obtain any allowance, rebate, or payment for damage, or otherwise, in connection with or growing out of the transportation of persons or property, or agreement to transport such persons or property, whether with or without the consent or connivance of such common carrier or any of its officers, agents, or employees, whereby the compensation of such carrier for such transportation shall be in fact made less than the rates then established and in force therefor. 

No person or corporation, nor any officer, agent, or employee thereof, who delivers property for transportation within the state to a common carrier, shall seek to obtain or obtain such transportation by any false representation, false statement, or false paper or token as to the contents or substance thereof, where the transportation of such property is prohibited by law. [1911 c 117 § 23; RRS § 10359.] 

1. Where a carrier has agreed that a preferential rate on carriage of freight shall apply to other shippers of the same commodity between points named, its published tariff is, in effect, replaced by a lower one, and a shipper who has been compelled to pay rates established in the published tariff is damaged to the extent of the difference between the two rates. Tacoma 

Eastern Railroad Co. v. Public Service Comm., 117 Wash. 609 (1921).

2. Nothing in this act provides for either a criminal prosecution or a penalty action against a shipper who merely accepts or receives commodities from carrier who transported them at less than the published rate, where shipper did not induce nor procure such lower rate. Op. Atty. Gen. April 24, 1923. 

81.28.220 Action for treble damage. The attorney general, whenever he has reasonable grounds to believe that any person has knowingly accepted or received from any carrier of persons or property subject to the jurisdiction of the commission, either directly or indirectly, any unlawful rebate, discount, deduction, concession, refund, or remittance from the rates or charges filed and open to public inspection as provided in this title, shall prosecute a civil action in the name of the state in the superior court of Thurston county to collect three times the total sum of such rebates, discounts, deductions, concessions, refunds, or remittances so accepted or received within three years prior to the commencement of the action.

All penalties collected under this section shall be paid to the state treasurer and deposited in the public service revolving fund. [1937 c 169 § 5; RRS § 10447-1.] 

Penalties Grain and terminal warehouses: RCW 22.08.430. 

81.28.230 Commission to fix just, reasonable and compensatory rates. When the commission finds, after hearing had upon its own motion or upon complaint, that rates, fares or charges demanded, exacted, charged, or collected by common carrier for the transportation of persons or property within the state, or in connection therewith, or that the regulations or practices of the carrier affecting such rates are unjust, unreasonable, discriminatory, unduly preferential, or in any wise in violation of the provisions of law, or that such rates, fares, or charges are insufficient to yield a reasonable compensation for the service rendered, it shall determine the just, reasonable, or sufficient rates, fares, or charges, regulations, or practices to be thereafter observed and enforced and shall fix them by order. [1911 c 117 § 53, part; RRS § 10389, part.] 

1. The reduction of rates as to ten per cent of the passengers carried by an interurban road to a figure that will give a profit on the actual cost of that part of the haul, although not an adequate return upon the investment, is justified where the company thereby earns a revenue that it could not otherwise obtain and its profits on its other business are not affected and when the same is all that such patrons can afford to pay for the service; and such a rate is not unjust discrimination against persons and places. Puget Sound Elec. R. 

Co. v. Railroad Comm., 65 Wash. 75; Ann. Cas. 1913B 763 (1911). 

2. Courts have jurisdiction of damages for denial of service or extortionate charge. Johnson v. Pacific P. & L. Co., 90 Wash. 492 (1916). 

3. The granting of milling privileges in transit must be without wrongful prejudice to the rights of other shippers, and its control is within the jurisdiction of the Commission. State ex rel. Northern Pacific Ry. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 95 Wash. 376 (1917). 

4. Commission cannot relieve street railway of franchise burdens though charge for service inadequate. State ex rel. Tacoma R. & P. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 101 Wash. 601 (1918).

5. Under this section the Commission has power to abrogate a pre-existing franchise provision requiring the sale of commutation tickets at a certain price; since the city could not bind itself as against the state's exercise of the police power in fixing reasonable rates. State ex rel. Seattle v. Public Service Comm., 103 Wash. 72 (1918). 

6. Court ordered rates higher than contract rates, Commission having ordered filed rates reduced. North Coast Power Co. v. Kuykendall, 117 Wash. 

563 (1921). 

7. Shippers' special equipment or quantity of freight cannot be basis of lower rate. Public Service Comm. v. Great Northern Ry. Co., 118 Wash. 629 (1922). 

8. Findings not discredited because Department filed charges-Prudent investment valuation-Working capital-Duplicated plant in use-Not in use-- Former valuation and betterments-Correction of mistakes and errors-Retro active orders-Overcharges. Pacific Coast Elev. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 

130 Wash. 620 (1924). 

9. Department is not justified in fixing rates on the basis of erroneous reports filed by carriers without affording carrier an opportunity to correct reports. State ex rel. Clear Lake Lbr. Co. v. Kuykendall, 130 Wash. 674 (1924). 

10. Sweeping power to readjust rates contrary to franchise denied. Monroe Water Co. v. Monroe, 135 Wash. 355 (1925). 

11. Direct findings required-Question is one of fairness between carrier and public. Great Northern Ry. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 161 Wash. 29 

(1931). 

12. If road is private carrier to state line, state may fix rates. Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul R. R. Co. v. Campbell River Mills Co., 53 F. (2d) 69. 

13. Cited in holding Department may sue to set aside differential rates fixed by I.C.C. Dept. of Public Works v. Interstate Commerce Commission, 

55 F. (2d) 392. 

14. Federal control as affecting power to regulate rates on railroad not under Federal control. 8 A.L.R. 982. 

15. Pullman and sleeping car service, regulation by Commission. 11 A.L.R. 996. 

16. Street railways, power of Commission to regulate. 39 A.L.R. 1517. 

17. Motor trucks or buses, jurisdiction over carriers. 51 A.L.R. 820. 

18. Transportation Act as affecting power of Commission to award reparation for excessive rates. 52 A.L.R. 326. 

81.28.240 Commission may order improved facilities and service. 

When the commission finds, after such hearing, that the rules, regulations, practices, equipment, appliances, facilities, or service of a common carrier in respect to the transportation of persons or property are unjust, unreasonable, unsafe, improper, or inadequate it shall determine the just, reasonable, safe, adequate, and proper rules, regulations, practices, equipment, appliances, facilities, or service to be observed, furnished, constructed, or enforced and used in the transportation of persons and property by the carrier, and fix them by its order or rule. [1911 c 117 § 53, part; RRS § 10389, part.] 

1. This act revokes the power of a city, after taking effect of the law, to legislate upon the subject of overcrowding the street cars, or operating in accordance with specified schedules, and vests the same in the Commission. 

Seattle Electric Co. v. Seattle, 78 Wash. 203 (1914). 

2. Courts have jurisdiction of damages for denial of service or extortionate charge. Johnson v. Pacific P. & L. Co., 90 Wash. 492 (1916). 

3. Courts have no jurisdiction in first instance of subjects vested in Commission. Robinson v. Pacific Tel. & Tel. Co., 118 Wash. 318 (1922).

4. Requiring transportation of caretakers of livestock is regulation of interstate commerce and invalid. Camas Prairie Railroad Co. v. Dept. of

Public Works, 125 Wash. 653 (1923). 

5. Findings not discredited because Department filed charges-Prudent investment valuation-Working capital-Duplicated plant in use-Not in use- Former valuation and betterments-Correction of mistakes and errors-Retro active orders-Overcharges. Pacific Coast Elevator Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 130 Wash. 620 (1924). 

6. Order requiring cars, etc., authorized. Puget Sound Tr. L. & P. Co. v. 

Reynolds, 223 Fed. 371. 

7. Pullman and sleeping car service, regulation by Commission. 11 A.L.R. 996. 

8. Street railways, power of Commission to regulate. 39 A.L.R. 1517. 

9. Right of way, Commission's power to require company to grant or renew leases or other privileges on. 47 A.L.R. 109. 

10. Motor trucks or buses, jurisdiction over carriers. 51 A.L.R. 820. 

11. Commission may order railroad to furnish tank cars for transportation of oil in bulk where shipping demand exists and when railroad has held itself out as carrier of such products. Op. Atty. Gen. Nov. 10, 1910. 

12. Commission has no power to compel railroad to give up part of its passenger station to a telephone company for installation of a phone bootha railroad cannot be required to give up the use of property acquired and held by it for discharging its duties as a common carrier for purposes not connected with railroad transportation. If after hearing Commission were to determine that in order to render adequate service, a telephone was needed in its station, then it could order such installation. Op. Atty. Gen. Dec. 19, 1913. 

13. Duty of carrier and shipper in connection with cleaning and cooperate of cars for grain carriage. Op. Atty. Gen. Aug. 22, 1927. 

14. Commission has no authority to order railroad to renew lease with shipper for whom railroad built warehouse on its right of way unless it appears that failure to do so would result in an unlawful discrimination. Op. Atty. 

Gen. March 20, 1929. 

81.28.250 Commission may complain of interstate rates. The commission shall investigate all interstate rates, fares, charges, classifications, or rules, or practices relating thereto, for or in relation to the transportation of persons or property. 

Where any acts in relation thereto shall take place within this state, which are, in the opinion of the commission, excessive or discriminatory, or are levied or laid in violation of the act of congress entitled "An Act to regulate commerce," approved February 4, 1887, and the acts amendatory thereof and supplementary thereto, or are in conflict with the rulings, orders, or regulations of the Interstate Commerce Commission, the commission shall apply, by petition, to the Interstate Commerce Commission for relief, and may present to that commission all facts coming to its knowledge respecting violations of the rulings, orders, or regulations of that commis sion, or violations of the said acts. [1911 c 117 § 58; RRS § 10394.] 

81.28.260 Bicycles as baggage. Bicycles are baggage, and shall be transported as baggage for passengers by railroad and steamboat companies, and subject to the same liabilities as other baggage. No passenger shall be required to crate, cover, or otherwise protect any such bicycle: Provided, That a railroad or steamboat company shall

not be required under this section to transport more than one bicycle for one person. [1899 c 15 § 1; RRS § 10495.] 

81.28.270 Limitation of action for collection of transportation charges. All actions at law by railroads, common and contract carriers by motor truck, and all other public carriers for recovery of their charges, or any part of them, for any common carrier service performed by said carriers, shall be begun within two years from the time the cause of action accrues, and not after. [1945 c 117 § 1;

Rem. Supp. 1945 § 167-1.] 

81.28.280 Reports of wrecks, etc. The commission may require reports to be made by any common carrier of all wrecks, collisions, or derailments occurring on its line. Such notice shall not be admitted as evidence or used for any purpose against the company giving it in any action for damages growing out of any matter mentioned in the notice. [1911 c 117 § 63, part; RRS § 10399, part.] 

81.28.290 Investigation of accidents, wrecks. The commission shall investigate all accidents that may occur upon the lines of any common carrier resulting in loss of life, to any passenger or employee, and may investigate any and all accidents or wrecks occurring on the line of any common carrier. Notice of the investigation shall be given in all cases for a sufficient length of time to enable the company affected to participate in the hearing and may be given orally or in writing, in such manner as the commission may prescribe.

Such witnesses may be examined as the commission deems necessary and proper to thoroughly ascertain the cause of the accident or wreck and fix the responsibility therefor. The examination and investigation may be conducted by an inspector or deputy inspector, and they may administer oaths, issue subpoenas, and compel the attendance of witnesses, and when the examination is conducted by an inspector or deputy inspector, he shall make a full and complete report thereof to the commission. [1911 c 117 § 63, part; RRS § 10399, part.]
