81.52.010 Physical connections. Whenever the commission finds, after a hearing had upon its own motion or upon complaint, that the public necessities and conveniences would be subserved thereby, it shall order any two or more railroads of the same or similar gauge to make physical connections at any and all crossings, and at all points where a railroad shall begin or terminate at or near any other railroad, and at or near all cities, so that the cars of any such railroad company may be speedily transferred from one railroad to another, and shall order whether the expense thereof shall be borne jointly or otherwise. [1919 c 153 § 1; 1911 c 117 § 61; RRS § 10397.] 

1. Under the act of 1907, vesting the Railroad Commission with power to make regulations concerning the sufficiency of the trackage, railroad connections, sidings, etc., and to order additional trackage or siding constructed, the 

Commission has power to order physical track connections between different railroads. State ex rel. Oregon R. & Nav. Co. v. Railroad Comm., 52 Wash. 17 (1909). 

2. Question of public necessity determinable by court from the consideration of all the facts-the places and persons interested, the volume of business to be affected, the saving in time and expenses to the shipper, as against the cost and loss to the carrier. N. P. Ry. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 117 Wash. 593 (1921). 

3. The Department has no power to order track connections between interstate railroads. Dept. Memo. Nov. 17, 1944. 

81.52.020 Sidetrack and switch connections, duty to construct.

A railroad company, upon the application of any shipper, shall construct, maintain, and operate upon reasonable terms a switch connection with a lateral line of railway or private sidetrack owned, operated, or controlled by the shipper, and shall upon the application of any shipper provide upon its own property a sidetrack and switch connection with its line of railway, whenever such a sidetrack and switch connection is reasonably practicable, and can be put in with safety and the business therefor is sufficient to justify it. [1911 c 117 

§ 13; RRS § 10349.] 

1. Act giving Railroad Commission power to compel track connections between competing carriers is not unconstitutional; however, there was no sufficient showing of public convenience and necessity to justify such an order here. State ex. rel. O. R. & N. Co. v. Fairchild, 224 Ụ. Š. 510, 56 L. Ed. 863; reversing 52 Wash. 17 (1909). 

2. This section is not unconstitutional, and if the Commission finds, after hearing, that business will reasonably justify it, the railroad can be compelled to construct a spur track at the shipper's expense. State ex rel. Chicago, M. & P. 

S. Ry. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 77 Wash. 529 (1914). 

3. Sufficiency of shipper's request for connections upheld where railroad refused further negotiations and made no objections to the form or sufficiency of the demand. Id. 

4. Under this section, an agreement by a railroad company to construct temporary spur tracks to a mill is binding as a voluntary contract of the mill company, which cannot recover sums paid in excess of the cost. Skagit Mill 

Co. v. Great Northern R. Co., 111 Wash. 378 (1920); 276 U. S. 99, 72 L. Ed. 485, 48 S. Ct. 223. 

5. An order of the Commission directing a street railway company to extend its line a distance of 900 feet is arbitrary and unreasonable where its

only purpose is to serve a lumber mill in the carriage of lumber cars, there being no other property to serve and the cost of the extension and the operation for the mill service would cause an annual loss to the railway company. 

State ex rel. Willapa Elec. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 117 Wash. 510 (1921). 

6. Under terms of contract, the location and establishment of another shipper on a spur track relieved the previously sole shipper from his obligation to pay annual maintenance charges to the railroad. N. & M. Lumber Co. v. Chicago, M. & St. P. Ry. Co., 134 Wash. 291 (1925). 

7. Specific performance of contract by railroad company to build or maintain sidetracks, extension or branch line. 4 A.L.R. 529. 

8. Period covered by covenant or condition for location and maintenance of spur track or siding. 7 A.L.R. 820. 

9. Duty of railroads to operate spur tracks and sidings. 18 A.L.R. 722. 

10. Abandonment or relocation of sidetrack, right to recover damages. 

23 A.L.R. 555. 

11. Covenant to construct switch or siding as running with land. 41 A.L.R. 1369. 

81.52.030 Sidetrack and switch connection may be ordered by commission. Whenever the commission finds, after a hearing had upon its own motion or upon complaint, that application has been made by a shipper for a switching connection with a lateral line of railway or private sidetrack owned, operated, or controlled by him, or that application has been made by a shipper for the installation of a sidetrack upon the property of the railroad, and that such switch connection or sidetrack is reasonably practicable, can be put in with reasonable safety, and the business therefor is sufficient to justify it, and that the company has refused to install or provide it, the commission shall enter its order requiring such connection or the construction of such sidetrack. The shipper shall pay the legitimate cost and expense of constructing the connection or sidetrack which shall be determined in separate items by the commission, and before the company shall be compelled to incur any cost in connection therewith the cost shall be secured to it in such manner as the commission may require. 

Whenever a lateral line of railway, private sidetrack, or sidetrack upon the property of a railroad company shall be constructed under the provisions of this section, any person may connect therewith or use it upon the payment to the shipper incurring the primary expense of a reasonable proportion of the cost thereof, to be determined by the commission after notice to the interested parties: 

Provided, That such connection can be made without unreasonable interference with the right of the shipper incurring the primary expense. [1911 c 117 § 62; RRS § 10398.] 

1. Order requiring railroad to construct and operate spur track over private land to sawmill three hundred feet from right of way reversed. N. P. Ry. Co. v. Railroad Comm., 58 Wash. 360 (1910). 

2. Order requiring extension of spur track two hundred feet, arranged to permit teams to unload at all points along extension, held reasonable. 

State ex rel. G. N. Ry. Co. v. Railroad Comm., 60 Wash. 218 (1910). 

3. This section is not unconstitutional as depriving the company of property without due process of law; since the spur is to be built entirely on land

of the company held for public use, there is a full hearing, and the spur is to be paid for by others, and is open to the use of all shippers on reasonable terms. State ex rel. Chicago, M. & P. S. R. Co., v. Public Service Comm., 77 

Wash. 529; Ann. Cas. 1915D 202; L.R.A. 1918B 786 (1914). 

4. Shipper cannot recover difference, spur costing less than contract price. 

Skagit Mill Co. v. Great Nor. Ry., 111 Wash. 378 (1920). 

5. Order of Commission requiring street railroad to extend its line nine hundred feet to serve lumber mill is unreasonable, when the company is losing money and the loss would be increased. State ex rel. Willapa E. Co. v. Public 

Service Comm., 117 Wash. 510 (1921). 

6. Duty of railroad to operate spur tracks and sidings. 18 A.L.R. 722. 

7. Railroad company may be compelled to install a sidetrack if reasonably practicable. Op. Atty. Gen. Aug. 4, 1927. 

81.52.040 Spur tracks. Any company owning or operating a railway in this state, may construct, maintain, and operate public spur tracks, from its railroad or any branch thereof, to and upon the grounds of any mill, elevator, storehouse, warehouse, dock, wharf, pier, manufacturing establishment, lumberyard, coal dock, or other industry or enterprise, with all sidetracks, storage tracks, wyes, turnouts, and connections necessary or convenient to the use thereof; and it may acquire by purchase or condemnation, in the manner provided by the laws of this state for the acquisition of real estate for railway purposes, all necessary rights-of-way for such spur tracks, sidetracks, storage tracks, wyes, turnouts, and connections; the spur when constructed to be a public spur for the use of all industries located thereon: Provided, That the right to acquire by condemnation herein granted shall not be exercised over unimproved land for a greater distance than five miles, or over improved land for a greater distance than one mile, or over land within the limits of a city for a greater distance than one-fourth of a mile: Provided further, That this section shall not limit the rights granted with respect to the construction of branch lines. [1907 c 223 § 1; RRS § 10465.] 

81.52.050 Fences-Crossings-Cattle guards. Every person or company having the control or management of any railroad shall, outside of any city, and outside the limits of any sidetrack or switch, construct and maintain in good repair on each side of the railroad, along the line of the right-of-way, a substantial fence. At every point where any public highway crosses the railroad, a safe and sufficient crossing must be built and maintained. On each side of such crossing and at each end of a sidetrack or switch, outside of any city, a sufficient cattle guard shall be built and maintained. Any person holding land on both sides of the right-of-way may put in gates for his own use at such places as may be convenient. [1907 

c 88 § 1; RRS § 10507.] 

1. Cited in 101 Wash. 460. 

2. The railroad fence act of 1893 requiring railroads, where the same person owns land on both sides of the track, to put in and maintain necessary crossings and gates, was impliedly repealed by this section. Huffman v. Oregon 

R. & Nav. Co., 57 Wash. 494 (1910).

3. This section is not supplanted by a right of way agreement whereby a railroad company was to maintain a private crossing and gates, which the owner could leave open at his own risk, and the owner, in leaving the_gates open, assumed only the risk of accidents happening at the crossing. Snodgrass v. Spokane & Inland Empire R. Co., 87 Wash. 308 (1915). 

4. Under this section, an omission to fence only makes a prima facie case of negligence which the company may overcome by evidence. Benn v. Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul R. Co., 89 Wash. 522 (1916). 

5. A railroad company is not liable for damages done by trespassing cattle that strayed from its unfenced right of way to and upon the lands of an adjoining land owner, under this section. Hanson v. Northern Pac. R. Co., 90 Wash. 

516; L.R.A. 1916E 446 (1916). 

6. This section being entitled an act compelling railroads to fence their right of way and to protect the owners of stock and declaring the law of negligence, has no application to the maintenance of sidewalks at railroad crossings for the convenience of pedestrians. Bullock v. Yakima Valley Transp. Co., 108 Wash. 413 (1919). 

7. Commission may direct Attorney General to commence an action either in mandamus or injuncion to compel a railroad company to comply with the act relating to the fencing of its right of way. Ops. Atty. Gen., 1911-12, p. 291; Op. Atty. Gen. May 10, 1926. 

8. Owner of property on both sides of railway right of way may have a private grade crossing without a contract with the railroad company. Op. 

Atty. Gen. July 18, 1933. 

9. Railroad has a mandatory duty to fence its right of way. The Hanson decision does not hold to the contrary. Statute applies to an exclusively freight railroad. Administrative Code of 1921 did transfer enforcement of fence law to department of labor and industries. Op. Atty. Gen. November 1, 1943. 

10. Children, duty of railroad to fence track as against. 16 A.L.R. 944, 11. Constitutionality of statutes requiring railroad to fence tracks and build cattle-guards. 31 L.R.A. (N.S.) 861. 

12. Dogs as within contemplation of statute as to duty of railroads to fence against live stock. 46 A.L.R. 1536. 

13. Duty of railroad to keep cattle-guards in condition. 36 L.R.A. (N.S.) 

997; L.R.A. 1915B 134. 

14. Employment of independent contractor as affecting employer's liability under legal provisions requiring railroad companies to maintain fences. 

23 A.L.R. 992. 

15. Failure to fence as rendering railroad company liable for damages to or by the live stock after leaving right of way. 24 A.L.R. 1057. 

16. Federal control as affecting liability for failure to fence. 52 A.L.R. 310. 

17. Highway leading to but not crossing railroad tracks, liability for injury because of failure to fence. 47 A.L.R. 863. 933. 

18. Measure of care of railroad to keep up fence. 11 L.R.A. (N.S.) 228. 

19. Municipality, railroad's duty to fence tracks, within limits of. 16 A.L.R. 

20. Sufficiency as to type of cattle-guards at public or private crossings. 

75 A.L.R. 936. 

81.52.060 Liability for injury to stock. A person or company owning or operating a railroad shall be liable for all damages sustained in the injury or killing of stock in any manner by reason of his or its failure to construct and maintain such fence, crossing, or cattle guards; but when fences, crossings, and guards have been installed, and kept in good repair, such person or company shall not be liable for any such damages, unless negligently or unlawfully done. [1907 c 88 § 2; RRS § 10508.]

1. Contributory negligence of owner- -Allowing animals to go at large. 

Dickey v. Northern Pac. R. Co., 19 Wash. 350 (1898); Willett v. Oregon-Washington R. & Nav. Co., 94 Wash. 71 (1916). 

2. Effect of stock laws or fence laws as to care required. Ryan v. Northern 

Pac. R. Co., 19 Wash. 533 (1898); Taylor v. Spokane Falls & N. R. Co., 32 

Wash. 450 (1903); Benn v. Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul R. Co., 89 Wash. 522 (1916). 

3. This and the next section do not embrace injuries not happening through the operation of trains; the title of the act being limited to stock "injured by railway trains." Thayer v. Snohomish Logging Co., 101 Wash. 458 (1918). 

81.52.070 Negligence, evidence of. In all actions against persons or companies operating railroads, for injury to stock by collision. with moving trains, it is prima facie evidence of negligence on the part of such person or company to show that the railroad track was not fenced with a substantial fence or protected by a sufficient cattle guard at the place where the stock was injured or killed. 

[1907 c 88 § 3; RRS § 10509.] 

1. Presumptions and burden of proof. Dickey v. Northern Pac. R. Co., 19 

Wash. 350 (1898). 

2. Admissibility of evidence. Oregon Ry. & Nav. Co. v. Dacres, 1 Wash. 

195 (1890). 

3. Sufficiency of evidence. Benn v. Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul R. Co., 89 Wash. 522 (1916); Willett v. Oregon-Washington R. & Nav. Co., 94 Wash. 

71 (1916). 

4. Verdict, findings and costs. Curtis v. Oregon R. & Nav. Co., 36 Wash. 

55 (1904); Jolliffe v. Brown, 14 Wash. 155 (1896). 

5. Company not liable for injury to stock within incorporated town. Ryan v. N. P. Ry. Co., 19 Wash. 533 (1898). 

6. Existence of natural barrier open at ends does not release company. 

Taylor v. Spokane Falls & N. Ry. Co., 32 Wash. 450 (1903). 

7. Fences-Sufficiency of evidence-Injury to stock-Cattle-guards. Byrne v. Spokane & I. E. Ry. Co., 56 Wash. 667 (1910). 

8. Agreement between railroad company and private owner whereby company was to maintain private crossing and gates which owner could leave open at own risk, he assumes only risks of accidents happening at crossing and the contract does not supplant the statute where cattle strayed through open gate and upon right of way at a distant point. Snodgrass v. Spokane & I. E. 

Ry. Co., 87 Wash. 308 (1915). 

9. This section has a two-fold object; first, puts burden of proof on railroad company killing stock; second, protects passengers and employees from hazards of derailment of trains. Hanson v. N. P. Ry. Co., 90 Wash. 516 (1916). 

81.52.080 Grade crossings-Definitions. As used in RCW 81.52- .090 to 81.52.330, inclusive: "Highway" means all state and county roads, streets, alleys, parkways, and other public places used for travel by the public; "Railroad" includes all logging or industrial railroads; "Logging or industrial railroad" means a railway owned or operated primarily for the purpose of carrying the property of its owners or operators, or a limited class of persons, with all tracks, spurs, and sidings used in connection therewith; "Over-crossing" means any point or place where a highway crosses a railroad by passing above it;

"Under-crossing" means any point or place where a highway crosses a railroad by passing under it; "Over-crossing" or "under-crossing" shall also mean any point or place where one railroad crosses another railroad not at grade; "Grade crossing" means any point or place where a railroad crosses a highway or a highway crosses a railroad or one railroad crosses another, at a common grade. [(i) 1913 c 30 § 1; RRS § 10511. 

(ii) 1941 c 161 § 1; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 10511-1.] 

Severability: "If any section, subdivision, sentence, or clause of this act is for any reason held to be unconstitutional, such decision shall not affect the validity of the remaining portions of this act." [1913 c 30 § 22.] 

1. A road open to the general public although used primarily by grain trucks is a public highway since it is the right of public use and not the exercise of that right which constitutes a way a public highway. State ex rel. 

O.-W. R. & N. Co. v. Walla Walla County, 5 Wn. (2d) 95 (1940). 

2. Joint use of bridge by railway and automobiles is not a crossing of railroad and highway and does not require approval of Commission. Op. Atty. 

Gen. April 30, 1926. 

3. Department has no jurisdiction over grade crossing at spur track which has been abandoned by railroad company and is no longer being used. Op. 

Atty. Gen. Dec. 29, 1939. 

4. Term "highway" includes all ways open to the public. Alley crossings are therefore brought under supervision of Department. Private crossings are not included. Grade crossings on spur tracks owned by private industries for connecting their plants with common carrier railroad are under jurisdiction of Department. Unless rail line is abandoned and tracks removed, Department must inspect crossings and railroad must pay fee whether operating trains or not. But act does not apply to government-owned railroads. 

Atty. Gen. May 6, 1941.

Op. 

81.52.090 Grade separation required where practicable. All railroads shall cross existing railroads and highways by passing either over or under them, when practicable, and shall in no instance cross any railroad or highway at grade without authority first being obtained from the commission. All highways hereafter constructed shall cross existing railroads by passing either over or under them, when practicable, and shall in no instance cross any railroad at grade without authority first being obtained from the commission: 

Provided, That this section shall not prohibit a railroad company from constructing tracks at grade across other tracks owned or operated by it within established yard limits. In determining whether a separation of grades is practicable, the commission shall take into consideration the amount and character of travel on the railroad and on the highway; the grade and alignment of the railroad and the highway; the cost of separating grades; the topography of the country, and all other circumstances naturally involved in such an inquiry. [1913 c 30 § 2; RRS § 10512.] 

1. State may impose on railroad companies whose tracks cross each other, duty of installing safety devices.

Where companies agree upon interlocking device for public safety, the expense of installation should be equally apportioned by the Commission between the two. State ex rel. P. S. & Willapa Harbor Ry. Co. v. N. P. Ry. Co., 94 Wash. 10 (1916).

2. Commission may on its own motion file petition to change or eliminate grade crossing. State ex rel. Hayford v. Public Service Comm., 98 Wash. 648 (1917). 

3. Cited in showing necessity for condemnation for state road to avoid grade crossing. State ex rel. Urquhart v. Court, 112 Wash. 34 (1920). 

4. Under earlier act Commission could not deny crossing, but only decide what kind of a crossing should be constructed. State ex rel. Toppenish v. 

Public Service Comm., 114 Wash. 301 (1921). 

5. Municipal corporations-power to assume duty of providing and maintaining railroad crossings. 1 A.L.R. 316. 

6. Federal control as affecting duty as to grade crossings. 4 A.L.R. 1719. 

7. Municipal corporations' power to require railroad to eliminate grade crossings. 35 A.L.R. 1322. 

8. Constitutional power to compel railroad company to relocate or reconstruct highway crossing or to pay or contribute to expense thereof. 62 A.L.R. 815. 

81.52.100 Petition for crossing-Hearing-Order. Whenever any railroad company desires to cross any highway or railroad at grade, it shall file a written petition with the commission setting forth the reasons why the crossing cannot be made either above or below grade, and whenever the county commissioners of any county, or the municipal authorities of any city, or the state officers authorized to lay out and construct state roads, or state parks committee, desire to extend any highway across any railroad at grade, they shall file a written petition with the commission, setting forth the reasons why the crossing cannot be made either above or below grade. 

Upon receiving such petition the commission shall immediately investigate it, giving at least ten days' notice to the railroad company and the county or city affected thereby, of the time and place of such investigation, to the end that all parties interested may be present and heard. If the highway involved is a state road or parkway, the director of highways or state parks committee shall be notified of the time and place of hearing. The evidence introduced shall be reduced to writing and be filed by the commission. If it finds that it is not practicable to cross the railroad or highway either above or below grade, the commission shall enter a written order in the cause, either granting or denying the right to construct a grade crossing at the point in question. The commission may provide in the order authorizing a grade crossing, or at any subsequent time, that the railroad company shall install and maintain proper signals, warnings, flagmen, interlocking devices, or other devices or means to secure the safety of the public and its employees. [1937 c 22 § 1, part; 1913 c 30 § 3; RRS § 10513, part.] 

1. Where there is another feasible route other than a crossing, railroad company may not condemn lands of an established railroad company for crossing purposes even though new company would be put to greater expense in going around. State ex rel. Portland & Seattle Ry. Co. v. Superior Court, 45 Wash. 270 (1907). 

2. One railroad company may take lands of another for purposes of crossing where there is necessity therefor and where the taking is not a material

detriment to the established road. State ex rel. Col. V. R. Co. v. Superior 

Court, 45 Wash. 316, 88 Pac. 332 (1907). 

3. Railroad first in time takes its right subject to being crossed by another railroad; it was proper for the Commission to apportion the cost of the crossing even though the statute in force at the time of construction of the pioneer road required the junior road to pay all the cost. State ex rel. P. S. & Willapa 

Harbor Ry. Co. v. Nor. Pac. Ry. Co., 94 Wash. 10; 97 Wash. 701. Affirmed: 

250 U. S. 332, 63 L. Ed. 1013 (1919). 

4. In apportioning cost of interlocking devices at grade crossing, Commission should not be governed by priority, but should exercise its discretion. 

Review will not lie unless action is arbitrary. State ex rel. Tacoma Eastern 

Ry. Co. v. N. P. Ry. Co., 104 Wash. 405 (1918). 

5. Under this section the Department has jurisdiction to determine the character of the grade, under a petition for a crossing "at grade, if undercrossings and over-crossings are impracticable"; and it is immaterial that the petitioner showed that a grade crossing was impracticable. 

A railroad must acquire the right to cross another by purchase or condemnation and under this section the Department may regulate the place and manner of crossing, permitting a grade crossing if no other is practicable, and apportion the costs after a hearing. 

Under this section, the Department may hear and determine a toll logging road's petition to cross another road before the right is acquired by condemna tion. State ex rel. O.W. R. & N. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 155 Wash. 665 

(1930). 

6. Abolition and removal of grade crossings. State ex rel. Toppenish v. 

Public Service Comm., 114 Wash. 301 (1921); State ex rel. Seattle v. Nor. Pac. 

Ry., 128 Wash. 73 (1924); State ex rel. Oregon-Wash. R. & N. Co. v. Dept. of 

Public Works, 135 Wash. 644 (1925). 

7. Evidence sustained findings and order of Department granting petition by commissioners of Walla Walla County asking Department's consent to construction of a grade crossing. State ex rel. O.-W. R. & N. Co. v. Walla Walla 

County, 5 Wn. (2d) 95 (1940). 

8. Public Service Commission may not contribute to expense of undercrossing where no grade crossing is eliminated. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1913-14, p. 513. 

9. Commission has jurisdiction to hear petition for establishment of grade crossing over railroad under federal control, but the Director General should be made a party to the proceeding. Op. Atty Gen. Sept. 5, 1918. 

10. It is the duty of railroads to construct, erect and maintain sawbuck crossing signs of standard design upon state highways. There is no similar duty pertaining to county roads; however, the Department, upon the establishment of new grade crossings may, in its discretion, order the railroad to install such signals or warnings as it may find necessary. Op. Atty. Gen. Oct. 26, 1948. 

81.52.110 Supplemental hearing-Change of route. If the commission finds that it is impracticable to construct an over-crossing or under-crossing on the established or proposed highway, and shall find that by deflecting the established or proposed highway a practicable and feasible over-crossing or under-crossing or a safer grade crossing can be provided, it shall continue the hearing and hold a supplemental hearing thereon. At least ten days' notice of the time and place of the supplemental hearing shall be given to all land owners that may be affected by the proposed change in location of the highway. At the supplemental hearing the commission shall inquire into the propriety and necessity of changing and deflecting the highway as proposed. If the proposed change in route of the highway involves the abandonment and vacation of a portion of

an established highway, the owners of land contiguous to the portion of the highway to be vacated shall, in like manner, be notified of the time and place of the supplemental hearing. At the conclusion of the hearing, the commission shall enter its findings in writing, and shall determine the location of the crossing which may be constructed, and whether it shall be an under-crossing, over-crossing, or grade crossing, and shall determine whether or not any proposed change in the route of an existing highway, or the abandonment of a portion thereof is advisable or necessary to secure an over-crossing, under-crossing, or safer grade crossing. [1937 c 22 § 1, part; 1913 

c 30 § 3; RRS § 10513, part.] 

81.52.120 Requirements of order on change of route. If the commission finds and determines that a change in route of an existing highway, or vacation of a portion thereof, is necessary or advisable, it shall further find and determine what private property or property rights it is necessary to take or damage for the purpose of constructing the highway along a new route, and what private property or property rights, will be affected by the proposed vacation of a portion of an existing highway. The property and property rights found necessary to be taken or damaged shall be described in the findings with reasonable accuracy. In any action brought to acquire the right to take or damage any such property or property rights, the findings of the commission shall be conclusive as to the necessity therefor. A copy of the findings shall be served upon all parties to the cause. [1937 c 22 § 1, part; 1913 c 30 § 3; RRS § 10513, part.] 

1. Under community property laws, the wife of owner of lands which may be taken or injuriously affected by change or establishment of grade crossing must be served with notice of the hearing on the crossing matter. Op. Atty. 

Gen. June 16, 1917. 

81.52.130 Petition for alteration of crossing. The governing body of any city, or the county commissioners of any county, within which there exists any under-crossing, over-crossing or grade crossing, or where any street or highway is proposed to be located or established across any railroad, or any railroad company whose road is crossed by any highway, may file with the commission a petition alleging that the public safety requires the establishment of an under-crossing or over-crossing, or an alteration in the method and manner of an existing crossing, and its approaches, or in the style and nature of construction of any existing crossing, or a change in the location of an existing highway or crossing, the closing of an existing highway crossing, and the diversion of travel thereon to another highway or crossing; or if not practicable to change the crossing from grade or to close and discontinue it, the opening of an additional crossing for the partial diversion of travel, and praying that it be ordered. 

If the existing or proposed crossing is on a state road, highway, or parkway, the petition may be filed by the director of highways or state parks committee.

Upon such petition being filed, the commission shall fix a time and place for hearing the petition and shall give not less than ten days' notice thereof to the petitioner, the railroad company, and the municipality or county in which the crossing is situate. If the highway involved is a state highway or parkway, like notice shall be given to the director of highways or state parks committee. If the change petitioned for requires that private property or property rights be taken or damaged to open up a new route for the highway, or requires that any portion of any existing highway be vacated, ten days' notice of the hearing shall be given to the owner of the private property and property rights which it is necessary to take or damage and to the owner of the private property or property rights that will be affected by the proposed vacation of the existing highway. 

The commission shall also cause the notice of hearing to be published once in some newspaper of general circulation in the community where the crossing is situate, which publication shall appear at least two days prior to the date of hearing. At the time and place fixed in the notice, all persons interested shall be entitled to be heard. [1937 c 22 § 2, part, last am'ds 1913 c 30 § 4; RRS § 10514, part.] 

1. Authority requiring grade separation not required to take bond to protect lienors. Dixon v. Parker, Moran & Parker, 102 Wash. 101 (1918). 

2. Notwithstanding the authority of the Highway Department to locate state roads, the Department of Public Works had jurisdiction in matters affecting grade crossings to make local changes in highways with the view of avoiding the crossing. State ex rel. O.-W. R. & N. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 135 Wash. 644 (1925). 

3. Absence of accidents at grade crossing for over forty years does not mean that the Department was wrong in finding that the public safety required that the crossing be closed. Dept. of Transportation v. Snohomish 

County, 35 Wn. (2d) 247 (1949). 

4. Evidence primarily established that the grade crossing was potentially dangerous, which was the major point with which the Department was concerned. Id. 

5. Many Washington statutes are based upon the theory that all grade crossings are dangerous. Id. 

6. Department upon proper showing of necessity may authorize the elimination of an overhead crossing and the establishment of a grade crossing but the Department is without power to alter any contract for the maintenance or replacement of under-crossings or over-crossings when such contract was made prior to the 1913 Crossing Act. Op. Atty. Gen. June 22, 1929. 

81.52.140 Hearing. At the conclusion of the hearing the commission shall enter written findings of fact in like manner as upon petition for new crossings. It shall also enter its order based upon the findings, which shall specify whether the highway shall continue at grade or whether it shall be changed to cross over or under the railroad in its existing location or at some other point, and whether an over-crossing or under-crossing shall be established at the proposed location of any street or highway or at some other point, or

fr ים 11 whether the style and nature of construction of an existing crossing shall be changed, or whether the highway shall be closed and travel thereon diverted to another channel, or any other change that the commission may find advisable or necessary: Provided, That in an emergency where a highway is relocated to avoid a grade crossing, or a new crossing is constructed in the vicinity of an existing crossing in the interest of public safety, the commission may order the existing crossing closed without notice or hearing. 

Any petition herein authorized may be filed by the commission on its own motion, and proceedings thereon shall be the same as provided for the hearing and determination of a petition filed by a railroad company. [1937 c 22 § 2, part, last am'ds 1913 c 30 § 4; RRS 

§ 10514, part.] 

1. Commission may file petition. State ex rel. Hayford v. Public Service 

Comm., 98 Wash. 648 (1917). 

81.52.150 Restrictions on structures in proximity of crossings.

No building, loading platform, or other structure which will tend to obstruct the vision of travelers on a highway or parkway of approaching railway traffic, shall be erected or placed on railroad or public highway rights-of-way within a distance of one hundred feet of any grade crossing located outside the limits of any city unless authorized by the commission, and no railway cars or equipment shall be spotted less than one hundred feet from such crossing except to serve existing facilities of industries. 

The commission may specify the minimum vertical and horizontal clearance of under-crossings constructed, repaired, or reconstructed, except as to primary state highways. [1937 c 22 § 2, part, last am'ds 1913 c 30 § 4; RRS § 10514, part.] 

81.52.160 Duty to maintain crossings. When a highway crosses a railroad by an over-crossing or under-crossing, the framework and abutments of the crossing shall be maintained by the company, and the roadway thereon and approaches thereto shall be maintained by the county or municipality in which they are situated, or if the highway is a state road or parkway, the roadway shall be maintained as provided for the maintenance and repair of state roads and parkways.

The railings of over-crossings shall be considered a part of the roadway. Whenever a highway intersects a railroad at common grade, the roadway approaches within one foot of the outside of either rail shall be maintained by highway authority, and the planking or other materials between the rails and for one foot on the outside thereof shall be installed and maintained by the railroad company. At crossings involving more than one track, maintenance by the railroad company shall include that portion of the crossing between and for one foot on the outside of each outside rail. The minimum length of such planking or other materials shall be twenty

feet on future installation or repairs. [1937 c 22 § 3; 1913 c 30 § 5;

RRS § 10515.] 

1. Everything above the framework of an overpass is a part of the roadway and the railroad is not responsible for negligent failure to maintain adequate railing. Pridmore v. Nor. Pac. Ry. Co., 109 Wash. 305 (1920). 

2. Federal control as affecting duty as to maintenance and repair of crossing. 4 A.L.R. 1719; 19 A.L.R. 693. 

3. Highway commission may contract with railroad for division of costs; same should be approved by Public Service Commission. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1913-14, p. 513. 

81.52.170 Cost when railroad crosses highway. Whenever new railroads are constructed across existing highways, or highway changes are made either for the purpose of avoiding grade crossings on such new railroads, or for the purpose of crossing at a safer and more accessible point, the entire expense of crossing above or below the grade of the existing highway, or changing the route thereof, shall be paid by the railroad company. [1937 c 22 § 4A, last am❜ds 

1913 c 30 § 6; RRS § 10516A.] 

81.52.180 Cost when highway crosses railroad. Whenever a new highway is constructed across a railroad, or an existing grade crossing is eliminated or changed, or the style or nature of construction of an existing crossing is changed, the entire expense of constructing a new grade crossing, an over-crossing, under-crossing, or safer grade crossing, or changing the nature and style of construction of an existing crossing, including the expense of constructing approaches to such crossing and the expense of securing rights-of-way for such approaches shall be apportioned by the commission between the railroad and the municipality, or county affected, or if the highway is a state road or parkway, between the railroad and the state, in such manner as justice may require, regard being had for all facts relating to the establishment, reason for, and construction of the improvement. If the highway involved is a state road or parkway, the amount not apportioned to the railroad company shall be paid as provided by law for constructing such state road or parkway. 

[1937 c 22 § 4B, last am'ds 1913 c 30 § 6; RRS § 10516B.] 

81.52.190 Cost when railroad crosses railroad. Whenever two or more lines of railroad owned or operated by different companies cross a highway, or each other, the portion of the expense of making such crossing not chargeable to any municipality, county, or to the state, and the expense of constructing and maintaining signals, warnings, flagmen, interlocking or other devices, to secure the safety of the public and the employees of the company, as the commission may require to be constructed and maintained, shall be apportioned between the companies in such manner as justice may require, regard being had for all facts relating to the establishment, reason for, and construction of the improvement, unless the companies

the mutually agree upon an apportionment. If it becomes necessary for the commission to make an apportionment between the companies, a hearing for that purpose shall be held, at least ten days' notice of which shall be given. [1937 c 22 § 4C, last am'ds 1913 c 30 § 6;

RRS & 10516C.] 

1. Under the statute providing that the portion of the expense of making a railroad crossing at grade not chargeable to a county or city shall be apportioned between the railroad companies, means not only the expense of constructing the actual crossing, but the expense of constructing and maintaining such protective devices as the Commission may require. 

In the absence of a statute to the contrary, a railroad desiring to cross another at grade must bear the expense of the crossing, including the cost of installing and maintaining such safety devices as the necessities of the case require. 

The railroad company first in time takes its right subject to being crossed by the line of another railway; hence the statute did not violate the constitutional inhibition against taking private property for public use without compensation.

Under the statute providing that the portion of the expense of making a railroad crossing at grade not chargeable to a county or city shall be apportioned between the railroad companies, the Commission could not charge the entire cost against the road desiring the crossing, merely because the older company was first in point of time, where the record shows no equity in favor of one road over the other; since to require one road to bear all the expense is not an "apportionment." State ex rel. Puget Sound & Willapa Harbor R. 

Co. v. Northern Pac. R. Co., 94 Wash. 10 (1916). Affirmed: Northern Pac. Ry. 

Co. and Public Service Comm. v. Puget Sound & Willapa Harbor Ry. Co., 250 

U. S. 332, 63 L. Ed. 1013 (1919). 

2. In apportioning the costs of interlocking device to be maintained at a grade crossing, the Commission should not be governed by the priority of the pioneer road, nor by the character of the service or construction by the crossing road; but is to exercise its discretion on the resulting convenience and inconvenience. 

In the absence of abuse or arbitrary action, the courts will not review the discretion of the Commission in apportioning the cost of interlocking devices; and no abuse appears in apportioning two-thirds of the cost to the petitioner seeking to cross a main line road by its branch line, to serve Camp Lewis on request of the government; considering the greater number of trains run on the main line and the fact that the branch line was for the benefit of territory devoted to a particular use. State ex rel. Tacoma Eastern R. Co. v. Northern 

Pac. R. Co., 104 Wash. 405 (1918). 

3. For apportionment cases not involving Public Service Commission see: 

State v. Northern Pac. Ry. Co., 128 Wash. 73 (1924); Oregon-Wash. R. & N.

Co. v. Pacific Coast R. Co., 138 Wash. 430 (1926); State v. Northern Pac. Ry. 

Co., 166 Wash. 437 (1932). 

4. Under this section, providing that where an existing grade crossing is eliminated or changed, the expense shall be apportioned between the railroad and the state or county, it is error to assess the entire cost to the county. State ex rel. O.-W. R. & N. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 135 Wash. 644 (1925). 

5. An order of the Department granting a toll logging road the right to construct a railroad crossing "forthwith" is not objectionable in that it must first obtain the right by purchase or condemnation. State ex rel. Oregon-Wash. 

R. & Nav. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 155 Wash. 665 (1930). 

6. A just and equitable distribution of railway contribution to the expense of a street viaduct or overhead crossing is made on the basis of the widths of the respective rights of way spanned, considering the cost of the approaches and of the span as one, regardless, in this instance, of the number of trains passing over the several tracks and other matters of a kindred nature. State ex rel. Seattle v. Northern Pac. Ry. Co., 166 Wash. 437 (1932). 

7. For case construing a contract between a city and a railroad providing

for division of cost of construction and reconstruction, see City of Seattle v. 

Northern Pac. Ry. Co., 12 Wn. (2d) 247 (1942). 

8. Constitutional power to compel railroad to pay or contribute to expense of relocating or reconstructing highway crossing. 62 A.L.R. 815. 

9. Where highway which crosses railroad tracks at grade is half within an incorporated city and half in a county, the Commission may apportion the cost of grade separation among the city, county and railroad. Op. Atty. Gen. 

March 28, 1917. 

10. Department upon proper showing of necessity may authorize the elimination of an overhead crossing and the establishment of a grade crossing but the Department is without power to alter any contract for the maintenance or replacement of under-crossings or over-crossings when such contract was made prior to the 1913 Crossing Act. Op. Atty. Gen. June 22, 1929. 

81.52.200 Apportionment of cost. In the construction of new railroads across existing highways, the railroads shall do all the work of constructing the crossings and road changes that may be required, and shall acquire and furnish whatever property or easements may be necessary, and shall pay the entire expense of such work including all compensation or damages for property or property rights taken or damaged.

In all other cases the construction work may be apportioned by the commission between the parties who may be required to contribute to the cost thereof as the parties may agree, or as the commission may consider advisable.

All work within the limits of railroad rights-of-way shall in every case be done by the railroad company owning or operating the line.

The cost of acquiring additional land, rights, or easements to provide for the change of existing crossings shall, unless the parties otherwise agree, in the first instance be paid by the municipality or county within which the crossing is located; or in the case of a state road or parkway, shall be paid in the manner provided for paying the cost of acquiring lands, rights, or easements for the construction of state roads or parkways. 

The expense accruing on account of property taken or damaged shall be divided and paid in the manner provided for dividing and paying other costs of construction. Upon the completion of the work and its approval by the commission, an accounting shall be had, and if it shall appear that any party has expended more than its proportion of the total cost, a settlement shall be forthwith made. If the parties are unable to agree upon a settlement, the commission shall arbitrate, adjust, and settle the account after notice to the parties. In the event of failure of any party to pay its proportion of the expense, the sum with interest from the date of the settlement may be recovered in a civil action by the party entitled thereto. In cases where the commission has settled the account, its finding as to the amount due shall be conclusive in any civil action brought to recover it if the finding has not been reviewed or appealed from, and the time for review or appeal has expired. If any party reviews or

DE 

appeals from any finding or order of the commission apportioning the cost between the parties liable therefor, the court shall cause judgment to be entered in such review proceedings for such sum as may be found lawfully due by one party to another. [1937 c 22 

§ 5; 1913 c 30 § 7; RRS § 10517.] 

1. Where the parties to construction of a crossing are unable to agree on sharing the cost, the Commission shall arbitrate the matter. Op. Atty. Gen. 

Dec. 6, 1920. 

81.52.210 Time for performance. The commission, in any order requiring work to be done, may fix the time within which it shall be performed: Provided, That if any party having a duty to perform within a fixed time under any order of the commission shall make it appear that the order cannot reasonably be complied with within the time fixed by reason either of facts arising after the entry of the order or of facts existing prior to the entry thereof that were not presented, and with reasonable diligence could not have been sooner presented to the commission, such party shall be entitled to a reasonable extension of time within which to perform the work. An order of the commission refusing to grant an extension of time may be reviewed as provided for the review of other orders. 

[1913 c 30 § 10; RRS § 10520.] 

81.52.220 Practice and procedure. Modes of procedure under 

RCW 81.52.080 to 81.52.300, inclusive, unless herein otherwise provided, shall be as provided in other provisions of this title. The commission may adopt rules to govern proceedings and to regulate the mode and manner of all investigations and hearings under this chap ter. [1913 c 30 § 11; RRS § 10521.] 

81.52.230 Service of process. All notices required to be served by RCW 81.52.080 to 81.52.300, inclusive, shall be in writing, and shall briefly state the nature of the matter to be inquired into and investigated. Notices may be served in the manner provided for the service of summons in civil cases, or by registered mail. When service is made by mail, the receipt of the receiving post office shall be sufficient proof of service. When it is necessary to serve notice of hearings on owners of private property or property rights, and the owners cannot be found, service may be made by publication in the manner provided for the publication of summons in civil actions, except that publication need be made but once each week for three consecutive weeks, and the hearings may be held at any time after the expiration of thirty days from the date of the first publication of the notice. [1913 c 30 § 12; RRS § 10522.] 

81.52.240 Review and appeal. Upon the petition of any party to a proceeding before the commission, any finding or order made hereunder, except as otherwise provided, may be reviewed in the superior court of the county wherein the crossing is situated, and

the reasonableness and lawfulness of such finding or order inquired into and determined, as provided in this title for the review of the commission's orders generally. [1937 c 22 § 6; 1913 c 30 § 13; RRS 

§ 10523.] 

1. Under this section, providing that an order of the Commission eliminating a grade crossing may be reviewed and the reasonableness and lawfulness determined, an order which the Commission had power to make will not be disturbed unless there is a showing of unreasonableness or unlawfulness. 

State ex rel. Hayford v. Public Service Comm., 98 Wash. 648 (1917). 

2. Statute relating to construction or maintenance of crossing of intersecting railroads as affecting appeal from decision of Commission. 40 A.L.R. 740. 

81.52.250 Eminent domain. Whenever to carry out any work undertaken under RCW 81.52.080 to 81.52.300, inclusive, it is necessary to take or damage any private property or property rights, the right so to take or damage may be acquired by condemnation as follows: 

(1) In cases where new railroads are constructed and laid out by railroad company authorized to exercise the power of eminent domain, the right to take or damage private property or property rights shall be acquired by a condemnation proceeding brought in its own name and prosecuted as provided for the exercise of the power of eminent domain by railroad companies. (2) In cases where it is necessary to take or damage private property or property rights to permit the opening of a new highway or highway crossing across a railroad, the right to take or damage such property or property rights shall be acquired by the municipality or county petitioning for the new crossing by a condemnation proceeding brought in the name of the municipality or county as provided for the exercise of the power of eminent domain by such municipality or county. If the highway involved is a state highway, the right to take or damage private property or property rights shall be acquired by a condemnation proceeding prosecuted under the laws relative to the exercise of the power of eminent domain in aid of such state highway. (3) In cases where the commission orders changes in existing crossings and it is necessary to take or damage private property or property rights to execute the work, the right to take or damage such property or property rights shall be acquired in a condemnation proceeding prosecuted in the name of the state under the laws relating to the exercise of the power of eminent domain by cities of the first class for street and highway purposes: Provided, That in the cases mentioned in this subdivision the full value of any land taken shall be awarded, together with damages accruing to the remainder of the land not taken by reason of the severance of the part taken, but in computing the damages to the remainder, the jury shall offset against such damages the special benefits accruing

3 to the remainder by reason of the proposed improvement.

[1913 

c 30 § 15; RRS § 10525.] 

81.52.260 Abatement of illegal crossings. If a crossing is constructed, maintained, or operated, or is about to be constructed, operated, or maintained in violation of RCW 81.52.080 to 81.52.300, inclusive, or in violation of any order of the commission, such construction, operation, or maintenance may be enjoined or abated, as provided for the abatement of nuisances. Suits to enjoin or abate may be brought by the attorney general, or by the prosecuting attorney of the county in which the unauthorized crossing is located. 

[1913 c 30 § 16; RRS § 10526.] 

81.52.270 Mandamus to compel performance. If any railroad company, county, municipality, or officers thereof, or other person, fails to perform any duty required of it or them under RCW 81.52- .080 to 81.52.300, inclusive, or any order of the commission, the performance of such duty may be compelled by mandamus, or other appropriate proceeding prosecuted by the attorney general upon the request of the commission. [1913 c 30 § 17; RRS § 10527.] 

81.52.280 Penalty. If any railroad company fails to obey the requirements of RCW 81.52.080 to 81.52.300, inclusive, or any order of the commission made thereunder, it shall be liable to a penalty not to exceed five thousand dollars, such penalty to be recovered in a civil action brought in the name of the state by the attorney general. [1913 c 30 § 18; RRS § 10528.] 

81.52.290 Obstructions in highways. Whenever, in order to carry out any work ordered under RCW 81.52.080 to 81.52.300, inclusive, it is necessary to erect and maintain posts, piers, or abutments in a highway that right is hereby granted: Provided, That, in case of a state road they shall be placed only at such points on the road as may be approved by the director of highways and fixed after such approval by order of the commission. [1925 ex.s. c 179 

§ 2; 1913 c 30 § 19; RRS § 10529.] 

1. Right of railroad to construct and maintain piers, pillars or abutments within street or highway at crossing. 62 A.L.R. 1519. 

81.52.300 Scope of chapter. RCW 81.52.080 to 81.52.300, inclusive, shall not be operative within cities authorized to frame their own charters, and shall not apply to street railway lines operating on or across any street, alley, or other public place within the limits of any city, except that no streetcar line outside of cities authorized to frame their own charters shall cross a railroad at grade without express authority from the commission. The commission may not change the location of a state highway without the approval of the director of highways, or the location of any crossing thereon adopted or approved by the highway commission, or grant a railroad

authority to cross a state highway at grade unless the director of highways consents thereto. [1925 ex.s. c 179 § 3; 1913 c 30 § 21; RRS § 10531.] 

81.52.310 Annual inspection of industrial crossings. All crossings on the line of every logging and other industrial railway shall be inspected annually by the commission as to condition, maintenance, and safety in the interest of the public in order that it may require necessary and proper improvements, changes, and repairs. [1941 

c 161 § 2; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 10511-2.] 

81.52.320 Reimbursement of inspection cost. Every person operating any logging railroad or industrial railway shall, prior to July 1st of each year, file with the commission a statement showing the number of, and location, by name of highway, quarter section, section, township, and range of all crossings on his line and pay with the filing a fee not to exceed ten dollars for each crossing so reported. 

The commission shall, by order, fix the exact fee based on the cost of rendering such inspection service. All fees collected shall be deposited in the state treasury to the credit of the public service revolving fund. Intersections having one or more tracks shall be treated as a single crossing. Tracks separated a distance in excess of one hundred feet from the nearest track or group of tracks shall constitute an additional crossing. Where two or more independently operated railroads cross each other or the same highway intersection, each independent track shall constitute a separate crossing. 

Every person failing to make the report and pay the fees required, shall be guilty of a misdemeanor and in addition be subject to a penalty of twenty-five dollars for each day that the fee remains unpaid after it becomes due. [1951 c 111 § 1; 1941 c 161 § 3; formerly 

Rem. Supp. 1941 § 10511-3.] 

81.52.325 Not operative within charter cities. RCW 81.52.310 and 81.52.320 shall not be operative within the limits of cities authorized to frame their own charters. [1951 c 111 § 2.] 

81.52.330 Employment of experts. The commission may employ temporarily such experts, engineers, and inspectors as may be necessary to supervise changes in existing crossings undertaken under this chapter, the expense to be included in the cost of the particular change of grade on account of which it is incurred. 

The commission may also employ such engineers and other persons as permanent employees as may be necessary to properly administer this chapter. [1937 c 22 § 7; 1913 c 30 § 14; RRS § 10524.]

Sections Chapter 81.56 RAILROADS—SHIPPERS AND PASSENGERS

state Constitution. 

Facilities for loading grain, hay or other commodities from farm vehicles into cars: RCW 22.08.340.

Stealing railway or steamboat tickets: RCW 9.54.110. 

81.56.010 Distribution of cars. Every railroad company shall, upon reasonable notice, furnish to all persons who may apply therefor and offer property for transportation, sufficient and suitable cars for the transportation of the property in carload lots. In case at any particular time a railroad company has not sufficient cars to meet all the requirements for transportation of property in carload lots, all cars available for such purpose shall be distributed among the several applicants therefor, without unjust discrimination between shippers, localities, or points of delivery. [1911 c 117 § 11; RRS § 10347.] 

1. This section is not unconstitutional as an unlawful delegation of legislative powers and imposes expressly the duty to furnish cars to shippers promptly. State ex rel. Chicago, M. & St. P. R. R. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 94 Wash. 274 (1917). 

2. Shortage of cars as affecting liability of carrier to furnish. 10 A.L.R. 342. 

3. Validity of statutory provision including attorneys' fees as part of penalty for failure to furnish cars. 11 A.L.R. 893; 90 A.L.R. 530. 

4. Jurisdiction of state courts of actions in relation to interstate shipments. 

64 A.L.R. 351. 

81.56.020 Distributing book must be kept. Every railroad company shall keep, subject to the inspection of any bona fide shipper,

a book known as "car distributing book," which shall be kept by officers or employees of the company in such manner and form as the commission shall direct, showing, among other things, all orders for cars received by the company, the name of the person ordering them, the time when and place where the cars are required and the time when and place where such cars were supplied, and such other information as the commission may prescribe. [1911 c 117 § 12; RRS § 10348.] 

81.56.030 Discrimination prohibited. Every railroad company shall, under such regulations as may be prescribed by the commission, afford all reasonable, proper, and equal facilities for the interchange of passengers, tonnage, and cars, loaded or empty, between the lines, owned, operated, controlled, or leased by it and the lines of every other railroad company; and shall, under such regulations as the commission may prescribe, receive, and transport, without delay or discrimination, the passengers, tonnage, and cars, loaded or empty, of any connecting line of railroad: Provided, That perishable freight of all kinds and livestock shall have precedence of shipment.

Every railroad company shall receive from every other railroad company at a connecting point the tonnage carried by the other company in the cars in which it is loaded, and haul it through to the point of destination if the destination is upon a line owned, operated, or controlled by such company, or, if the destination is upon the line of some other company, haul such tonnage in such cars through to the connecting point upon the line operated, owned, controlled, or leased by it by way of route over which the car is billed, and there deliver the tonnage to the next connecting carrier, under such regulations as the commission may prescribe. [1911 c 117 

§ 24; RRS § 10360.] 

1. Common carrier cannot be compelled to interchange shipment with a private carrier but may be compelled to furnish the same service to all shippers or a private line. State ex rel. Clear Lake Lbr. Co. v. Dept. of 

Public Works, 130 Wash. 674 (1924). 

2. State's power to require interstate carrier to make track connections with other roads. 22 A.L.R. 1078. 

3. Where no question of through routes or joint rates is presented, Commission has jurisdiction to consider petition of shippers for establishment of local interchange tracks between two railroads where such tracks would not be an "extension" of the line of either road. Op. Atty. Gen. July 15, 1926, and supplemental Op. Aug. 24, 1926. 

81.56.040 Must grant equal privileges. No railroad company doing business in this state shall allow any telegraph or telephone company, or any individual, any facilities, privileges or rates for transportation of men or material, or for repairing their lines, not allowed to all telegraph and telephone companies and individuals. 

[1890 p 292 § 4; RRS § 11341.]

81.56.050 Joint rates and through routes. Whenever the commission is of the opinion, after hearing had upon its own motion or upon complaint, that the rates and charges in force over two or more railroads, between any two points in the state, are unjust, unreasonable, or excessive, or that no satisfactory through route or joint rate exists between such points, and that the public necessities and convenience demand the establishment of a through route and a joint rate between such points, it may order such railroads to establish such through route, and may establish and fix a joint rate which will be fair, just, reasonable, and sufficient, to be demanded and collected, and the commission may order that carload freight moving between such points shall be carried by the different companies, parties to such through route and joint rate, without being transferred from the originating cars. In case no agreement exists between the railroads for the interchange of cars, the commission, before making such order, shall make rules for the expeditious and safe return and proper compensation for the cars so loaded by the company receiving them. [1911 c 117 § 57; RRS § 10393.] 

1. Commission has power to apportion joint rates and through routes under this section between common carriers. The law does not violate either the state or Federal constitution. State ex rel. Great Northern Ry. Co. v. 

Public Service Comm., 76 Wash. 625 (1913). 

2. The through joint rate and milling-in-transit privilege is a facility granted by railroads. A through joint rate is a public necessity and a millingin-transit privilege a facility which may become public by reason of its use. 

Being granted at North Yakima and denied at Prosser, there is a public discrimination. Such being the case, the Commission acted wholly within its powers when it found the facts sustaining its order and ordered that a through joint rate and milling-in-transit privilege be granted by the railroads to the complainant at Prosser. State ex rel. N. P. Ry. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 95 Wash. 376 (1917). 

3. Joint rates with private logging carrier refused. State ex rel. Clear Lake 

Lumber Co. v. Kuykendall, 132 Wash. 700 (1925). 

4. Commission has no authority to make order for joint rates and through route between points not in existence at the time of the promulgation of such order. Ops. Atty. Gen. 1907-8, p. 120. 

81.56.060 Scales at junctions for forest products. All railroad companies operating within this state, shall provide scales, and weigh at junction or at some common point within this state, all cars loaded with lumber, shingles, or other forest products for shipment. [1905 c 126 § 1; RRS § 10474.] 

81.56.070 Charges, how based. All charges for freight on said commodities, except where error is apparent, shall be based on the weights determined by such weighing stations, and all bills of lading of railroad companies operating within the limits of this state shall specify these provisions: Provided, That these provisions shall not apply to switching charges or to the handling of logs where the charge is by the car or by the thousand feet. [1905 c 126 § 2; RRS § 10475.]

81.56.080 Shipper's count and weight. Any railroad company employee acting as weigher shall upon request of any shipper give him a statement showing gross and net weight of any shipment by him. Sworn count and weight of shipper shall be presumptive evidence of true weight where error in railroad weights is apparent. 

[1905 c 126 § 3; RRS § 10476.] 

81.56.090 Cars weighed separately. All cars shall be weighed on the scales separately, and not attached to other cars, and at a stand still. [1905 c 126 § 4; RRS § 10477.] 

81.56.100 Penalty. The violation of any provision of RCW 81.56- .060, 81.56.070, 81.56.080 and 81.56.090 by a railroad company shall be punished by a fine of twenty dollars for each car improperly weighed and billed. Action for the recovery of this penalty may be maintained in any county in which the company has an agent who may be served with process, and the penalty shall be paid into the county treasury. [1905 c 126 § 5; RRS § 10478.] 

81.56.110 Special contracts regarding weights. Nothing herein shall prevent the shipper and carrier from entering into a private contract regarding weights when it is impracticable to weigh. [1905 

c 126 § 6; RRS § 10479.] 

81.56.120 Cruelty to stock in transit. Railroad companies in carrying or transporting animals shall not permit them to be confined in cars for a longer period than forty-eight consecutive hours without unloading them for rest, water, and feeding for a period of at least two consecutive hours, unless prevented by unavoidable accident. In estimating such confinement, the time during which the animals have been confined without such rest on connecting roads from which they are received shall be included. Animals so unloaded shall, during the rest, be properly fed and watered by the person having the custody of them, or in case of his default in so doing, then by the railroad company transporting them, at the expense of the owner or person in custody thereof, and the company shall in such case have a lien upon the animals for food, care, and custody furnished, and shall not be liable for such detention of the animals. 

If animals are transported where they can and do have proper food, water, space, and opportunity to rest, the foregoing provision in regard to their being unloaded shall not apply. Violators of this section shall be punished by a fine not exceeding one hundred dol lars. [1893 c 27 § 4; RRS § 10494.] 

81.56.130 Commission rules to expedite traffic. The commission may provide by proper rules and regulations the time within which all railroads shall furnish, after demand therefor, all cars, equipment, and facilities for the handling of freight in carload and less than carload lots, and receiving, gathering, and transporting, after

demand, of all express packages and the delivery thereof at destination; the extent of free gathering and distributing limits for express packages in cities; the distance that freight shall be transported each day after receipt; the time within which consignors or persons ordering cars shall load them; and the time within which consignees shall unload the freight and receive freight from the freight rooms, and provide the penalties to be paid to consignors and consignees for delays on the part of railroads to conform to such rules, and prescribe the penalty to be paid by consignors and consignees to railroads for failure to observe such rules. [1911 c 117 

§ 59; RRS § 10395.] 

1. Orders of the Commission respecting railroad service and facilities will not be set aside on appeal as unreasonable, unless they clearly so appear, the presumption being that they are reasonable. State ex rel. Great Northern R. 

Co. v. Railroad Comm., 60 Wash. 218 (1910). 

2. An order of the Commission requiring a daily mixed freight and passenger service over a branch line fourteen miles long is unreasonable, where the income from passenger travel resulting therefrom was only nine cents per mile, and cost over thirty cents per mile, the present bi-weekly freight service was adequate, and a financial loss to the company, which loss would be increased by the change without any great necessity for it. State ex rel. Nor. 

Pac. R. Co. v. Railroad Comm., 62 Wash. 193 (1911). 

3. Although a railroad company operates only interstate trains, it may be required by the Commission to render an adequate local service for the accommodations of the traveling public. State ex rel. Great Northern R. Co. 

v. Public Service Comm., 81 Wash. 275 (1914). 

4. This section, empowering the Commission to fix the time for furnishing cars and provide the penalty to be paid shippers for delays, is not unconstitutional as an unlawful delegation of legislative powers, in contravention of 

Constitution, Art. 2, Sec. 1. 

The statute providing no method for the collection of demurrage charges to be paid to shippers for delay in furnishing cars, the remedy of the shipper is by action at law; there being no right without a remedy. 

It will not be assumed that the legislature intended to adopt the construction of other state courts that such rules applied to both kinds of commerce when to do so would make the law unconstitutional. State ex rel. Chicago, Milwaukee & St. P. R. Co. v. Public Service Comm., 94 Wash. 274 (1917). 

5. Order of Department requiring railroad to change name on station house, tickets, literature, etc., affirmed. State ex rel. Bingen v. Dept. of Public 

Works, 164 Wash. 563 (1931). 

6. Power of Commission to require carrier to furnish cars of a special type.

23 A.L.R. 411. 

7. A municipal election day should not be excluded in computing time on demurrage tariffs. Op. Atty. Gen. March 19, 1917. 

8. Under certain circumstances, the Department may lawfully authorize a waiver of the collection of demurrage charges. Op. Atty. Gen. March 20, 1929. 

81.56.140 Agent must have fixed place of business. Every person selling, issuing, or dealing in railroad transportation in this state, must have a fixed place of business in the city or town wherein he transacts said business, and shall keep his certificate of authority posted in a conspicuous place in his place of business. [1905 c 180 

§ 2; RRS § 10497.]

81.56.150 Regulating sale of passenger tickets. Every person or corporation engaged wholly or in part in the business of carrying passengers for hire, shall provide every agent authorized to sell its passage tickets in this state, with a certificate of his authority, attested by its seal and the signature of its manager, secretary, or general passenger agent, which shall contain a designation of the place of business at which such authority shall be exercised. 

Every person and every corporation or association, and every officer, agent, or employee thereof who sells, exchanges, or transfers, or has in his possession with intent to sell, exchange, or transfer, or maintains, conducts, or operates any office or place of business for the sale, exchange, or transfer of any passage ticket or pass or part thereof, or any other evidence of a right to travel upon any railroad or boat, whether the same is owned or operated within or without the limits of this state, in any place except his place of business, or within such place of business without having rightfully in his possession and posted in a conspicuous place therein the certificate of authority hereinabove provided for, shall be guilty of a misde meanor. [1909 c 249 § 396; RRS § 2648.] 

81.56.160 Redemption of unused tickets. Every person or corporation engaged wholly or in part in the business of carrying passengers for hire in this state, and every authorized ticket agent thereof, to whom there shall be presented by the holder thereof, within one year after its expiration, any passage ticket or part thereof, or other evidence of right to travel, wholly or in part upon the railroad or boat of such person or corporation, which shall be wholly or partially unused, who fails to redeem the same within three days after presentation, upon the following terms, to wit: (1) When wholly unused, for the price paid therefor; and (2) When partially unused, for the price paid therefor, less the regular toll or charge for the passage had; shall be punished by a fine of not more than five hundred dollars, and in addition thereto shall forfeit to the holder of such ticket or part thereof or other evidence of a right to travel, three times the redeemable value thereof. [1909 c 249 § 397; RRS § 2649.]

Chapter 81.60 RAILROADS-SPECIAL POLICE AND POLICE REGULATIONS Sections

RCW 9.91.020.

Reward for arrest and conviction of persons placing obstructions on railroad tracks: RCW 10.85.020. 

Stealing railway or steamboat tickets: RCW 9.54.110. 

Tampering with light or signal so as to endanger engine, motor, train or car: RCW 88.08.020. 

Train robbery: RCW 9.75.020. 

Trespass on railway track: RCW 9.83.010. 

81.60.010 Governor may appoint special police. The governor may appoint and commission special police officers at the request of any railroad corporation and may revoke any such appointment at his pleasure. [1915 c 118 § 1; RRS § 10542.] 

81.60.020 Application for appointment. Any railroad corporation desiring the appointment of any of its officers, agents, or servants, not exceeding five in number, for any part of a railroad under the jurisdiction of any one division superintendent, as special police officers, shall file with the governor an application stating the name, age, and place of residence of the person whose appointment it desires, the position he occupies with the corporation, the nature of his duties and the reasons why his appointment is desired, which application shall be signed by the president or some managing officer of the corporation and shall be accompanied by an affidavit of the officer to the effect that he is acquainted with the person whose appointment is sought, that he believes him to be of good moral character, and that he is of such character and experience that he can be safely intrusted with the powers of a police officer. 

[1915 c 118 § 2; RRS § 10543.] 

81.60.030 Oath of policemen. Before receiving his commission each appointee shall take, subscribe, and file with the governor an oath to support the Constitution of the United States, the Constitution and laws of the state and to faithfully perform the duties of his office. [1915 c 118 § 3; RRS § 10544.]

81.60.040 Duties of policemen. Every such police officer shall when on duty have the authority of peace officers, but shall exercise such authority only in the protection of the property belonging to or under the control of the corporation at whose instance he is appointed and in preventing, and making arrest for, violations of law upon or in connection with such property. [1915 c 118 § 4; RRS § 10545.] 

81.60.050 Badge. Every special police officer shall, when on duty, wear in plain view a metal shield bearing the words "special police" and the name of the corporation by which he is employed. 

[1915 c 118 §5; RRS § 10546.] 

81.60.060 Liability for unlawful acts. The corporation procuring the appointment of any special police shall be solely responsible for the compensation for his services and shall be liable civilly for any unlawful act of such officer resulting in damage to any person. 

[1915 c 118 § 6; RRS § 10547.] 

81.60.070 Malicious injury to railroad property. Every person who, in such manner as might, if not discovered, endanger the safety of any engine, motor, car, or train, or any person thereon, in any manner interferes or tampers with or obstructs any switch, frog, rail, roadbed, sleeper, viaduct, bridge, trestle, culvert, embankment, structure, or appliance pertaining to or connected with any railway, or any train, engine, motor, or car on such railway; and every person who discharges any firearm or throws any dangerous missile at any train, engine, motor, or car on any railway, shall be punished by imprisonment in the state penitentiary for not more than twenty five years. [1909 c 249 § 398; RRS § 2650.] 

81.60.080 Sabotaging rolling stock. Any person or persons who wilfully or maliciously, with intent to injure or deprive the owner thereof, takes, steals, removes, changes, adds to, alters, or in any manner interferes with any journal bearing, brass, waste, packing, triple valve, pressure cock, brake, air hose, or any other part of the operating mechanism of any locomotive, engine, tender, coach, car, caboose, or motor car used or capable of being used by any railroad or railway company in this state, shall be guilty of a felony, and upon conviction thereof shall be punished by imprisonment in the penitentiary for not more than five years, or by a fine not exceeding one thousand dollars, or by both such fine and imprisonment. [1941 

c 212 § 1; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 2650-1.] 

81.60.090 Receiving stolen railroad property. Every person who buys or receives any of the property described in RCW 81.60.080, knowing the same to have been stolen, shall be guilty of a felony, and upon conviction thereof shall be punished as provided in such section. [1941 c 212 § 2; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 2650-2.]
