81.64.010 Grant of franchise. The legislative authority of a city or town or the board of county commissioners may grant authority to construct, maintain, and operate a railway motivated by any power other than steam together with all necessary appurtenances thereto, on or across any public street or road within its jurisdiction and may prescribe the terms and conditions thereof and the grade and elevation. [1907 c 99 § 1, part; 1903 c 175 § 1; RRS § 11082, part.] 

81.64.020 Notice-Hearing-Order. If the application for such authority is made to the board of county commissioners, it shall fix the time and place of hearing thereon and the county auditor shall, at the expense of the applicant, post notice thereof in three public places in the county seat and in at least one place on the road affected, at least thirty days before the date set for the hearing, and shall publish a like notice three times in a daily newspaper published in the county, or if there is no such newspaper, then in the newspaper doing the county printing; the last publication to be at least five days before the date set for the hearing. The notice shall state the time and place of the hearing, the name of the applicant and a description of the road to be affected and the purpose of the application. The hearing may be adjourned from time to time by order of the board.

After the hearing the board may enter an order granting the application in whole or in part as it deems for the best public

interest, and prescribing the location of the railway so as to cause the least interference with other uses of the road. [1907 c 99 § 1, part; 1903 c 175 § 1; RRS § 11082, part.] 

81.64.030 May cross public road. If the railway is located in part on a private right-of-way, it may cross any public road, doing no unnecessary damage thereby. The person constructing a railway on or across a public road shall be liable to the county for all necessary expense to the county for restoring the road to its former condition. 

[1907 c 99 § 1, part; 1903 c 175 § 1; RRS § 11082, part.] 

81.64.040 Eminent domain. Every corporation incorporated under the laws of any state or territory of the United States and doing business in this state for the purpose of operating railroads or railways by electric power, may appropriate real estate and other property for any corporate purpose, in the same manner and procedure as in the case of ordinary railroad corporations authorized to exercise the right of eminent domain: Provided, That such right of eminent domain shall not be exercised with respect to any public road or street until the location of the electric railroad or railway thereon has been authorized in accordance with RCW 81.64.010, 81.64.020 and 81.64.030. [1903 c 175 § 2; RRS § 11083.] 

81.64.050 Right of entry. Every such corporation shall have the right to enter upon any land between the termini of its proposed road for the purpose of examining, locating and surveying such road, doing no unnecessary damage thereby. [1899 c 94 § 2; RRS § 11085.] 

Reviser's note: The 1899 act was superseded by 1903 c 173 and c 175 but there was no express repeal in the 1903 acts, so the nonconflicting part of the 1899 act still stands. 

81.64.060 Purchase or lease of street railway property. Any corporation incorporated under the laws of any state or territory of the United States, for the purpose of constructing, owning, or operating railroads or railways by electric power, may lease or purchase and operate the whole or any part of the electric railroad or electric railway, of any other corporation, together with the franchises, powers, and all property or appurtenances appertaining thereto, when consented to by the holders of at least two-thirds in amount of the capital stock of the lessor or grantor corporation. [1903 c 175 

§ 3; RRS § 11084.] 

81.64.070 Consolidation of companies. With the consent of the majority in interest of their shareholders, two or more corporations operating street railway lines within or in the suburbs of the same municipality, may amalgamate their businesses and properties by consolidation, sale, lease, or other appropriate means, and either by conveyance to a third corporation or one to the other. [1917 c 170 

§ 1; RRS § 11086.]

81.64.080 Fares and transfers. No street railroad company shall charge, demand, or collect more than five cents for one continuous ride within the corporate limits of any city or town: Provided, That such rate may be exceeded or lowered as to any municipally owned street railroad when the corporate authorities of the municipality, by ordinance authorize the collection of a higher or lower rate of fare, to be specified in the ordinance, and as to any other street railroad company the rate may be exceeded or lowered with the permission or upon the order of the commission, after the filing of a tariff or a complaint by the company and a hearing thereon. 

Every street railroad company shall, upon such terms as shall be just and reasonable, furnish to its passengers transfers entitling them to one continuous trip over portions of its lines within the city not reached by the originating car. [1919 c 33 § 1; 1911 c 117 § 25; RRS & 10361.] 

1. The 1911 law declared unconstitutional-see State v. Crawford, 74 Wash. 

248; 46 L.R.A. (N.S.) 1039 (1913). 

2. Under this section, prior to amendment, providing that no street railroad company shall charge or collect more than five cents for one continuous ride within the corporate limits, the Commission has no power to increase the fare which a street railway company may charge within city limits to more than five cents, although that sum is found insufficient to pay a reasonable return on the property devoted to the public service and provide an adequate and sufficient service, since to harmonize the various provisions of the act, it must be construed as intending to give power to regulate rates so long only as it does not exceed the limit of five cents. State ex rel. Tacoma R. & 

Power Co. v. Public Service Comm., 101 Wash. 601 (1918). 

3. This act does not abrogate a prior franchise ordinance by which a street car company was obligated to carry city policemen and firemen in uniform free while in the discharge of their duties. State ex rel. Seattle v. Seattle & 

Rainier Valley R. Co., 113 Wash. 684; 15 A.L.R. 1194 (1921). 

4. Free transportation of policemen, firemen and mail carriers, municipal contract or statute requiring. 8 A.L.R. 682. 

5. Franchise rates of street railway, power of Public Service Commission to increase. 29 A.L.R. 356. 

6. Public Service Commission's power to regulate street railway fares. 

39 A.L.R. 1534. 

81.64.090 Competent employees required. Street railway or streetcar companies shall employ none but competent men to operate or assist as conductors, motormen, or gripmen upon any street railway, or streetcar line. [1901 c 103 § 1; RRS § 11073.] 

81.64.100 Competency defined. A man shall be deemed competent to operate or assist in operating cars usually used by street railway or streetcar companies, only after first having served at least three days under personal instruction of a regularly employed conductor, motorman, or gripman on a car or dummy in actual service on the particular street railway or streetcar line for which the service of an additional man may be required: Provided, That 

E during a strike on the streetcar lines the railway companies may employ competent men who have not worked three days on that particular streetcar line. [1901 c 103 § 2; RRS § 11074.]

81.64.110 Penalty. Any violation of RCW 81.64.090 by a stockholder or any officer or employee of any company owning or operating any street railway or streetcar line or any receiver thereof appointed by any court within this state to operate such line shall be a misdemeanor, and punishable by a fine of not less than fifty dollars nor more than two hundred dollars, or imprisonment in the county jail for a term of thirty days, or both such fine and imprisonment. 

[1901 c 103 § 3; RRS § 11075.] 

81.64.120 Car equipment specified. Every street car shall be provided with good aprons, pilots, or fenders so constructed as to prevent any person from being thrown down and run over or caught beneath the car, and equipped with efficient brakes, steps, and grab irons and such other appliances necessary for the safe operation of the car as the commission may require. [(i) 1911 c 117 § 66; RRS 

§ 10402. (ii) 1897 c 94 § 1; RRS § 11076.] 

81.64.130 Penalty. The owner or operator of any streetcar line failing to comply with the provisions of RCW 81.64.120 shall forfeit to the state a penalty of not less than twenty-five dollars for each violation thereof, and each car run shall be considered a separate violation, and every period of five days of failure to comply with that section shall be deemed a separate violation. [1897 c 94 § 2; RRS § 11077.] 

81.64.140 Weather guards. The owner, manager or operator of a street railway shall equip all cars used during the rainy or winter season, with good vestibules or weather guards sufficient for the protection of the employees from wind, rain, or snow. [(i) 1895 

c 144 § 1; RRS § 11078. (ii) 1895 c 144 § 2; RRS § 11079.] 

81.64.150 Penalty. Any violation of RCW 81.64.140 shall be punished by a fine of not less than fifty dollars nor more than two hundred and fifty dollars and each car on which such violation occurs shall be a separate offense and each period of ten days of failure to comply with that section shall be a separate offense. 

[1895 c 144 § 3; RRS § 11080.] 

81.64.160 Hours of labor. No person, officer, agent, or receiver or any owner of streetcars shall require gripmen, motormen, drivers, or conductors to work more than ten hours in any twenty-four hours. [1895 c 100 § 1; RRS § 7648.] 

81.64.170 Penalty. Any person, officer, agent, or receiver or any owner of streetcars, violating any provision of RCW 81.64.160 shall upon conviction thereof be guilty of a misdemeanor, and fined not less than twenty-five dollars nor more than one hundred dollars for each day in which the employee is required to work more than ten hours during each twenty-four hours. [1895 c 100 § 2; RRS § 7649.]

Chapter 81.68 AUTO TRANSPORTATION COMPANIES Sections 

46.

Surety 

Mileage fees: RCW 46.16.125. 

Penalty for carrying passengers without license: RCW 46.16.180. 

81.68.010 Definitions. As used in this chapter:

The term "auto transportation company" means every person or his lessees, receivers, or trustees, owning, controlling, operating, or managing any motor propelled vehicle not usually operated on or over rails, used in the business of transporting persons for compensation over any public highway in this state between fixed termini or over a regular route. It does not include persons or their lessees, receivers, or trustees, in so far as they own, control, operate, or manage taxicabs, hotel buses, or school buses. 

This chapter shall not apply to persons operating motor vehicles wholly within the limits of incorporated cities, and for a distance not exceeding three road miles beyond the corporate limits of the ¿city in which the original starting point of such vehicles is located, and which operation either alone or in conjunction with another vehicle or vehicles is not a part of any journey beyond said three mile limit.

The term "public highway" means every street, road, or highway in this state.

The term "between fixed termini or over a regular route" means the termini or route between or over which any auto transportation company usually or ordinarily operates any motor propelled vehicle, even though there may be departure from said termini or route, whether such departures be periodic or irregular. Whether or not any motor propelled vehicle is operated by any auto transportation company "between fixed termini or over a regular route" within the meaning of this chapter shall be a question of fact and the finding

of the commission thereon shall be final and shall not be subject to review. [1935 c 120 § 1; 1921 c 111 § 1; RRS § 6387.] 

Severability: "If any section, subsection, sentence, clause or phrase of this act is for any reason held to be unconstitutional such decision shall not affect the validity of the remaining portion of this act." [1921 c 111 $ 10.] 

1. The provisions of this chapter, except for express shipments, were made inapplicable to the business of transportation of property for hire by motor vehicle by section 45, chapter 184, Laws of 1935. 

2. While motor buses operating within the incorporated limits of cities and towns do not come under regulation by virtue of this chapter, they are nevertheless subject to the regulatory authority of the Commission as to rates and service under the common carrier sections of the 1911 Act. State ex rel. 

Spokane United Railways v. Dept. of Public Service, 191 Wash. 595 (1937). 

3. Person driving passenger car over a regular route in delivering U. S. mail and frequently accepting passengers and freight for which he received compensation was violating this act by_so_operating without a certificate of convenience and necessity. State v. H. B. Price, 122 Wash. 421 (1922). 

4. This chapter does not deprive cities of the right to regulate the use of their streets, and a street railway, where it is specially damaged, has a right by injunction to prevent the violation of ordinances and laws and of an order of the Department. State ex rel. Seattle and Rainier Valley Railway Company v. Superior Court of King County, 123 Wash. 116 (1923). 

5. A holder of a certificate of convenience and necessity authorizing passenger service over a certain highway is entitled to an injunction against a company operating trucks carrying mail which repeatedly picks up and carries passengers between the termini of petitioner's route even where no charge was made when it appears that the free transportation is being given to injure petitioner's business. Davis and Banker d/b/a Methow Valley Transportation Co. v. Nickell, 126 Wash. 421 (1923). 

6. Owner of taxicabs, having no certificate of convenience and necessity for operating between fixed termini violates this act when two of its vehicles habitually meet a certain train soliciting and taking passengers for two nearby cities, for which a uniform fare was paid by each passenger. Cook v. 

Lawwill, 127 Wash. 92 (1923). 

7. A mail carrier operating over a regular route and between fixed termini without a certificate of convenience and necessity will be enjoined from habitually picking up passengers and freight and carrying them free where it is done for the purpose of injuring a certificate holder. Davis v. Clevenger, 127 

Wash. 136 (1923). 

8. The determination of the "fixed termini" of a carrier's route is expressly made a question of fact for the Department by this section-which determination is not subject to review by the courts. Chelan Transfer Co. v. Foote, 130 Wash. 511 (1924). 

9. Where certificate holder, a freight carrier, sells out his equipment and "all hauling" and rights under the certificate, there is a sale of good will; and the seller will be barred from for hire hauling over that route even though the hauling done did not require a certificate under this and following sections. Low v. Clark, 134 Wash. 245 (1925). 

10. A certificate of convenience and necessity authorizing bus service between two named cities over the only existing route does not authorize service by a new and different route opened later where both routes were to remain open between the two cities, and an injunction will lie against such service. Northern Pacific Railway Co. v. Yakima Northern Stages, Inc., 135 

Wash. 595 (1925). 

11. The court considered the phrase "fixed termini" and "regular route" and affirmed departmental order denying an extension of certificate authority. 

North River Transportation Co. v. Denney, 149 Wash. 489 (1928). 

12. The mere fact that one wishing to operate motor vehicles for carriage of passengers for hire over regular route interstate highway applied to state

II 3 officials for a certificate as required by state statute does not prevent his assailing the statute as unconstitutional in case the certificate is denied. Buck v. Kuykendall, 267 U. S. 307, 69 L. Ed. 623 (1925). 

13. A state cannot forbid the use of its highways to interstate passenger carriers by motor vehicle. Such action is not only a burden on, but an obstruction of, interstate commerce. Id. 

14. A through service eliminating changes and delays in the public interest may be provided by extending a present certificate holder's route over territory already locally served by one having a prior right and monopoly and already giving satisfactory local service where the order granting the extension prohibits transportation other than through traffic over the route formerly monopolized, prospective loss, if any, being damnum absque injuria. North Bend Stage Liñe, Inc., v. Denney, 153 Wash. 439 (1929). 

Affirmed: North Bend Stage Line, Inc. v. Denney, 283 U. S. 786, 75 L. Ed. 

1414 (1931).

An order extending a certificate of convenience and necessity between points in eastern Washington and Easton to include a through service between Seattle and points in eastern Washington beyond Easton, must be construed to include through service between eastern Washington points and intermediate points between Seattle and Easton, where the order, without specifically mentioning such intermediate points, authorized through service "in accordance with the schedule filed with the application," which schedule recited the proposed through time and tariff, not only from Seattle, but from all intermediate points; and where the application made no disclaimer of such service. North Bend Stage Line, Inc. v. Washington Motor Coach Co., 167 

Wash. 9 (1932). 

15. The opening of a new highway between two large cities and paralleling the old dirt road at a distance not greater than two and one-half miles does not affect the right of an existing certificate holder over the old route to an amendment of its certificate to include service over the new route where both routes serve the same territory and over 93 percent of the passengers were through passengers and the old road was to be abandoned. 

North Coast Transportation Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 157 Wash. 79 (1930). 

16. Granting to a railroad company of a certificate of convenience and necessity for motor bus service did not violate stage line's rights under its prior certificate where the railroad was forbidden from rendering local_service between points covered by the existing service. North Bend Stage Line, Inc. v. Dept. of Public Works, 162 Wash. 46 (1931). 

17. The provisions of this section excluding taxicabs, hotel buses, school buses, and certain farm vehicles does not violate that section of the State Constitution guaranteeing equal protection of the laws, since the classifications created are reasonable and are differences not in degree but in kind. 

State ex rel. Dept. of Public Works v. Inland Forwarding Corp., 164 Wash. 

412 (1931). 

18. For two cases treating the nature and extent of terminal boundaries, see: Chelan Transfer Co. v. Foote, 130 Wash. 511 (1924); and S. & S. Auto

Freight v. Dept. of Public Works, 175 Wash. 471 (1933). 

19. One making regular trips in truck to deliver daily newspapers who sometimes took a passenger or two, without authority from the Department but without definite arrangement for compensation and merely accepting whatever the passenger felt disposed to give was not acting unlawfully, and the travel bureau by which the transportation was arranged was not required to secure a certificate. Strickler v. Schaaf, 199 Wash. 372; 123 A.L.R. 226 (1939). 

20. Owners of private automobiles occasionally taking passengers who shared expenses are not required to obtain certificate from Department. Id. 

21. A bus company having only the right to carry passengers from points in eastern Washington to Seattle through points in western Washington does not thereby have the right to use one of the latter points as a terminus and carry passengers between such point and intermediate points to Seattle. 

State ex rel. North Bend Stage Line, Inc. v. Dept. of Transportation, 26 Wn. 

(2d) 485 (1946).

22. On an issue as to the scope of a bus certificate of convenience and necessity authorizing service between certain towns and "the site of the Grand Coulee Dam," the certificate authorized the company to carry passengers to and from all the settlements, now towns, which were and now are located near the dam area. Northwest Greyhound Lines, Inc. v. Dept. of Transporta tion, 34 Wn. (2d) 157 (1949). 

23. Although act specifically excludes taxicabs, cabs may be enjoined from regularly soliciting passengers over a route served by a certificated carrier. 

Op. Atty. Gen. Dec. 9, 1926. 

24. Person operating on a road through government land constructed by private individuals and maintained by them for their own use is required to obtain certificate of convenience and necessity if he holds himself out as willing to carry passengers between fixed termini over a regular route. Op. 

Atty. Gen. May 18, 1938. 

25. Motor buses operating entirely within limits of incorporated city must pay seat tax required of "auto stages" regardless of fact that they carry licenses as "For Hire" vehicles. Op. Atty. Gen. Dec. 12, 1940. 

81.68.020 Compliance with chapter required. No person, his lessees, receivers, or trustees, shall engage in the business of operating as a common carrier any motor propelled vehicle for the transportation of persons between fixed termini or over a regular route, for compensation on any public highway in this state, except in accordance with the provisions of this chapter. [1927 c 166 § 1; 1921 

c 111 § 2; RRS § 6388.] 

1. Suit to enjoin enforcement must show operation, preparation or promise to operate. Arneson v. Denny, 25 F. (2d) 988 (1928). 

2. Revocation of certificate of convenience and necessity for operation of motorbuses because of operation through third parties was not justified after operation for ten years with knowledge of the Department. State ex rel. 

Northeast Transportation Co. v. Schaaf, 198 Wash. 52 (1939). 

3. A person operating a travel bureau to bring persons desiring transportation together with persons traveling by auto and seeking others to share expenses is not engaged in business in violation of this section. Strickler v. 

Schaaf, 199 Wash. 372 (1939). 

81.68.030 Regulation by commission. The commission shall su pervise and regulate every auto transportation company in this state, (1) By fixing and altering its rates, fares, charges, classifications, rules, and regulations; (2) By regulating the accounts, service, and safety of operations; (3) By requiring the filing of annual and other reports and data; and (4) By supervising and regulating such companies in all other matters affecting the relationship between them and the traveling public.

The commission, at any time, after the holding of a hearing of which the holder of any certificate has had notice and an opportunity to be heard, and at which it shall be proven that the holder has wilfully violated or refused to observe any of the commission's orders, rules, or regulations, may suspend, revoke, alter, or amend any certificate issued under the provisions of this chapter. [1921 

c 111 § 3; RRS § 6389.]

1. For rules and regulations promulgated under authority of this section, see pamphlet "Rules and Regulations Governing Auto Transportation Companies," obtainable on request to the Secretary of the Commission. 

2. In the exercise of its power under this section, the Department could provide in the order granting a certificate that the vehicles of the applicant bus company should be operated subject to the ordinances of the cities between which they were to serve. In re Sound Transit Co., 119 Wash. 684 (1922). 

3. Under this section the Department has the power to adopt and proceed under a rule stating that a certificate acquired in an ex parte proceeding can be revoked or canceled if it is found to have been obtained upon a false representation or affidavit. Knowles v. Kuykendall, 122 Wash. 315 (1922), and see current Rule 6 of Rules and Regulations Governing Auto Transportation omitting the words "ex parte." 

4. Where the Department mistakenly assumed that the service sought to be rendered by an applicant seeking an amendment to his certificate was identical to that service he was already authorized to give and granted the application, finding only that ". we cannot find in the record that public convenience and necessity have ceased to exist," the findings were inadequate to support the order and the certificate should be canceled. North ern Pacific Ry. Co. v. Yakima-Northern Stages, Inc., 135 Wash. 595 (1925). 

5. This act exists only for the purpose of regulating and supervising the public utilities named therein, and does not give the Department authority to decide a dispute between individuals as to the ownership of a certificate. 

State ex rel. Northeast Transportation Co. v. Schaaf, 198 Wash. 52 (1939). 

6. Revocation of a certificate is not warranted where the only violation of the Department's orders charged was that the certificate holder allowed a third person to operate his buses and it appears that the Department acquiesced therein for eleven years and is therefore estopped from asserting the violation and the hearing did not disclose any willful violation of law relative to such operation. Id. 

7. The Department is a creature of statute and its powers, duties and jurisdiction are limited to what the statute prescribes. Id. 

8. The right of the respondent to operate its buses over the route designated in its certificate is a valuable one and must not be infringed, save strictly in accordance with the law, and then only when existing facts justify such infringement. Id. 

9. The Department has only such powers as are vested in it by the statute as regards passenger transportation by motor vehicle. North Bend Stage Lines, Inc. v. Schaaf, 199 Wash. 621 (1939). 

10. The statute requires that a violation of orders, rules or regulations must be willful to justify revocation of a certificate of convenience and necessity, and the Department is without jurisdiction to revoke a certificate in the absence of a special finding of a willful violation. State v. Abel, 10 Wn. (2d) 349 (1941). 

11. In order to cancel a certificate of convenience and necessity because of a carrier's failure to comply with an order, the Department must at least find that such failure was willful and likely to be continued and repeated. 

Dept. of Public Service ex rel. Anacortes Chamber of Commerce v. Shelton, 

23 Wn. (2d) 526 (1945). 

12. In a proceeding to cancel an outstanding certificate of convenience and necessity, evidence of conditions subsequent to the filing of the complaint was admissible and relevant since an order of cancellation would necessarily have to be based upon a finding that as of the date of the order the carrier willfully violates or refuses to comply with any of the Department's rules or regulations. Id.

Certificate of convenience and necessity. No auto transportation company shall hereafter operate for the transportation of persons for compensation between fixed termini or over a

regular route in this state, without first having obtained from the commission a certificate declaring that public convenience and necessity require such operation. 

When an applicant requests a certificate to operate in a territory already served by a certificate holder under this chapter, the commission may, after hearing, issue the certificate only if the existing auto transportation company or companies serving the territory will not provide service to the satisfaction of the commission. 

In all other cases, the commission may, with or without hearing, issue certificates, or for good cause shown refuse to issue them, or issue them for the partial exercise only of the privilege sought, and may attach to the exercise of the rights granted such terms and conditions as, in its judgment, the public convenience and necessity may require.

Any right, privilege, certificate held, owned, or obtained by an auto transportation company may be sold, assigned, leased, transferred, or inherited as other property, but only upon authorization by the commission. [1921 c 111 § 4; RRS § 6390.] 

1. This act as to the transportation of property for hire by motor vehicle, except for express shipments, superseded by Chapter 184, Laws of 1935 

(Chapter 81.80 R.C.W.). 

2. The Department cannot grant a certificate of convenience and necessity for a stage route to a certain station, without a hearing, where it was already served by a line running past and within 100 yards of the station, in view of the express terms of this section. State ex rel. United Auto Transportation Co. 

v. Dept. of Public Works, 119 Wash. 381 (1922). 

3. Notwithstanding the provisions of this section which make the granting of a certificate of convenience and necessity a matter of right to a bus company in operation prior to January 15, 1921, the Director of Public Works had the power and authority to provide in the certificate that the bus operation authorized therein should be subject to the ordinances of the city whose streets were part of the route. In re Sound Transit Co., 119 Wash. 684 (1922). 

4. Person driving passenger car over a regular route in delivering United 

States mail and frequently accepting passengers and freight for which he received compensation was violating this act by so operating without a certificate of convenience and necessity. State v. Price, 122 Wash. 421 (1922). 

5. This act does not deprive cities of the right to regulate the use of their streets and a street railway company where it is specially damaged has a right by injunction to prevent the violation of ordinances and laws and of an order of the Department of Public Works. State ex rel. Seattle and Rainier 

Valley Railway Company v. Superior Court of King County, 123 Wash. 116 (1923). 

6. Resort to the Department is not a prerequisite to an action for an injunction brought by a railroad company engaged in interstate commerce and suffering loss by the unlawful competition of motor vehicles operating without a certificate of convenience and necessity. Northern Pacific Ry. Co. v. 

Schoenfeldt, 123 Wash. 579; but see Buck v. Kuykendall, 267 U. S. 307, 69 L.

Ed. 623 (1925) disagreeing with other holdings in this case. 

7. A holder of a certificate of convenience and necessity authorizing passenger service over a certain highway is entitled to an injunction against a company operating trucks carrying mail which repeatedly picks up and carries passengers between the termini of petitioner's route even where no charge was made when it appears that the free transportation is being given to injure petitioner's business. Davis and Banker d/b/a Methow Valley Transportation Co. v. Nickell, 126 Wash. 421 (1923).

נד 8. Owner of taxicabs, having no certificate of convenience and necessity for operating between fixed termini, violates this act when two of its vehicles habitually meet a certain train soliciting and taking passengers for two nearby cities, for which a uniform rate was paid by each passenger. Cook v. 

Lawwill, 127 Wash. 92 (1923). 

9. A mail carrier operating over a regular route and between fixed termini without a certificate of convenience and necessity will be enjoined from habitually picking up passengers and freight and carrying them free where it is done for the purpose of injuring a certificate holder. Davis v. Clevinger, 127 Wash. 136 (1923). 

10. A state cannot prohibit the use on an interstate highway of auto vehicles by common carrier for hire by requiring that they secure a certificate from a public official declaring that public convenience and necessity require such operation. Buck v. Kuykendall, 267 U. S. 307, 69 L. Ed. 623 (1925). 

11. The mere fact that one wishing to operate auto stages for hire on an interstate highway applied to state officials for a certificate, as required by state statute, does not prevent his assailing the statute as unconstitutional in case the certificate is denied. Id. 

12. Priority in time of making application for a certificate of convenience and necessity is not controlling although it is a proper factor to consider. 

State ex rel. United Auto Transportation Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 129 

Wash. 5 (1924). 

13. On an issue as to the infringement of relator's certificate of convenience and necessity, the operator of taxicabs is guilty of contempt in violating a restraining order where it appears that the cabs made repeated trips over the route in question carrying passengers for hire who were solicited on the streets until the vehicles were loaded. State ex rel. Savage v. Dean, 129 Wash. 

584 (1924). 

14. A certificate of convenience and necessity authorizing bus service between two named cities over the only existing route does not authorize service by a new and different route opened later where both routes were to remain open between the two cities, and an injunction will lie against such service. Northern Pacific Railway Co. v. Yakima Northern Stages, Inc., 135 

Wash. 595 (1925). 

15. Where a new road is to be built into a forest, said road beginning at a terminus of a certificate holder's route, and certificate holder had, as the need arose, served over the old road prior to January 15, 1921, the certificate holder was entitled to an extension of his certificate to authorize service over the new road and the granting of a certificate to a new company was a violation of the certificate holder's statutory rights. Yelton & McLaughlin v. Dept. of Public Works, 136 Wash. 445 (1925). 

16. Applicant for certificate for through service between Seattle and 

Hoquiam was properly denied a certificate, it appearing that three separate companies were serving respectively between Seattle-Tacoma, Tacoma- Olympia, and Olympia-Hoquiam, and that where their routes met they used common terminals and had close schedule connections, and where the Department had had no complaints as to the existing service. State ex rel. 

Krakenberger v. Dept. of Public Works, 141 Wash. 168 (1926). 

17. When at a hearing on an application for through service over the routes of several protesting connecting carriers, the connecting carriers offered to establish a through service if the Department or the courts should find it necessary, such offers while not binding must be taken as manifestations of good faith and considered by the Department before the granting of another certificate. Id. 

18. The Department, in awarding certificates of public convenience and necessity for stage routes, may properly consider the needs for the immediate future, expected to continue for several years, and its finding that an extension of service "is required," supported by abundant evidence of present increased activities by reason of prospective work, is a finding as to the present fact, and not objectionable as based upon contingencies of the future. 

Northern Pac. R. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 144 Wash. 47 (1927).

19. An extension of a bus certificate is warranted where the route serves a farming community on the line of a state highway and farms seven or eight miles from the nearest railway station. Id. 

20. After finding that convenience and necessity require service over new highway between Seattle and Everett, the company operating over old highway has prior right in law when the traffic picked up between the cities is negligible and 60 per cent of traffic originates or is destined to points north of Everett. Seattle and Everett are part of the territory served. Existing company was serving territory which would be served by operation over new highway and was entitled to certificate. Pacific Northwest Traction Co. v.

Dept. of Public Works, 151 Wash. 659 (1929). 

21. A through service eliminating changes and delays and in the public interest, may be provided by extending a present certificate holder's route over a territory already locally served by one having a prior right and monopoly and already giving satisfactory local service, where the order prohibits transportation other than through traffic over the route formerly monopolized; prospective loss thereto, if any, being damnum absque injuria. North Bend 

Stage Line v. Denney, 153 Wash. 439 (1929). 

22. Public convenience and necessity does not mean "necessary" in the ordinary sense and the statute authorizing certificates should be construed to encourage improvements and appliances devoted to the public use and authorizes whatever is highly important or desirable for the public welfare. Id. 

23. Granting an extension of a certificate of convenience for through service, over part of a local route held by another, with a limitation prohibiting the furnishing of any local service, is fully justified where the public convenience would be promoted by needed connections and additional facilities furthering through traffic. Auto Interurban Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 

153 Wash. 479 (1929). 

24. "Connected service by two independent operators is always unsatisfactory to the public. The operators usually have divergent interests. The inconvenience of transferring one's self and one's baggage from one car to another is often sufficient to divert patronage to some other transportation system." " Id. 

25. A through service eliminating changes and delays in the public interest may be provided by extending a present certificate holder's route over territory already locally served by one having a prior right and monopoly and already giving satisfactory local service where the order granting the extension prohibits transportation other than through traffic over the route formerly monopolized, prospective loss thereto, if any, being damnum absque injuria. North Bend Stage Line, Inc. v. Denney, 153 Wash. 439 (1929). Af firmed North Bend Stage Line, Inc. v. Denney, 283 U. S. 786, 75 L. Ed. 1414 (1931). 

An order extending a certificate of convenience and necessity between points in eastern Washington and Easton to include a through service between Seattle and points in eastern Washington beyond Easton, must be construed to include through service between eastern Washington points and intermediate points between Seattle and Easton, where the order, without specifically mentioning such intermediate points authorized through service "in accordance with the schedule filed with the application," which schedule recited the proposed through time and tariff, not only from Seattle but from all intermediate points; and where the application made no disclaimer of such service. North Bend Stage Line, Inc. v. Washington Motor Coach Co., 167 

Wash. 9 (1932). 

26. This section limits the power of the Department to grant a certificate in territory already served to cases where the existing certificate holder will not perform the service to the satisfaction of the Department. Id. 

27. An order of the Department_authorizing a partial assignment of a certificate of public convenience and necessity, and a consolidation of an existing certificate, can not be complained of by another certificate holder because the order did not expressly reserve its exclusive right to a through service, where it did not affect such through service or extend any rights

OTR theretofore enjoyed. Washington Motor Coach Co. v. Baker, 158 Wash. 588 

28. A finding of convenience and necessity for a motor service paralleling the applicant's railway, and covering short distances, is sustained where it is highly probable that the proposed motor service will be regarded by the public as more attractive and convenient than, and a substitute for, the rail service.

Upon conflicting applications for certificates of convenience and necessity, a through service is a favored service. 

The Department's decision as to public convenience and necessity will not be disturbed on appeal unless it clearly appears to be arbitrary. North Bend 

Stage Line v. Dept. of Public Works, 162 Wash. 46 (1931). 

29. Where stage lines will coincide for but part of the distance, a holder cannot object to the granting of a certificate expressly restricting local service over that portion of the route. Id. 

30. The courts will take judicial notice of the present motor-mindedness of the public with respect to short distance service. Id. 

31. An order extending a certificate of convenience and necessity between points in eastern Washington and Easton to include a through service between Seattle and points in eastern Washington beyond Easton, must be construed to include through service between eastern Washington points and intermediate points between Seattle and Easton, where the order, without specifically mentioning such intermediate points, authorized through service "in accordance with the schedule filed with the application," which schedule recited the proposed through time and tariff, not only from Seattle, but from all intermediate points; and where the application made no disclaimer of such service. North Bend Stage Line, Inc. v. Washington Motor Coach Co., 167 

Wash. 9 (1932). 

32. The question as to what constitutes "territory already served" within the meaning of this section is a question of fact to be determined by the Department, involving consideration of questions of traffic, population, and economic conditions, and in many cases the topography of the country into which service is to be introduced; and where, after considering such factors, the Department finds that existing service did not meet the needs of the public along the proposed route, and the record does not show that the finding was arbitrary and capricious, it will not be disturbed. Suburban Transportation System v. Furse, 13 Wn. (2d) 345 (1942). 

33. A question not raised before the superior court cannot thereafter be raised for the first time before the Supreme Court. Id. 

34. The Department, under this section, does not have authority to grant a certificate to operate in territory already served unless the existing company or companies would not provide satisfactory service. Shelton v. Anacortes-Mount Vernon Stage Co., 23 Wn. (2d) 840 (1945). 

35. In passing upon conflicting applications for bus service between two points not already served, the finding of the Department that the applicant to whom the certificate was granted is more capable of rendering adequate and reliable service than the other applicant will not be disturbed unless it discloses evidence of arbitrariness and disregard of the material rights of the parties. Id. 

36. A bus company which has only the right to carry passengers from eastern Washington to Seattle through points in western Washington, does not thereby have the right to use one of the latter points as a terminus and carry passengers between such point and intermediate points to Seattle. State ex rel. North Bend Stage Line, Inc. v. Dept. of Transportation, 26 Wn. (2d) 485 

37. A bus company has a vested right in its certificate which cannot be taken away until a hearing is had and a proper order based thereon is entered and a further showing is made that the company has refused to obey the order. Northwest Greyhound Lines, Inc. v. Dept. of Transportation, 34 

Wn. (2d) 157 (1949). 

38. On an issue as to the scope of a certificate of convenience and necessity authorizing bus service between certain towns "and the site of the Grand

Coulee Dam," held that the certificate authorized the company to carry passengers to and from all the settlements, now towns, which were and now are located near the dam area. Id. 

39. Where a person seeking a certificate to operate a bus line over a certain route had in a previous agreement acknowledged that a bus company had the sole right to operate passenger and express bus service over that route, he is foreclosed by that admission from claiming a right to a certificate. Id. 

40. Validity and applicability of statutes relating to the use of highways by private motor carriers and contract motor carriers for hire. 109 A.L.R. 

550; 175 A.L.R. 1333. 

41. Operation of a sight-seeing bus over regular route; advertised daily and leaving and running on schedule, is transportation of persons for hire and company is subject to provisions of this act. Op. Atty. Gen. July 28, 1925. 

42. Department may permit leasing of certificate where it appears the service to the public will not be jeopardized. Op. Atty. Gen. Sept. 11, 1929. 

43. Department apparently has power to grant certificate for transportation of express only upon showing of public convenience and necessity or that present service is inadequate. Op. Atty. Gen. Feb. 10, 1931. 

44. Related laws-rules of road require school buses to stop when either loaded or empty before crossing railroad at grade. Op. Atty. Gen. Aug. 2, 1937. 

45. Granting stop-over privileges to passenger of through carrier in territory served by local carrier is not the initiation of new business in that area but merely a convenience to the public. Dept. Memo. Dec. 29, 1937. 

46. Department may allow segregation of one bus line into two units, one to operate entirely within city and the other both within and outside, but they must file separate reports. Dept. Memo. June 16, 1938. 

47. Department not empowered to issue certificate of convenience and necessity to an incorporated city for operation of intracity bus system, nor does law provide for issuance of certificate to city for operation of a bus line outside its city limits. Dept. Memo. Dec. 5, 1939. 

48. Department has no jurisdiction over operation of buses by Fort Lewis (Army Reservation) Post Exchange which are used for carriage of soldiers and families and may not require the Exchange to apply for a certificate of convenience and necessity. Op. Atty. Gen. Aug. 19, 1941. 

49. Factory operating_bus carrying only its own employees between 

Seattle and its factory in Renton would not be a common carrier and would not need certificate of convenience and necessity. Op. Atty. Gen. March 7, 1942. 

50. Certificate holder must, in order to lawfully mortgage his certificate: (1) Apply to Department for permission and pay $10; (2) If mortgagee is a public service company, he must secure authorization of Department to acquire the interest; and (3) Pay additional fee.

Act does not forbid mortgaging of bus certificate without tangible property accompanying it. Op. Atty. Gen. July 7, 1942. 

51. Group of factory workers banding together for purpose of hiring a bus for transportation to and from work where bus driver owned bus and was also a factory worker and only members of the group were allowed to ride and each paid a certain fee, does not constitute a public service company subject to regulation by the Department. Op. Atty. Gen. July 9, 1942. 

52. Organization of factory workers into a non-profit corporation for purpose of hiring transportation which would carry no one but members does not constitute a public service company. Id. 

53. Defense plant owning and operating its own buses for its workers and charging one cent per mile to each employee would be required to obtain a certificate of convenience and necessity if workers carried were sufficient in number to require that carrier property be dedicated to public use. Id. 

54. Individual farmers or groups of farmers offering transportation for hire in buses owned by them to prospective workers and operating between

!

OVE ​fixed termini and over regular route would be required to obtain certificate of convenience and necessity since they would carry anybody who presented himself for employment. Id. 

55. Transportation offered farm workers in buses for hire but not operating over a regular route or between fixed termini would not require certificate of convenience and necessity. Id. 

56. Holder of certificate of convenience and necessity is entitled to bring injunction against unauthorized operator to enjoin infringment of certificate rights. Op. Atty. Gen. Aug. 3, 1948. 

57. Bus operation between Federal military reservation and nearby cities carrying only soldiers and their families and civilian personnel employed on the reservation and operated by the Post Exchange is not subject to the jurisdiction of the Commission unless they begin carrying the general public in which case a certificate of convenience and necessity would be required. Op. 

Atty. Gen. March 16, 1950. 

58. Parties having sold bus certificate and business and desiring bank loan, security for which they are to assign their seller's interest in the amount unpaid under the purchase and sale agreement, need not secure authorization of the Commission to assign the proceeds since seller's interest does not make them an "auto transportation company” subject to jurisdiction of the Commission. Dept. Memo. Feb. 27, 1950. 

81.68.050 Fees. The commission shall collect the following miscellaneous fees from auto transportation companies:

Application for a certificate of public convenience and necessity or to amend a certificate, twenty-five dollars; application to sell, lease, mortgage, or transfer a certificate of public convenience and necessity or any interest therein, ten dollars. [1937 c 158 § 2, part; 

RRS § 10417-1, part.] 

81.68.060 Liability and property damage insurance - Surety bond. The commission, in granting certificates to operate an auto transportation company, shall require the owner or operator to first procure liability and property damage insurance from a company licensed to make liability insurance in the state, or a surety bond of a company licensed to write surety bonds in the state, on each motor propelled vehicle used or to be used in transporting persons for compensation in the amount of not to exceed five thousand dollars for any recovery for personal injury by one person, and not less than ten thousand dollars and in such additional amount as the commission shall determine, for all persons receiving personal injury by reason of one act of negligence, and not to exceed one thousand dollars for damage to property of any person other than the assured, and to maintain such liability and property damage insurance or surety bond in force on each motor propelled vehicle while so used. 

Each policy for liability or property damage insurance or surety bond required herein shall be filed with the commission and kept in full force and effect and failure so to do shall be cause for revoca tion of the delinquent's certificate. [1921 c 111 § 5; RRS § 6391.] 

1. For terms of the endorsement required on insurance filed with Commission see pamphlet, "Rules and Regulations Governing Auto Transportation Companies.' 

2. Writ of mandate issued to compel the Director of Public Works to accept and file surety bonds written by a certain company as one authorized

to do so. State ex rel. Auto Insurance Exchange v. Kuykendall, 119 Wash. 

235, 205 Pac. 392 (1922). 

3. Application of this section to carriers operating in interstate commerce held an unreasonable burden thereon. Cobb v. Dept. of Public Works, 60 F. (2d) 631 (1932). Earlier Washington case holding the contrary, Williams v. 

Denney, 151 Wash. 630 (1929). 

4. Only limitation of insurer's liability under this section is for damage arising from one accident. Op. Atty. Gen. May 16, 1921. 

5. Liability insurance excluding insurer from liability for loss or damage to baggage of bus passenger would not comply with the law. Op. Atty. Gen. Oct. 6, 1921. 

6. Law intends to provide liability for loss or damage of all property being transported whether baggage or otherwise. It is, further, broad enough in its meaning to provide liability for "loss" as well as "damage." Op. Atty. 

Gen. Aug. 29, 1930. 

7. Commission may not authorize suspension of liability insurance during periods of suspension of certificate. Op. Atty. Gen. June 9, 1932. 

8. Where party required to furnish liability insurance or bond is unable to secure it from other companies and fact is found by Insurance Commissioner, he may secure it through Lloyds of London. Op. Atty. Gen. March 12, 1940. 

81.68.070 Public service law invoked. In all respects in which the commission has power and authority under this chapter, applications and complaints may be made and filed with it, process issued, hearings held, opinions, orders and decisions made and filed, petitions for rehearing filed and acted upon, and petitions for writs of review, to the superior court filed therewith, appeals or mandate filed with the supreme court of this state, considered and disposed of by said courts in the manner, under the conditions, and subject to the limitations, and with the effect specified in this title for public service companies generally. [1921 c 111 § 6; RRS § 6392.] 

1. See "Rules of Practice and Procedure before the Washington Public 

Service Commission" pamphlet. 

2. The findings of the Department in granting a certificate of convenience and necessity will not be disturbed except for arbitrariness or disregard of the material rights of the parties: Northern Pacific Ry. Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 144 Wash. 47 (1927); Deppman v. Dept. of Public Works, 151 Wash. 

78 (1929); North Bend Stage Line v. Denney, 153 Wash. 439 (1929); North Bend

Stage Line v. Dept. of Public Works, 162 Wash. 46 (1931); Suburban Trans portation System v. Furse, 13 Wn. (2d) 345 (1942); Shelton v. Anacortes- 

Mount Vernon Stage Co., 23 Wn. (2d) 840 (1945). 

3. Writ of prohibition will not lie to prevent the superior court from hearing a petition for rehearing of a writ of review when a real party in interest was not notified of the first review proceedings. State ex rel. Sumner-Tacoma 

Stage Co. v. Superior Court, 138 Wash. 376 (1926). 

4. Upon proceedings to review an order of the Department granting a stage certificate to one of two contesting applicants, the successful applicant is a necessary party to the proceeding in order to authorize the entry of any order affecting its certificate rights, notwithstanding the statute does not ex pressly so provide. Sumner-Tacoma Stage Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 142 

Wash. 594 (1927). 

5. Where upon proceedings to review an order of the Department granting a stage certificate to one of two contesting applicants, the order was reversed without having made the successful applicant a party to the proceedings, and thereupon such party_asked that the cause be reopened, and also sued out a writ to review the Department's second order entered in pursuance of the court's judgment, the court, on such review, having all parties

before it, has the right on determining the merits, to enter a judgment sustaining the first order. Id. 

6. Findings of the Department upon conflicting applications for stage certificates will not be reviewed by the court if supported by substantial evi dence. Auto Interurban Co. v. Dept. of Public Works, 153 Wash. 479 (1929). 

81.68.080 Penalty. Every person who violates or fails to comply with, or who procures, aids, or abets in the violation of any provisions of this chapter, or who fails to obey, or comply with any order, decision, rule, regulation, direction, demand, or requirement of the commission, or any part or provision thereof, is guilty of a gross misdemeanor. [1921 c 111 § 7; RRS § 6393.] 

81.68.090 Scope of chapter. Neither this chapter nor any provi- 

I sion thereof shall apply, or be construed to apply, to commerce with foreign nations or commerce among the several states except in so far as the same may be permitted under the provisions of the Constitution of the United States and the acts of congress. [1921 c 111 § 8; 

RRS § 6394.] 

1. Interstate bus company may be required to file evidence of liability insurance with Department, but may procure it from outside of state if no admitted company is able to write it. Dept. Memo. Aug. 23, 1941. 

81.72.090 Self-insurers-Exemptions as to insurance or bond. 

Any auto transportation company now or hereafter authorized to transport persons for compensation on the highways and engaging in interstate, or interstate and intrastate, operations within the state which is or becomes qualified as a self-insurer with the Interstate Commerce Commission of the United States in accordance with the provisions of the United States interstate commerce act applicable to self insurance by motor carriers, shall be exempt, so long as such qualification remains effective, from all provisions of law relating to the carrying or filing of insurance policies or bonds in connection with such operations.

The commission may require proof of the existence and continuation of such qualification with the Interstate Commerce Commission to be made by affidavit of the auto transportation company, in such form as the commission shall prescribe. [(i) 1949 c 127 § 1; Rem. 

Supp. 1949 § 6386-5a. (ii) 1949 c 127 § 2; Rem. Supp. 1949 § 6386-5b.] 

(Chapter 81.72, RCW, except for section 81.72.090, is not published herein as it relates to subject over which Public Service Commission is without jurisdiction.)

81.76.010 Definitions. As used in this chapter: "Motor carrier transportation agent," means any person, including a trustee, assignee, or personal representative, acting as an intermediary between the public and a motor carrier in arranging for transportation, or who sells, offers for sale, or negotiates for transportation, when such transportation is furnished or offered by a motor carrier; "Motor carrier," means any person, including a trustee, assignee, or personal representative, except private automobile owners making occasional or casual trips and not engaging in the business, transporting or offering to transport persons by motor vehicle for compensation otherwise than under a certificate of convenience and necessity issued pursuant to chapter 81.68; "Agent's license," means a motor carrier transportation agent's license. [1941 c 198 § 1; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-13.] 

Severability: "If any provision of this act be invalid, such invalidity shall not affect other provisions which can be given effect without the invalid provision, and to this end the provisions of the act are declared to be severable." [1941 c 198 § 16.] 

1. Persons arranging, selling or offering for sale, or who negotiate for the transportation of persons by motor carrier are subject to the jurisdiction of state regulatory body. Op. Atty. Gen. Oct. 1, 1945. 

2. Private auto owners transporting persons for hire on casual or occasional trips and not engaging therein as a business, are not "motor carriers" within the meaning of this act, and therefore persons arranging for such transportation are not "motor carrier transportation agents." Id.

81.76.020 Application of chapter. This chapter shall not apply to transportation of persons wholly within the limits of any city within the state. [1941 c 198 § 14; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-26.] 

81.76.030 License required. No person shall engage in the business or act in the capacity of a motor carrier transportation agent without first obtaining a license so to do from the commission. [1941 

c 198 § 2; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-14.] 

81.76.040 Application for license. Application for an agent's license shall be made to the commission. It shall be verified, shall be in such form and contain such information as the commission may require. [1941 c 198 § 3; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-15.] 81.76.050 Hearing-Issuance of license-Fee-Revocation. Upon receiving an application for an agent's license, the commission shall set the matter down for hearing upon not less than thirty days' notice, and at such hearing it shall take such proof as will enable it to determine whether the applicant is satisfactory, willing, and able to perform the functions of a motor carrier agent and to properly conform to the provisions of this chapter and to such requirements, rules, and regulations, as the commission shall promulgate. 

If granted, the license shall contain: (1) Name of licensee; (2) Location at which licensee is licensed to act as a motor carrier transportation agent; (3) Motor carriers for whom licensee is licensed to negotiate or arrange transportation and, (4) Such other regulations and restrictions as the commission may deem necessary in the public interest in the particular case. 

The license shall be displayed in a prominent place at the location named therein, and the licensee shall not do business at any other location, except upon permission of the commission, nor shall the license be transferable or assignable.

The fee to be paid to the commission for an agent's license shall be twenty-five dollars per year, to be paid at the time of application therefor. Every agent's license shall expire one year from the date of its issuance. 

The commission may suspend a license for sixty days, pending a hearing before it, and upon findings made as a result of such hearing may revoke any agent's license if the licensee in acting as a motor carrier transportation agent has engaged in false advertising or false representations, or has arranged for, sold, offered for sale, or negotiated for sale, transportation by any motor carrier operating in violation of law or of any order, rule or regulation. [1941 c 198 § 4; 

Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-16.] 

81.76.060 Bond or insurance policy. Before issuing an agent's license the commission shall require the applicant to furnish satis-

factory bond or insurance policy to the state in the sum of five thousand dollars for the use and benefit of any person suffering injury by reason of the operations of the motor carrier transportation agent holding such license, and conditioned for the faithful performance of the undertakings of the motor carrier transportation agent. Any person who suffers injury by reason of a breach of the condition of such bond or insurance policy may bring an action thereon to recover damages, which damages shall include such attorneys' fee as the court may allow, plus all costs. [1941 c 198 § 5; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-17.] 

81.76.070 Agent's record to be kept. Every motor carrier transportation agent shall maintain and keep an exact record of all transactions as such agent, which record shall include: (1) The amount paid to him by each person transported, and by any motor carrier, and the name of each such payor; (2) the point of destination of the haul of each passenger for whom transportation is sold or arranged; (3) the name of the motor carrier performing each transportation service; (4) the name of the driver of each motor vehicle used in the performance of each passenger transportation sold or arranged; and (5) the license number or other identification plate number, make, and motor number of the vehicle used in transporting each person to whom transportation is sold or arranged.

The commission or its authorized agent shall be permitted to inspect such records at any time. [1941 c 198 § 6, part; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-18, part.] 

1. Insurance provision herein relating to transportation in private cars does not mean that one engaged solely in selling, offering or arranging transportation in private cars used only occasionally for such purpose is a "motor carrier transportation agent" within the meaning of this act. Section only requires one who is otherwise a motor carrier transportation agent to provide insurance for transportation in private cars. Op. Atty. Gen. Oct. 1, 1945. 

81.76.080 Private cars-Insurance required of agent. Every motor carrier transportation agent arranging transportation for persons in private automobiles making casual or occasional trips, and when such private automobile is not engaged in transportation as a business, must provide accident insurance coverage to the amount of one thousand dollars, or more, for every person for whom such arrangements are made. [1941 c 198 § 6, part; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 639718, part.] 

81.76.090 Persons deemed to be agents. Any person and his trustee, assignee, or personal representative, shall be deemed to be acting as a motor carrier transportation agent who: (1) Orally or by card, circular, pamphlet, newspaper, radio, sign, billboard, or in any other manner advertises as one who arranges for, sells, furnishes, negotiates for, or provides transportation over any street, road, or highway in this state, when such transportation is furnished

or offered by a motor carrier; (2) manages or conducts as manager, conductor, agent, proprietor, ticket collector, or otherwise, a place at which such transportation may be arranged, or is offered or pro posed to be arranged. [1941 c 198 § 7; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-19.] 

81.76.100 Violations by agent-Penalty. Any person acting as a motor carrier transportation agent without a license, or any licensed agent violating any provisions of this chapter or condition of his license, shall be guilty of a misdemeanor. [1941 c 198 § 8; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-20.] 

81.76.110 Motor carrier's car license. No person shall engage in the business, or act in the capacity, of a motor carrier without first obtaining from the director of licenses the license required for a motor vehicle for hire, and paying therefor the same fee as is required for the operation of such a motor vehicle. [1941 c 198 §9; 

Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-21.] 

81.76.120 Motor carrier's bond. Every motor carrier shall upon obtaining his vehicle license deposit with and thereafter keep on file with the director of licenses a surety bond running to the state covering each motor vehicle used or to be used in the business of a motor carrier, in the sum of one thousand dollars for any recovery for death or personal injury by one person, and five thousand dollars for all persons killed or receiving personal injury by reason of one act of negligence, and one thousand dollars for damage to property of any person other than the assured, with a surety company licensed to do business in this state, to be approved by the director of licenses, conditioned for the faithful compliance by the principal of the bond with the provisions of this chapter, and the payment of all damages which may be sustained by any person injured by reason of any negligent or unlawful act on the part of the principal, his agents, or employees, in the conduct of the business or in the operation of any motor propelled vehicle used in transporting passengers as a motor carrier over or along any public highway. [1941 c 198 § 10; 

Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-22.] 

81.76.130 Insurance in lieu of bond. In lieu of the surety bond, there may be deposited and kept on file with the director of licenses, a public liability insurance policy for each motor vehicle operated or intended to be operated, executed by an insurance company licensed to write such insurance policies in the state, assuring the applicant for a license, against property damage and liability to the public, with the premiums paid and payment noted thereon. The policy of insurance shall provide a minimum coverage identical to the coverage required in the surety bond. No provision of this chapter shall limit the right of any injured person to any private cause of action against a motor carrier. [1941 c 198 § 11; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-23.]

81.76.140 Right of action for damages. Every person injured by any negligent or unlawful acts of any person receiving a license as a motor carrier, or his agents or employees conducting or carrying on the business as a motor carrier, or in operating any motor propelled vehicle used in the business, and his heirs, executors, and administrators, shall have a cause of action against the principal and surety upon the bond, or against the insurance company and the insured, provided for in RCW 81.76.130, for all damages sustained, and in any action to recover for the loss the full amount of damages sustained may be recovered against the motor carrier, but the recovery against the surety or insurance company shall be limited to the amount of the bond or insurance policy. [1941 c 198 § 12; 

Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-24.] 

81.76.150 Violations by carrier-Penalty. Any person engaging in the business, or acting in the capacity, of a motor carrier without having first obtained a license from and having deposited a surety bond or insurance policy with the director of licenses shall be guilty of a gross misdemeanor. [1941 c 198 § 13; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-25.] 

81.76.160 Public service law invoked. In all respects in which the commission has power and authority under this chapter, applications and complaints may be made and filed with it, process issued, hearings held, opinions, orders and decisions made and filed, petitions for rehearing filed and acted upon, and petitions for writs of review, to the superior court filed therewith, appeals or mandate filed with the supreme court, considered and disposed of by said courts in the manner, under the conditions, and subject to the limitations, and with the effect specified in this title for public service companies generally. [1941 c 198 § 15; Rem. Supp. 1941 § 6397-27.]
